Title: Optimal Bounds for the k-Disjoint Paths Problem

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.14902v1

Published Time: Fri, 15 May 2026 01:33:00 GMT

Number of Pages: 166

Markdown Content:
# Optimal Bounds for the k-Disjoint Paths Problem ∗

Dario Cavallaro † Maximilian Gorsky ‡ Stephan Kreutzer †

Dimitrios M. Thilikos § Sebastian Wiederrecht ¶

Abstract 

The Graph Minors Series of Robertson and Seymour forms the foundation of algo-rithmic structural graph theory, yielding fixed-parameter algorithms for problems such as Disjoint Paths , Rooted Minor Checking , and Folio . A key ingredient behind the fixed-parameter tractability of the k-Disjoint Paths problem is the irrelevant-vertex technique, which is a key mechanism in a wide range of algorithms. The strength of this machinery is governed by the Vital Linkage Theorem and the so-called Linkage Function ℓ. However, despite its foundational role, the best known bounds on the Linkage Function have remained enormous and are only implicitly understood. The quantitative bounds behind these results have traditionally been so large that the resulting algorithms are often regarded as “galactic.” We show that this view is overly pessimistic and can be overturned in an essentially optimal way. Our main result is a general irrelevant-vertex theorem for a common generalisation of k-Disjoint Paths and Rooted Minor Checking for graphs of size at most d, commonly called the (k, d )-Folio problem. Specifically, we show that for any graph G in which the k terminals are chosen from some set R, if the treewidth of G exceeds β(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k) then we can locate an irrelevant vertex for the (k, d )-Folio problem. Here, the quantity b is the bidimensionality of R, that is, the largest b for which a (b × b)-grid minor in G can be rooted on R. Thus, the exponential component of the irrelevant-vertex threshold is driven by the bound on the bidimensionality, rather than by the number of terminals, and we argue that this dependence is essentially optimal up to polynomial factors. As a consequence, the Linkage Function satisfies ℓ(k) ∈ 2poly (k). Beyond its structural significance, our result yields improved parameter dependencies for algorithms for Disjoint Paths 

and Rooted Minor Checking , and provides a radical quantitative improvement for a broad range of graph-minor-based algorithmic frameworks.

References 157 1 Introduction 

The k-Disjoint Paths and H-Minor Checking problems are among the central algorithmic problems in graph theory. In Graph Minors XIII [RS95], Robertson and Seymour showed that both k-Disjoint Paths , for fixed k, and H-Minor Checking , for a fixed graph H, 

can be solved in cubic time. The fact that these algorithmic results originate from the Graph Minors Series is not a coincidence. Rather, in this series Robertson and Seymour introduced a structural–algorithmic paradigm – built on tree-decompositions, excluded-grid dualities, and the irrelevant-vertex technique that has profoundly influenced modern graph algorithms, particularly in parameterized complexity [FL88, DHK05, Thi12, LSZ20]. In this sense, the significance of the Graph Minors Series extends far beyond a single routing problem. It established a general methodology for designing parameterized and structural algorithms (see [LSZ20]). 

1.1 The algorithmics of Graph Minors 

From the outset, however, there was a striking gap between qualitative solvability and quantitative efficiency. Already in 1987, David Johnson, in “ The NP-Completeness Column: An Ongoing Guide ” [Joh87], emphasised that while Robertson and Seymour’s results were a major theoretical breakthrough, they were far from being practically applicable. He writes: “For any instance 

G = ( V, E ) that one could fit into the known universe, one would easily prefer |V |70 to even constant time, if that constant had to be one of Robertson and Seymour’s” . Moreover, Johnson’s back-of-the-envelope reconstruction of just one structural constant in Graph Minors V [RS86] already illustrates the scale of the problem. Unwinding his estimate leads to a quantity roughly of the form 

2↑2222↑2↑Θ( r)

where 2↑r denotes a tower of r twos. Thus, the difficulty is not merely a matter of implementation or low-order polynomial factors: the hidden constants arise from the structural theorems themselves. In a later retrospective, Lokshtanov, Saurabh, and Zehavi expressed as follows: “While Parameterized Complexity does provide an extremely rich toolkit for designing efficient parameterized algorithms, one of its foundations and still most powerful tools yields algorithms that are wildly impractical. ” [LSZ20]. Indeed, the original Graph Minors dependencies are enormous, and the actual capacity for these theorems to come with reasonable bounds has largely remained opaque until recently [Chu15b, CC16, KTW18, KTW21, CT21, GSW25, GSW26]. In particular, the constants governing the irrelevant-vertex machinery are not merely large, but also only implicitly constructible and deeply intertwined with the more arcane structural results deeper within the theory. Therefore making the Graph Minors paradigm efficient requires more than just faster implementations of existing arguments. Instead it requires precise control and improvement of the structural thresholds that drive the entire framework. This supports Mike Fellows’ 1989 prescient appraisal: “it is likely to be many years before the practical significance of Robertson-Seymour theorems is fully understood ” [Fel89]. 1The Linkage Function. Given a graph G, a linkage in G is a set L of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths, and it is vital if its paths span all vertices of G and there is no other linkage in G joining the same pairs of endpoints (see [RS09]). A central structural threshold in the Graph Minors Series is the Linkage Function . It stems from a theorem of Robertson and Seymour in Graph Minors XXI [RS09], which states that every graph containing a vital linkage has treewidth bounded by a function of the number of endpoints of that linkage. This result lies at the heart of the celebrated irrelevant-vertex technique : Given an instance of the k-Disjoint Paths problem, when the treewidth of the graph is sufficiently large, one can delete a non-terminal vertex without affecting the outcome of the instance. By iterating this reduction, one eventually obtains an equivalent instance of bounded treewidth, that is amenable to dynamic programming. This paradigm lies at the heart of Robertson and Seymour’s algorithm for Disjoint Paths , the more general folio machinery for rooted pattern detection, and Minor Checking as we discuss below, as well as numerous subsequent developments in structural algorithmics, parameterized complexity, and meta-algorithmics [RS95, DHK05, RS09, RS12, Thi12, LSZ20, GST23c, SSS +24, FGS +25, SST25]. The main contribution of this paper is that this core graph minors mechanism can in fact be made quantitatively reasonable. We prove an optimal estimate of the Linkage Function, up to polynomial dependencies in the relevant parameters. As a consequence, we obtain a drastically improved parameter dependence for Disjoint Paths , Rooted Minor Checking , Folio , and a broad range of algorithms based on the irrelevant-vertex technique. In this sense, we turn the algorithmic core of the Graph Minors Algorithm into a controlled tool, by optimally taming its “galactic-scale” behaviour. 

Disjoint Paths problem. An instance of the k-Disjoint Paths problem consists of a graph G

together with a pattern τ = {(si, t i) | i ∈ { 1, . . . , k }} of terminal pairs. The problem is to decide whether G contains a linkage consisting of k paths whose pairs of endpoints agree with τ .In Graph Minors XIII [RS95], Robertson and Seymour gave an algorithm for the k-Disjoint Paths problem running in f (k) · | G|3 time 1 for some function f : N → N. At a high level, the irrelevant-vertex technique is applied as follows. The algorithm proceeds by repeatedly identifying and deleting irrelevant vertices, that is, vertices whose removal preserves equivalence of the instance. More precisely, if the input graph contains a large clique-minor, then one can find an irrelevant vertex inside a model of this clique. Otherwise, assuming that large clique-minors are excluded and that G has sufficiently large treewidth, the graph contains a sufficiently large flat wall. Robertson and Seymour further proved in [RS95] that the central vertices of such a flat wall are irrelevant . Thus removing these vertices again yields an equivalent instance. Iterating this reduction eventually produces an equivalent instance of bounded treewidth, which can then be solved by standard dynamic programming techniques. Although the algorithm of [RS95] outlined above is conceptually simple, its correctness hinges on a highly non-trivial structural claim: the central vertices of a sufficiently large flat wall are indeed irrelevant for the instance. Equivalently, every linkage that passes through such a vertex can be rerouted and replaced by one with the same pattern that avoids it. A proof of this statement only appeared later, in Graph Minors XXI [RS09], where Robertson and Seymour established it         

> 1We write |G|as a shorthand for |V(G)|and || G|| as a shorthand for |E(G)|.

2through a long and intricate sequence of arguments building on many of the structural results developed in the intervening volumes and combining their large parametric dependencies. We say that a linkage L is a T -linkage if T is the set of endpoints of its paths. 

Proposition 1.1 (Robertson and Seymour [RS09]) . There is a function ℓ : N → N such that if a graph G has a vital T -linkage L, then the treewidth of G is bounded by ℓ(|T |).This function ℓ is the aforementioned Linkage Function . In view of the above, the algorithm of [RS95] is correct and runs in O(f (k) · | G|3) time for some function f (k) intrinsically depending on ℓ(k). The polynomial part of this running time was improved to quadratic in [KKR12], and more recently an almost-linear-time algorithm was given in [KPS24a]. As for the parametric dependence f, any improvement in the dependencies of the algorithm of [RS95] relies mainly on upper bounds for the Linkage Function ℓ in Proposition 1.1. 

Improving the Linkage Function As we already mentioned, the estimation of ℓ emerging from an analysis of the Graph Minors Series is colossal. An important improvement appeared in [KW10], where Kawarabayashi and Wollan gave a new proof of Proposition 1.1 with better dependence on k. While they do not state any concrete estimate for their bound on ℓ, it appears to be no better than triple exponential in k [AKK +17], which remains huge, though still much better than colossal. Another line of research has aimed to improve the dependence in Proposition 1.1 for particular graph classes. In the planar case, Adler, Kolliopoulos, Krause, Lokshtanov, Saurabh, and Thilikos proved in [AKK +17] that ℓ(k) ∈ 2O(k). Conversely, they showed in [AKK +11] (see also [AK19]) that exponential growth of ℓ is unavoidable even for planar graphs, by giving a suitable counterexample (see Figure 32). Subsequently, Mazoit proved in [Maz13] that an exponential bound likewise holds for graphs embeddable in any fixed surface. His approach differs from that of [AKK +17] and builds on results of Geelen, Huynh, and Richter in [GHR18], who had earlier obtained bounds on ℓ for graphs embeddable in surfaces. 

1.2 Our results 

In this paper we prove that ℓ(k) ∈ 2poly (k). In fact, we give a much more refined bound on the Linkage Function. To state it, we need the notion of bidimensionality. Given a graph G and a set of vertices R ⊆ V (G), we refer to the pair (G, R ) as an annotated graph .For a given graph H, we say that H is an R-minor of G if there is a collection S = {Sv | v ∈ V (H)}

of pairwise vertex-disjoint connected 2 subsets of V (G), each containing at least one vertex of R, 

and such that, for every edge xy ∈ E(H), the set Sx ∪ Sy is connected in G. We also work with a stronger version of annotated graphs: A k-rooted graph is a pair (G, R) where R is an ordered multiset of k not necessarily distinct vertices in V (G). Rooted graphs come with a respective notion of rooted minors; we defer the details to Section 2.2. The bidimensionality of R in G, denoted by bidim (G, R ), is the maximum k for which G contains a (k × k)-grid as an R-minor. Note that bidim (G, R ) ≤ √|R|. This parameter was introduced in [TW23, SST25] and intuitively captures the limited structure of a vertex set in a plane graph that lies on a bounded number of faces and translates this into a non-topological definition applicable in general annotated graphs. Our main result is the following.            

> 2A set X⊆V(G)is connected in Gif the induced subgraph G[X]is connected.

3Theorem 1.2. There exists a function β : N2 → N such that, for every annotated graph (G, R ),

if there exists a vital T -linkage for some T ⊆ R in G, then G has treewidth at most β(k, b ),

where k := |T | and b := bidim (G, R ). Moreover, β(k, b ) ∈ poly (k) · 2poly (b).The above shows that the source of the exponential behaviour of the Linkage Function is not the number k of terminals, but rather the extent to which they are “bidimensionally” distributed in G, where this measure is itself bounded by √k. As a simple illustration of Theorem 1.2, consider the following consequence: If G is a graph embedded in the sphere and all k terminals lie in b faces of G, then β(k, b ) ∈ poly (k) · 2poly (b). The same conclusion holds for graphs embedded in any fixed surface, even when the terminals are at a bounded distance from b

faces. Since general graphs do not come equipped with faces in which terminals can be placed, Theorem 1.2 suggests that bidimensionality is the right notion for expressing this phenomenon in full generality. 

From linkages to folios. While the discussion above focused on the k-Disjoint Paths problem, our result holds at a more general level. Already in Graph Minors XIII [RS95], Robertson and Seymour worked in the broader framework of folios, of which k-Disjoint Paths is only a special case. For our purposes, the appropriate abstraction is the (k, d )-Folio problem. Given a rooted graph (G, R), the d-folio of (G, R), denoted by d-folio (G, R), is the set of all rooted graphs of detail at most d which are rooted minors of (G, R), where the detail d sets an upper bound on the number of edges and non-root vertices that may be used in the rooted minors. Notice that every rooted graph (G, R) naturally gives rise to an annotated graph 

(G, R R) by simply forgetting the labels of the multiset R and removing duplicates. This allows us to extend the definition of bidimensionality from annotated to rooted graphs by setting 

bidim (G, R) := bidim (G, R R). Given an annotated graph (G, R ), the (k, d )-folio of (G, R ),

denoted by (k, d )-folio (G, R ) is the union of all d-folios taken over the k-rooted graphs (G, R),

created from any ordered multisubset R of R such that |R| = k. Thus, the folio is a “bounded-complexity summary” of all rooted structures visible from the annotated set. The (k, d )-Folio problem asks us to compute the (k, d )-folio of a given annotated graph (G, R )

while the Rooted d-Folio problem asks for the d-folio of a given rooted graph (G, R). We stress that computing the (k, d )-folio of an annotated graph (G, R ) corresponds to computing 

all d-folios of the rooted graphs with k roots generated by selecting roots from the set R.This perspective is natural for the Graph Minor Algorithm. Irrelevant-vertex arguments in fact do not merely preserve the existence of a specific routing pattern, but rather preserve the entire collection of bounded-detail rooted patterns that remain realisable after each reduction step. At a conceptual level, the (k, d )-Folio problem and its rooted sibling interpolate between several basic tasks. For example, Rooted 0-Folio captures the family of feasible linkage patterns on a terminal set and, in particular, encodes the k-Disjoint Paths problem. When the set of annotated vertices is empty, (0 , d )-Folio expresses the computation of all minors of bounded size, therefore it expresses the Minor Checking problem, asking whether some graph 

G contains some fixed graph H as a minor. More generally, it provides a unified language for routing, rooted minor checking, and bounded-pattern detection in graphs. For this reason, an irrelevant-vertex theorem for folios should be viewed as a genuine strengthening of the classical Vital Linkage Theorem, and it is precisely the natural general form already suggested in Graph Minors XIII [RS95]. 4That the irrelevant-vertex argument applies to folios is shown in Graph Minors XXII [RS12]. The parameter dependence in [RS12] remains enormous, since the result relies heavily on Proposition 1.1 (proved in Graph Minors XXI [RS09]) and on the implied Linkage Function. Our main technical theorem shows that folios admit irrelevant vertices once the treewidth is large relative to an exponential function on the detail parameter and the bidimensionality of the annotated vertices and a polynomial on the number of roots that are chosen from the annotated set. Thus it lifts Theorem 1.2 from linkages to the full collection of bounded-detail rooted patterns. Let (G, R ) be an annotated graph. We say that a vertex v ∈ V (G) \ R is strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R ) if for every ordered multiset R with |R| ≤ k and RR ⊆ R it holds that d-folio (G − v, R) = d-folio (G, R).

Theorem 1.3. There exists a function β : N3 → N such that for every annotated graph (G, R )

with bidim (G, R ) ≤ b, if tw (G) > β (k, b, d ), then there exists a vertex v ∈ V (G) \ R such that v

is strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R ). Moreover, β(k, b, d ) ∈ poly (k) · 2poly (b+d).

At this point, we would like to highlight the following feature of both Theorem 1.2 and The-orem 1.3. In both statements, we impose no upper bound on the size of the annotated set 

R; we require only that its bidimensionality b is bounded. The parameter k only bounds the number of the terminals in the linkage or the size of the R’s in the folio under consideration. Thus, R should be viewed as a “repository” from which the k terminals of T or the multisets R

may be selected. Our combinatorial bound depends polynomially on k, whereas its exponential part depends only on the bidimensionality of this repository R, while the size of R itself may be arbitrarily large. Another key point of Theorem 1.3 arises from our definition of strong irrelevancy: a strongly irrelevant vertex is naturally also irrelevant for the d-folio of any rooted graph (G, R) where R consists of k roots taken from R.Theorem 1.3 yields an algorithm for computing folios: If the graph has treewidth upper bounded by β(k, b, d ), one computes (k, d )-folio (G, R ) via dynamic programming, in 2poly (β(k,b,d )) ·| G| time; otherwise, one deletes an irrelevant vertex and recurses. This gives the following algorithmic application of Theorem 1.3. 

Theorem 1.4. There exists an algorithm that, given a rooted graph (G, R) with bidim (G, R) ≤ b

and |R| ≤ k, computes (d, k )-folio (G, R) in 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G|2 time. As bidim (G, R ) ≤ √|R|, we readily obtain the following corollary of Theorem 1.4. 

Corollary 1.5. There exists an algorithm that, given a rooted graph (G, R) where |R| ≤ k, 

computes (k, d )-folio (G, R) in 22poly (k+d)

· | G|2 time. A nice feature of the above is that Theorem 1.4 and Corollary 1.5 also give rise to algorithms that compute the (k, d )-folio of (G, R ), when |R| ≤ k, in 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

·| G|2 time or in 22poly (k+d)

·| G|2

time (based on the fact that bidim (G, R ) ≤ √|R|), see Lemma 13.1 and Corollary 13.2. For a discussion on possible extensions of this without demanding |R| ≤ k, see Section 13.2. We emphasise an additional feature of how Theorem 1.3 can be applied to prove Theo-rem 1.4. What the irrelevant vertex argument provides, under the light of Theorem 1.3, is a 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G|2 time preprocessing algorithm that transforms an annotated graph 

(G, R ), where bidim (G, R ) ≤ b, into an annotated graph (G′, R ) where for every ordered multiset 5R with |R| ≤ k and RR ⊆ R it holds that d-folio (G′, R) = d-folio (G, R). Notice that this is precisely the definition of v being strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R ). Here the annotated set R is preserved and G′ is an induced subgraph of G with treewidth in poly (k) · 2poly (b+d).Interestingly, this data reduction applies to every ordered multiset R that may be specified 

after the preprocessing. Consequently, one can subsequently compute d-folio (G, R), via dynamic programming, in 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G′| time. For an application of this feature, see the paragraph on “meta-folios” of the conclusion section, that is Section 13.3. 

Some consequences and applications. Our results have both structural and algorithmic consequences. The key point is that the complexity is governed not merely by the number of terminals, but more precisely by the bidimensionality of the terminal set. Some applications of this follow below. As already mentioned, the case d = 0 of Theorem 1.4 specialises to the k-Disjoint Paths 

problem and yields a 

2poly (k)·2poly (b)

· | G|2

time algorithm for k-Disjoint Paths , where b = bidim (G, T ) ≤ √k for the terminal set T .Even in this special case, no better parametric dependence is known beyond the galactic bounds stemming from the Graph Minors Series and the still enormous – and not explicitly specified – bounds arising from the Linkage Function estimates in [KW10]. For planar graphs, single-exponential bounds were obtained recently in [LMP +20, COO23] and kernelization algorithms have been given in [WZ23]. As we discuss below, our bounds for the Linkage Function in Theorem 1.2 are asymptotically optimal. This implies that, in order to improve the presented running time for the k-Disjoint Paths problem, one would need to expand on the techniques from [LMP +20, COO23] which avoid explicit dynamic programming on tree-decompositions. Another natural specialisation is Rooted Minor Checking . This problem asks, given a rooted graph (G, R) and a rooted graph (H, T ), whether (G, R) contains (H, T ) as a rooted minor. Note that this is just a membership query to the folio of (G, R). Therefore, Theorem 1.4 yields an algorithm for Rooted Minor Checking on general graphs with running time 

2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G|2,

where b := bidim (G, R) and d is the detail of (H, T ). This yields, for the first time, an algorithm for rooted minor checking with reasonable parametric dependencies. The special case k = 0 

gives a 

22poly (|H|)

· | G|2

time algorithm for deciding whether a graph G contains a graph H as a minor. We again emphasise that, to the best of our knowledge, this marks the first time an explicit running time for this problem can be stated. In our view, this goes a long way toward dispelling the reputation of graph minors algorithms as being “galactic-scale”. We now consider a refinement of the main meta-algorithmic consequence of the Graph Minors Series. A graph parameter is a function p mapping graphs to non-negative integers and it is 

minor-monotone if its value on a graph is no smaller than its value on each of its minors. Given such a parameter p, we define Obs (p, k ) as the set of all minor-minimal graphs G with p(G) > k .6The seminal result of the Graph Minors Series is that graphs are well-quasi-ordered under the minor relation [RS04]. This implies that, for every minor-monotone parameter p, there exists a function fp : N → N such that for every k ∈ N, every graph in Obs (p, k ) has size at most fp(k). Since the proof that graphs are well-quasi-ordered by minors is non-constructive (see [FRS87, KR20]), there is no a priori general mechanism for constructing the function fp

from a description of the parameter p.In [FL88], Fellows and Langston revealed the meta-algorithmic potential of the Graph Minors Series (along with its constructibility limitations). We refine this line of reasoning as follows. Suppose that, for some particular p, we are in position to know some fixed upper bound on 

fp(k) and, moreover, p is exponential-time computable, i.e., there is an algorithm computing 

p(G) in 2poly (|G|) time. We directly have an algorithm to compute Obs (p, k ) in 2poly (fp(k)) ,

since there are 2O

> ((fp(k)) 2)

graphs in Obs (p, k ) each of size at most fp(k). By the definition of 

Obs (p, k ), for every k and every graph G, one can decide whether p(G) ≤ k by checking whether 

G contains some graph in Obs (p, k ) as a minor. Therefore, the check whether G contains some graph in Obs (p, k ) can be done in time 2O

> ((fp(k)) 2)

· 22poly (fp(k)) 

· | G|2, by our results. We obtain the following. 

Theorem 1.6. For every exponential-time computable minor-monotone parameter p, there is an algorithm that, given a graph G, decides whether p(G) ≤ k in time 22poly (fp(k)) 

· | G|2.

We view Theorem 1.6 as a refinement of the main algorithmic consequence of the fact that graphs are well-quasi-ordered by the minor relation, proved in [RS04]. The significance of the above statements extends beyond the two endpoint problems discussed above, i.e., Disjoint Paths and Minor Checking . Folios are reusable summaries of bounded-detail rooted structure. Consequently, any algorithm that repeatedly asks whether one of a bounded number of rooted patterns occurs in a graph can benefit from faster folio computation. This includes rooted routing tasks and algorithms that use bounded rooted minors as certificates or obstructions. More generally, every algorithmic framework whose reduction phase proceeds by finding an irrelevant vertex in a big clique-minor or in a big flat wall inherits its quantitative behaviour from the same threshold. Prominent examples of such results whose (meta-)parametric dependencies can be improved using our bounds include finding embeddings in R3 [KKM12], modification problems [FLP +19, SST22, BST23, SST23, MSST24], as well as combinatorial upper bounds to minor obstructions [SST22, PPTW24b]. 

Optimality of our bounds. We also show that our upper bounds are essentially optimal. The lower bounds of [AKK +11, AK19] already show that the exponential dependence in Theorem 1.2 is unavoidable, even in planar graphs. Moreover, as we show in Section 12, this phenomenon is not merely a consequence of having many terminals. The same construction also yields an exponential lower bound when the parameter is expressed in terms of bidimensionality. More precisely, if Theorem 1.2 holds for some threshold function β, then necessarily β(k, b ) ∈ 2Ω( √b).We prove a similar optimality statement for Theorem 1.3, indicating that the contribution of the detail d is essentially optimal. In this case, the argument relies on enriching the counterexample of [AKK +11, AK19] with many pairwise non-isomorphic bounded-degree gadgets of very small size, obtained via asymptotic enumeration of regular graphs. This implies that any threshold function β as the one in Theorem 1.3 must satisfy β(k, b, d ) ∈ 2Ω( d log log d 

> log d
> )

.71.3 Proof overview 

There are two major steps towards our proof of Theorem 1.4. At its heart lies the general bound on the Linkage Function and thus the proof of Theorem 1.2, which on its own spans several sections. Our ultimate goal for this part can be intuitively understood as follows: if there exists an annotated graph (G, R ) admitting a vital R-linkage L with b := bidim (G, R ), then there exists an annotated graph (G′, R ′) admitting a vital R′-linkage L′, such that bidim (G′, R ′) ∈

poly (|L| ) · 2poly (b) and G′ can be embedded in some surface Σ of genus g(b) admitting h(b) holes with g(b), c (b) ∈ poly (b) such that the terminals T ′ are embedded on the boundary components of Σ. Given such an instance (G′, R ′) together with L′, we show that the treewidth of (G′, R ′)

must be bounded in poly (|R′| + b′), where b′ := bidim (G′, R ′). As a consequence of the above we have b′ ∈ poly (|R|) · 2poly (b), concluding the proof of Theorem 1.2. In a second step we leverage Theorem 1.2 to derive an algorithmic version of Theorem 1.3. This involves another two-step process. First, we make use of Theorem 1.2 directly to prove an analogue of the Annulus Combing Lemma of Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [GST23a], with better bounds, and respecting the bidimensionality of the terminals. Later, we leverage this lemma to show that minor models of partially disc-embedded graphs may be redrawn inside a large flat wall – given certain homogeneity assumptions. This hues close to a lemma by Baste, Sau, and Thilikos [BST23]. However, their result focussed solely on the detail and does not analyse the role of the terminals, requiring more work from our side again. Finally, our result may then be combined with a recent homogenisation technique by Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht [GSW26] in order to complete the proof of Theorem 1.3. We start with a sketch of the proof of Theorem 1.2. Let (G, R ), L, and b be as above. Assume, towards a contradiction, that G has larger treewidth than β1.2(k, b ) where k := |L| .As a first step, we use standard machinery of Robertson and Seymour [RS95] together with recent results of Protopapas, Thilikos, and Wiederrecht [PTW25] on R-minors to prove that G

cannot admit a clique of order poly (b) as a minor, as otherwise L was not vital. 

Hunting down the apex. Applying the main result of [GPW26] (see Proposition 3.8), we use the absence of a clique to derive the existence of a surface Σ of genus at most poly (b), an apex set 

A of size |A| ∈ poly (b) such that the graph (G − A, R \ A) admits a so-called “almost embedding” with ≤ poly (b) regions – called the “vortices” – that exhibit non-planar behaviour – called 

depth – controlled in poly (b) and a “flat” (think of it as having “planar behaviour”) β(k, b )-wall 

W, such that all the vertices in R \ A are part of vortices. Note that since L was vital for G, 

there is a natural vital linkage L′ in G′ := G − A obtained from L by deleting A, where each vertex in A may result in two new terminals in L′. Thus, L′ is a vital R′-linkage in G − A

where |R′| ≤ | R| + 2 |A|. Further b′ := bidim (G − A, R ′) ∈ O(b). However, note that the newly introduced terminals may not be part of vortices and it is a priori unclear where they are located with respect to the wall W .Thus we start by finding a large subwall W ′ of W disjoint from these terminals and declaring each spot in which such a new terminal sits to be a new vortex, introducing at most poly (b)

new vortices. We then need to find disjoint sets of poly (b) concentric cycles around our vortices while maintaining a sizeable subwall of W ′ disjoint from these cycles. Here we build on ideas of Diestel, Kawarabayashi, Müller, and Wollan [DKMW12]. However, their techniques may introduce new apices outside of the existing vortices, further creating even more vortices, and 8thus threatening a loop. We therefore need to significantly alter their approach: we accomplish this by directly manipulating the surface and the almost embedding instead of deleting parts of the graph, while maintaining our bounds and the vitality of the linkage. 

Taming vortices. Given the set of poly (b) vortices we need to “tame” the behaviour of G

in these regions. Here we build on ideas pioneered by Huynh [Huy09] and refined by Diestel et al. [DKMW12], who take a vortex of depth at most d and (d + 1) concentric cycles tightly around it and “push” the cycles into the vortex as deeply as possible, where the bounded depth guarantees that at least one cycle will remain completely outside of that region. One then extends the vortex to the disc bounded by said cycle. Unfortunately, we cannot use these results directly, since they again introduce apices that we cannot afford and require that vortices come with (d + 1) “private” concentric cycles, such that no vortex is part of the disc bounded by a cycle of another vortex. We cannot guarantee this easily without introducing an exponential dependency on our parameters. Fortunately we do not need too. Expanding on the techniques in their proof, we show that if every vortex – call them c1, . . . , c m – comes with a family Ci of poly (d) many concentric cycles such that either all of the cycles Ci lie in the disc bounded by some cycle of Cj or are completely disjoint from it, then an “apex-less” variation of the above procedure still works. The main idea is that, if an untamed vortex c1 together with its concentric cycles C1 is completely contained in the disc of some cycle of C2 say, then when we tame c2 by pushing the (d + 1) closest cycles in C2 into c2, the resulting set of tight cycles will never interfere with most of the cycles in C1, as otherwise we could deflect them resulting in tighter cycles and in particular, the cycles will never penetrate c1. Thus the vortex c1 will be either completely absorbed by the vortex c2

or, after taming c2, it will still be outside of the vortex and have most of the cycles of C1 left in order to tame c1 in the future. That way, we can tame our vortices by guaranteeing only 

poly (d) ∈ poly (b) cycles around each of them, which the previous step of the proof provides. Note that after linking the vortices we are still embedded with up to poly (b) many vortices, although some vortices might have been swallowed by others along the way. 

No apex, no cry. We are left with an instance (G, T ), L embedded on a surface Σ of genus 

g(b) with h(b) holes, at most poly (b) vortices, all of them tame, and a set of β(k, b ) many concentric cycles C coming from the wall W . Building on ideas of [RS09, CKK24] we study the structure of vortices in the presence of vital linkages, proving that the vital linkage interacts with vortices in a very restrictive way. Robertson and Seymour [RS09] essentially prove that either a tamed vortex has a short boundary or most of the vital linkage must be contained in the vortex. However, the main technical lemma of their proof already produces a doubly exponential bound on the required length of the vortex that is involved. Thus we need to avoid this. Denote by S(L) the set of all the maximal subpaths of L with endpoints in vortices that are otherwise disjoint from vortices. We then prove that given a disc ∆( S1, S 2) ⊆ Σ, where S1, S 2

are disjoint regions on the boundary of some vortices (possibly the same), such that ∆( S1, S 2) is otherwise disjoint from all vortices, ∆( S1, S 2) cannot contain more than poly (k) · 2poly (d) paths in S(L) with one endpoint in S1 and the other in S2, where d ∈ poly (b) is the maximal depth of the vortices. Hence the number of homotopic paths of S(L) that are not null-homotopic – they do not bound a disc – is bounded in poly (k) · 2poly (b). Unfortunately, we cannot bound the number 9of null-homotopic paths – it may be more than single-exponential in b – and the respective techniques in [RS09] are of no direct use to us. Fortunately, it turns out that we do not need to bound the number of these paths. It suffices to prove that every null-homotopic path does not interact with most of the cycles in C. We are able to guarantee this by combining cheap linkage techniques in the spirit of [AKK +11, CKK24] in order to gather further structural insight on the interaction of S(L) with C. Assuming one of these paths reaches too deeply into C, we prove the existence of a set of null-homotopic paths in 

S(L) forming a large linkage with one endpoint in S1 and the other in S2 for two disjoint regions on some common vortex. Then we can again apply the result above, proving that ∆( S1, S 2)

cannot contain more than poly (k) · 2poly (b) paths in S(L) with one endpoint in S1 and the other in S2. In particular, at most poly (k) · 2poly (b) many circles of C were intersected. This in turn implies that a large subset C′ of the β(k, b ) cycles – for an appropriate choice as will be discussed in the next paragraph – have not been intersected. We then delete all of the vertices that are part of vortices, all simple loops, and the edges of all cycles outside C′. This produces an instance (G′, T ′) embedded in Σ without vortices, admitting 

C′ concentric cycles and a vital T ′-linkage L′, such that for each homotopy type of paths in L

there exist at most poly (k) · 2poly (b) many paths, none of which are simple loops. Finally, it is an easy combinatorial exercise to prove that a surface Σ with genus g(b) ∈ poly (b)

and h(b) ∈ poly (b) holes admits at most poly (b) paths that are pairwise not homotopic. Thus, L′

admits at most poly (k) · 2poly (b) paths and |T ′| ≤ poly (k) · 2poly (b). Note that this potentially results in 2poly (b) new terminals. However, the bidimensionality of (G′, T ′) is still bounded in 

poly (g(b) + h(b)) and hence in poly (b).

The bounded genus case. We now have an annotated graph (G, R ), with G embedded on a surface Σ of genus at most g = g(b) ∈ poly (b) and there exist at most h = h(b) ∈ poly (b) holes in Σ such that all vertices of R are drawn on the boundary of those holes. We aim to show that, if G contains a family C of λ(g, h, k ) ∈ poly (k) · 2poly (g+h) concentric cycles embedded in a disc ∆ ⊆ Σ and L is an R-linkage of order at most poly (k) · 2poly (b), then 

L avoids the innermost cycle from C. This implies that such a linkage L cannot be vital. Notice that here the total number of terminals, while polynomial in k, depends exponentially on b.In order to handle this situation and to ensure that all remaining exponential dependencies remain polynomial in this already large number of terminals, we revisit an unpublished proof by Mazoit [Maz13] from 2013. In his preprint, Mazoit gave a sketch of a proof establishing single-exponential bounds for the Linkage Function for graphs of Euler-genus at most g. While the core ideas presented in [Maz13] turn out to be correct and crucial for our goal, the paper contains several flaws and has – at the time of writing – only appeared on arxiv.org . For the sake of ensuring correctness and completeness, we include a full, detailed, and reworked proof of Mazoit’s core result. In particular, we extract and highlight a crucial dichotomy in the dependencies on h, g, and k from Mazoit’s ideas as indicated above: 

The exponential dependency of the function λ is entirely confined to h and g while the dependency on the number k of terminals remains polynomial in k.

The main idea of the proof is to make use of conditions on when a linkage that is feasible in the topological sense – meaning that it could be drawn on the surface, ignoring the underlying graph 10 itself – can actually be realised in the embedded graph. The proof reduces to three core pieces: (1) The case when h = g = 1 , that is Σ is a disc, requires only that λ(1 , 1, k ) ∈ O(k). (2) There exists a theorem by Geelen, Huynh, and Richter [GHR18] saying that for every non-separating curve γ with both ends on the boundary of some surface, any path of any linkage drawn in the surface can be redrawn to cross γ only twice. (3) One may interpret the surface Σ as consisting entirely of the disc ∆ together with a number s ∈ O(g + h) many strips attached to ∆. Using (1) as the base case, the claim now follows by induction on the number s of strips, guaranteed by (3), where (2) acts as the tool to replace a single strip with a bounded number of additional terminals by sacrificing a corresponding amount of the concentric cycles. Combining the steps described above concludes the proof sketch for Theorem 1.2; we continue with Theorem 1.3. 

Combing an annulus. We say that a graph G is partially disc-embedded if there exists a disc 

∆ and a triple (G1, G 2, Γ) where G = G1 ∪ G2, G 1 and G2 intersect in vertices only, and Γ is an embedding of G1 in ∆ such that precisely the vertices in V (G1) ∩ V (G2) are drawn on the boundary of ∆. Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [GST23a] proved a useful technical lemma. Let G be partially disc-embedded as above such that there exists a family C of a′(k) concentric cycles crossed by a′(k) paths R – called the rails – and let ⊚ ⊆ ∆ denote the annulus between the innermost and the outermost cycle of C. Then, if G contains a linkage L of order at most k

such that no terminal of L lies in ⊚, then there also exists a linkage L′ in G of the same pattern as L such that L′ intersects ⊚ only in R. This lemma is known as the Annulus Combing Lemma .The original version of this lemma had no additional assumptions on the terminals of L and had a quadratic dependency on the Linkage Function. We provide an independent proof using Theorem 1.2 while introducing the additional measure of bidimensionality on the terminals and improving the dependencies to being linear in the Linkage Function. Notably, our proof of the Annulus Combing Lemma is the only place where we directly use Theorem 1.2. Once this lemma is proven, all further algorithmic arguments build on it instead of Theorem 1.2. While this might already be of independent interest, we then proceed by proving a further strengthening of a similar combing lemma from the work of Baste, Sau, and Thilikos [BST23]. This lemma allows us, given that the partial disc-embedding is actually an almost embedding and there exists a large wall W drawn in the interior of the disc – given that W satisfies a condition known as homogeneity – to redraw any rooted minor model of bounded detail in a way such that it avoids the centre of W entirely. As before, we obtain much better dependencies than the original result in order to prove a version sensitive to the bidimensionality of the roots to adapt the lemma to our setting. As a side effect, our results in this part show that the highly technical definitions originating from recent controversies about technical details regarding the Flat Wall Theorem (see for example [Arn23, SST24]) may be completely avoided and simplified. 

Who cares about this vertex? At this point, most major pieces towards the proof of Theorem 1.3 are in place. Indeed, we actually prove an algorithmic version of Theorem 1.3 in Section 11 and all of our algorithmic main results are derived from it in Section 2. There are only two remaining pieces to be discussed: Firstly, we require a way to obtain a clique-minor, a small width tree-decomposition, or a large flat wall W in linear time in order to guarantee the quadratic running time of Theorem 1.4. Moreover, we require this flat wall W to satisfy some additional properties. One of them is a small connectivity property, and the other is the previously mentioned “homogeneity”. To find one of the three required outcomes while also ensuring that this connectivity requirement is met by our wall, we employ a recent result due to Sau, Stamoulis, 11 and Thilikos [SST24]. Homogeneity is slightly more tricky. It has been observed in the literature that [SST20, SST22, SST23, MSST24] the original definitions for homogeneity and the original proofs by Robertson and Seymour seem to require an additional exponential blow-up in order to ensure W to be homogeneous. However, within our proofs we are able to utilise the recent homogeneity framework, due to Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht [GSW26], which ensures that the price of homogeneity remains polynomial. This last piece of technology allows us to avoid the very last source of possible exponential explosion and, by combining these theorems with our strengthened Annulus Combing Lemma for rooted minors, we are able to show that no rooted minor of small detail in (G, R ) cares about the vertices in the very centre of W .

1.4 Organisation 

After the introduction, we start with a more in-depth explanation of the Graph Minor Algorithm in Section 2, which provides more careful definitions and explanations for the variants of the 

Folio -problem we consider and also illustrates how this allows us to resolve k-Disjoint Paths 

and H-Minor Checking . Once this is taken care of, we provide necessary definitions for the rest of the work (some of which are covered in Section 2) in Section 3. The first proper step into the proofs is taken in Section 4, which starts by introducing tools for a common setting we will encounter throughout our work – namely a set of concentric cycles in a disc with a linkage running through it – and then moves on to providing a fresh presentation and correction of the crucial core of Mazoit’s perspective on vital linkages in graphs embedded on a surface of bounded genus. This is followed by a pair of sections which shows how to reduce the case in which we are almost embedded on a surface without apices by first showing how to tame vortices in Section 5 and then doing the arduous technical core of the proof of Theorem 1.2 in Section 6 by reducing the apex-free case to graphs embedded on surfaces of bounded genus. Section 7 then takes care of getting rid of apices in an almost embedding and building the prerequisites to apply the results of Sections 5 and 6, while Section 8 presents a small toolkit to deal with the presence of large clique-minors in our graph. The proof of our variant of the Vital Linkage Theorem (see Theorem 1.2) is then laid out by combining the results of the previous sections in Section 9. The second half of our paper begins with Section 10, in which we provide all the technical tools we need for the Folio -problem that can be derived from Theorem 1.2, including the Annulus Combing Lemma and its variant for rooted minors. These results are then used to prove the existence of irrelevant vertices for the Folio -problem within graphs of high treewidth in Section 11, which is capped by showing that this existence-result can be used algorithmically to find such a vertex in linear time. We close our work by first giving proofs for several lower bounds to our results in Section 12, which demonstrate that what we prove is essentially optimal in several ways. This is then contrasted by some comments and suggestions for future research directions in Section 13 to explain that there is indeed still a lot of potential for further developments in this area. 12 2 The Graph Minor Algorithm 

In this section we provide proofs for our algorithmic main results, namely Theorem 1.3, Theo-rem 1.4, and – as a direct consequence – Theorem 1.6. To do this, we provide a small amount of formal definitions and references to later points in the paper for those definitions that are too bulky and technical to be explained at this point. The proofs of our main theorems are essentially reductions to more powerful and formal variants, namely Theorem 2.2 and Theorem 2.5 whose proof spans mostly Section 10 and Section 11. The proof of our most central result, that is the single-exponential bound on the Linkage Function conditioned on the bidimensionality of the annotated vertex set as manifested in the form of Theorem 1.2 is confined to the proof of Theorem 9.1 in Section 9 and spans all of Sections 4 to 9. We begin by introducing some general notation and some of our core concepts. 

2.1 A small set of preliminary definitions 

First, we introduce notation for commonly used concepts. By N we denote the set of non-negative integers. Given any two integers a, b ∈ N, we write [a, b ] for the set {z ∈ N : a ≤ z ≤ b}. Notice that the set [a, b ] is empty whenever a > b. For any positive integer c we set [c] := [1 , c ]. In order to avoid ambiguity for this notation, we denote closed intervals over the reals by [x, y ]R.In several contexts, we will have a set G of graphs and want to access the graph their union comprises or want to interact with its edges or vertices. We use ⋃ G as for ⋃ 

> G∈G

G and may thus access the vertices and edges as V (G) := V (⋃ G) and E(G) := E(⋃ G).

Annotated graphs and minors. A minor-model φ of a graph H in a graph G is a function mapping V (H) to a collection of pairwise disjoint connected subsets of V (G), called the branch sets , such that for all uv ∈ E(H) there is an edge in G between φ(u) and φ(v).An annotated graph is a pair (G, R ) where G is a graph and R ⊆ V (G) is the set of annotated vertices , which we often simply refer to as red vertices . If we need easy access to the number of vertices in R, we say that (G, R ) is an |R|-annotated graph . The bidimensionality of an annotated graph (G, R ) is the largest integer k such that there exists a minor-model φ of the 

(k × k)-grid where R ∩ φ(x)̸ = ∅ for all vertices of the grid. In general, we say that an annotated graph (H, R H ) is a red minor of (H, R G) if there exists a minor-model φ of H in G such that 

RG ∩ φ(v)̸ = ∅ for all v ∈ RH .We note that, in an annotated graph (G, R ), declaring a set S ⊆ V (G) to also be red increases the bidimensionality by no more than |S|.

Observation 2.1. Let (G, R ) be an annotated graph and S ⊆ V (G), then 

bidim (G, R ∪ S) ≤ bidim (G, R ) + |S|.

Grids and Walls. Let n, m ≥ 1 be integers. The (n × m)-grid is the graph with vertex set 

[n] × [m] where {(i1, j 1), (i2, j 2)} is an edge if and only if |i1 − i2| + |j1 − j2| = 1 . Given an integer n, the elementary n-wall is the graph obtained from the (n × 2n)-grid by deleting all edges of the form  

> •

{(i, j ), (i + 1 , j )} for i ∈ [n − 1] odd and j ∈ [2 n] even, and 13 Figure 1: A 7-wall.  

> •

{(i, j ), (i + 1 , j )} for i ∈ [n − 1] even and j ∈ [2 n] odd. See Figure 1 for an illustration. An n-wall is any graph that can be obtained from the elementary 

n-wall by subdividing its edges. One can easily see that any graph that contains the (n × 2n)-grid as a minor must contain an 

n-wall as a subgraph. Moreover, every n-wall contains the (n × n)-grid as a minor. 

Treewidth and tree-decompositions. A tree-decomposition for a graph G is a pair (T, β ) such that T is a tree, β : V (T ) → 2V (G) assigns to each node of T a subset of the vertices of G

known as a bag , ⋃   

> t∈V(T)

G[β(t)] = G, and for each v ∈ V (G), the set {t ∈ V (T ) : v ∈ β(t)}

is connected. The adhesion of (T, β ) is 0 if T has only one node and max dt ∈E(T ) |β(d) ∩ β(t)|

otherwise. The width of (T, β ) is defined as max t∈V (T ) |β(t)| − 1. The treewidth of a graph G, 

denoted by tw (G), is the smallest integer k such that G has a tree-decomposition of width at most k.

A rough description of the Flat Wall Theorem. The Flat Wall Theorem of Robertson and Seymour [RS95] may be seen as a fundamental stepping stone towards the full structure theorem for H-minor-free graphs. Indeed, it is sometimes referred to as the “weak structure theorem” as it asserts that every graph of large treewidth must contain a big “area” that roughly behaves like a planar graph. To formally define flat walls requires some amount of machinery that is beyond the scope of this section. Instead, here we provide some intuition behind the meaning of the Flat Wall Theorem and refer the reader to Section 3.2 for the formal definitions. The so-called Two Paths Theorem (see for example [Jun70, Shi80, Sey80, Tho80]) provides a characterisation for all No -instances of the 2-Disjoint Paths problem: Given distinct terminals 

s1, s 2, t 1, t 2 in a graph G, then there do not exist vertex-disjoint paths P1 and P2 such that Pi

connects si and ti in G if and only if G can be “reduced” to a planar graph where the four vertices s1, s 2, t 1, t 2 appear on a common face in the order listed. Here the term “reduced” means that one is allowed to hide non-planar parts of the graph behind separators of size at most 3.The core observation here is that, in case s1, s 2, t 1, t 2 appear on a common face of some planar graph H in the order listed, then any two paths P1 and P2 as above would be forced to cross each other, thereby contradicting the planarity of H.14 The Flat Wall Theorem 3 now asserts that for any graph G with a large enough wall W ⊆ G one of two things hold: i) either G contains a big clique-minor, or ii) there exists a small set A ⊆ V (G) and a big subwall W ′ ⊆ W − A such that, in the subgraph H of G − A consisting of everything that attaches to the “interior” of W ′, there are no two vertex-disjoint paths joining the pairs of opposing corners of W ′.In other words, the Two Paths Theorem says that in the second outcome above, the part of H

that contains “most” of W ′ can be seen as being planar up to separations of order at most 3.We refer to the graph H has the compass of the flat wall W ′ in G − A.It is an intriguing feature of Robertson and Seymour’s Graph Minor Algorithm that most of the effort goes into the proof of its correctness. That is, in order to show that in any instance 

(G, R) of large treewidth there exists some vertex v ∈ V (G) such that (G, R) and (G − v, R) are equivalent instances they employ almost the entire breadth of structure theorems that appeared in their Graph Minors Series. However, once the correctness is proven, it suffices to find either a clique-minor or a flat wall of large enough order to detect such an irrelevant vertex v as above. 

2.2 The folio problem 

At the heart of the Graph Minor Algorithm sits the so-called Rooted d-Folio problem. In this problem one is given as input a graph G together with a set of k special vertices r1, . . . , r k

with labels and an integer d. The question is, what are the minors of G “rooted” on the ri and with “detail” at most d, that is, with at most d additional vertices and at most d edges. This means, instead of finding a single minor of bounded size, or a single pattern of disjoint paths with ends on the roots, the algorithm finds all possible such rooted minors and patterns up to the bound d + k on the number of vertices. 

Rooted graphs. Let k ≥ 0 be an integer. A k-rooted graph is a pair (G, R) where R =

{r1, r 2, . . . , r k} ⊆ V (G) is a multiset of k not necessarily distinct vertices. We sometimes write 

(G, r 1, . . . , r k) instead of (G, R). The ri are called the roots of (G, R). A pair (G, R) is a rooted graph if it is a k-rooted graph for some k.Notice that we make a distinction between a rooted graph (G, R) and an annotated graph 

(G, R ). One core difference between the two concepts is that for a rooted graph the labels of the roots matter, while for an annotated graph, annotated vertices are interchangeable. The other key difference is the fact that R is an ordered multiset . That is, we allow for vertices to appear more than once in R and all vertices in R carry distinct labels. In fact, as mentioned in the introduction, every rooted graph (G, R) gives rise to a uniquely determined annotated graph (G, R R) where RR is obtained from R by forgetting the labels and removing duplicates. We also extend the notion of bidimensionality of annotated graphs to rooted graphs in this way. That is, bidim (G, R) := bidim (G, R R).We say that two rooted graphs (G1, r 11, . . . , r 1

> k

) and (G2, r 21, . . . , r 2

> k

) are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism φ from G1 to G2 such that φ(r1 

> i

) = r2 

> i

for all i ∈ [k].The detail of a rooted graph (G, R) is defined as detail (G, R) := max {| V (G) \ R| , |E(G)|} .

> 3Recall that the formal definitions may be found in Section 3.2 and the variant of the Flat Wall Theorem we are using may be found in Proposition 11.10.

15 The d-folio of a rooted graph. A rooted graph (H, q 1, . . . , q k) is a rooted minor of another rooted graph (G, r 1, . . . , r k) if there exists a minor model φ of H in G such that for every i ∈ [k]

it holds that ri ∈ φ(qi).Given an integer d ≥ 0 and a rooted graph (G, R), the d-folio of G, denoted as d-folio (G, R), is the set of all rooted graphs (H, Q) of detail at most d that are rooted minors of (G, R).

The (k, d )-folio with respect to a vertex set. We now let (G, R ) be an annotated graph. The 

(k, d )-folio of (G, R ), denoted by (k, d )-folio (G, R ), is the union of the d-folios over all rooted graphs (G, R) where R = {r1, . . . , r k} is an ordered multisubset of R.

The (k, d )-Folio problem and its rooted sibling. The (k, d )-Folio problem may now be formally defined as the problem that takes as input an annotated graph (G, R ) and whose task is the computation of the (k, d )-folio of (G, R ).The Rooted d-Folio problem is closely related to the (k, d )-Folio problem. Here, the input is a rooted graph (G, R) with |R| ≤ k and the task is to determine d-folio (G, R).We stress that (k, d )-Folio with input (G, R ) may be reduced to computing d-folio (G, R) for all rooted graphs (G, R) where R is a set of k roots selected from R.To see that the k-Disjoint Paths problem is contained in the Rooted 0-Folio problem on 2k roots, consider the following example. Let H = ( H, x 1, x 1, x 2, x 2, . . . , x k, x k) be the rooted graph with 2k roots and vertex set {x1, . . . , x k} and where E(H) = ∅. Now, if G =(G, s 1, t 1, s 2, t 2, . . . , s k, t k) is a rooted graph with distinct vertices s1, . . . , s k, t 1, . . . , t k, then there exist k pairwise vertex-disjoint paths P1, . . . , P k such that Pi has endpoints si and ti if and only if H is a rooted minor of G. To see this, notice that the existence of the paths Pi is enough to produce a rooted minor model of H in G. For the other direction, if there is a rooted minor model φ of H in G, then for each i ∈ [k] it must be true that si, t i ∈ φ(xi) and, since the φ(xi)

are connected and pairwise disjoint it follows that each φ(xi) contains one such path Pi.It is easily seen that by definition, H is a rooted graph with detail 0 and thus, in or-der to solve the k-Disjoint Paths problem on the instance G, it suffices to solve the 

Rooted 0-Folio problem on the rooted graph (G, {s1, . . . , s k, t 1, . . . , t k}) and check if H ∈

0-Folio (G, {s1, . . . , s k, t 1, . . . , t k}).Hence, in order to prove Theorem 1.4, Theorem 1.6, and to obtain a double-exponential FPT -algorithm for k-Disjoint Paths it suffices to prove the following theorem. 

Theorem 2.2. There exists an algorithm for Rooted d-Folio that takes as input a rooted graph (G, R) with bidim (G, R) ≤ b and |R| ≤ k and runs in time 22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|2.Since the number of possible k-tuples with elements from a set R of order k is in O(kk) and by our discussion on how (k, d )-Folio may be reduced to Rooted d-Folio , Theorem 2.2 gives rise to the following corollary. 

Corollary 2.3. There exists an algorithm for (k, d )-Folio that takes as input an annotated graph (G, R ) with bidim (G, R ) ≤ b and |R| ≤ k and runs in time 22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|2.When (G, R ) is an annotated graph with |R| ≤ k then known algorithms are already able to solve 

(k, d )-Folio and its rooted counterpart in single-exponential time depending on the treewidth of G. See for example the algorithm of Adler, Dorn, Fomin, Sau, and Thilikos [ADF +11]. 16 Proposition 2.4 (Adler, Dorn, Fomin, Sau, and Thilikos [ADF +11]) . There exists an algorithm for Rooted d-Folio that takes as input a rooted graph (G, R) with tw (G) ≤ w and |R| ≤ k

and runs in time 2poly (w+k+d) · | G|.Hence, the only remaining step towards deriving Theorem 2.2 is an algorithmic version of Theorem 1.3. In particular, in order to meet the quadratic running time of the algorithm from Theorem 2.2, we require a way to find a single irrelevant vertex in linear time . Indeed, the following is the algorithmic core of this paper. 

Strongly irrelevant vertices. Let k, d be non-negative integers and (G, R ) be an annotated graph. We say that a vertex v ∈ V (G) \ R is strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R ) if for every ordered multiset R consisting of k roots chosen from among the vertices of R it holds that 

d-folio (G − v, R) = d-folio (G, R).

Theorem 2.5. There exists a function f2.5 : N3 → N and an algorithm that takes as input an annotated graph (G, R ) of bidimensionality at most b, and either correctly determines that 

tw (G) ≤ f2.5(k, b, d ) or finds a vertex v ∈ V (G) \ R such that v is strongly irrelevant for 

(k, d )-folio (G, R ) in time 22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|. Moreover, f2.5(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k).Here something interesting happens. Our observation that (k, d )-Folio can naturally be reduced to solving Rooted d-Folio on all arising rooted graphs also gives rise to a reverse observation when it comes to the irrelevance of some vertex. If there is a vertex v ∈ V (G) that is strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R ) then this means in particular that also d-folio (G, R) = 

d-folio (G − v, R) for all possible ways to select an ordered multiset R of k roots from R. Hence, in the main body of the paper we will only be concerned with the annotated version, that is with (k, d )-folio (G, R ).The linear running time of the algorithm from Theorem 2.5 allows for the following simple algorithm meeting the requirement of Theorem 2.2. 

Input: A rooted graph (G, R) with bidim (G, R R) ≤ b and |R| ≤ k.

Step 1: Run the algorithm from Theorem 2.5 on (G, R). This yields one of two outcomes: 

Case 1.1: The treewidth of G is at most f2.5(k, b, d ). In this case we forward (G, R) to 

Step 2 .

Case 1.2: The algorithm returns a vertex v ∈ V (G) \ RR such that v is strongly irrelevant for (k, d )-folio (G, R R). In this case forward (G − v, R) back to the beginning of 

Step 1 .

Step 2: Run the algorithm from Proposition 2.4 on (G, R), then return d-folio (G, R) and 

terminate the algorithm. Since the instance shrinks by one vertex every time the algorithm enters Case 1.2 , the loop in 

Step 1 repeats at most |G| times. Hence, since the algorithm from Theorem 2.5 runs in linear time, we are sure to enter Step 2 after at most |G| iterations and at most |G|2 steps. 

# 3 Preliminaries 

There are a variety of established frameworks for the work in the structural theory surrounding the Graph Minors Series and careful definitions are needed throughout. We opt to mainly work 17 from a set of definitions for annotated graphs that is taken from [GPW26] and which are largely based on definitions that evolved from the Graph Minors Series by Robertson and Seymour and were refined in [KTW18, KTW21, GSW25]. Later on in Section 10, we will expand our view and consider graphs in which vertices may be marked with many different colours. As we will need these notions briefly in Section 8, we introduce them here as well, but will be able to avoid working in this more complicated setting for most of the paper. 

3.1 General definitions 

Here, we introduce a series of fundamental definitions required throughout the paper. 

Paths, linkages, and separations. A linkage L in a graph G is a set of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths. Let T ⊆ V (G) be the set of endpoints of paths in L, then we call L a T -linkage . We define the pattern of L via τ (L) := {{ s, t } | there is L ∈ L with endpoints s and t}.We say that a path P in G is internally disjoint from a set X ⊆ V (G) if V (P ) ∩ X does not contain any vertex of P that is not an endpoint. Given a graph G and two subsets A, B ⊆ V (G),

an A-path in G is a path with both endpoints in A and internally disjoint from A, and an 

A-B-path is a path with one endpoint in A, the other in B, and internally disjoint from A ∪ B.An A-B-linkage in G is a linkage consisting of A-B paths. If H is a subgraph of G, an H-path 

is a V (H)-path of length at least one with no edge in E(H).Let G be a graph and k be a positive integer. A separation of G is a tuple (A, B ) such that 

A, B ⊆ V (G) with A ∪ B = V (G) and for every edge uv ∈ E(G) we have u, v ∈ A or u, v ∈ B.We call |A ∩ B| its order . The following classic tool will be helpful to us in many ways. 

Proposition 3.1 (Menger [Men27]) . Let k be a positive integer, let G be a graph, and let 

X, Y ⊆ V (G) be two sets with at least k elements each. Then there does not exist a separation 

(A, B ) in G of order less than k with X ⊆ A and Y ⊆ B if and only if there exist k pairwise disjoint X-Y -paths in G.

Surfaces. By a surface we mean a compact 2-dimensional manifold Σ with or without boundary. Let X ⊆ Σ. The boundary and interior of X will be denoted bd (X) and int (X), respectively. The topological closure of X is denoted by cl (X). If Σ is a surface with boundary then we refer to the connected components of bd (Σ) as its cuffs .Given a pair (h, c) ∈ N × N we define Σ(h,c) to be the surface without boundary created from the sphere by adding h handles and c crosscaps (see [MT01] for more details). By the surface classification theorem, every compact connected surface without boundary is homeomorphic to Σ(h,c) for some h, c ≥ 0. Note that by Dyck’s theorem [Dyc88, FW99], two crosscaps are equivalent to a handle in the presence of a third crosscap. If c = 0 the surface Σ(h,c) is an orientable surface, otherwise it is called non-orientable . We let the genus of Σ be 2h + c, where Σ(h,c) is a surface to which Σ is homeomorphic. Let Σ and Γ be surfaces. A homeomorphism h : Σ → Γ is a bijective function from Σ to Γ which is continuous and has a continuous inverse. A homeomorphism h : Σ → Σ on a surface Σ with boundary such that h fixes every point on the boundary of Σ is called a bd -homeomorphism .18 Let γ : [0 , 1] R → Σ be continuous and injective on [0 , 1[ R, then we call its image γ([0 , 1] R) a

curve in Σ and γ(0) , γ (1) its endpoints. A curve is closed if its endpoints agree. A curve with both endpoints on the boundary of Σ is called a boundary curve .Two curves C, C ′ ⊆ Σ are homeomorphic if there is a homeomorphism h : Σ → Σ which maps 

C to C′. C and C′ are homotopic if there is a continuous function f : Σ × [0 , 1] R → Σ such that f (x, 0) = C(x) and f (x, 1) = C′(x) and for every t ∈ [0 , 1] R the function ft(x) := f (x, t )

maps the boundary of Σ to the boundary of Σ. We call f a homotopy . Homotopy induces an equivalence relation on the set of curves on a surface Σ. We refer to the equivalence classes as 

homotopy classes and to the class containing a curve C ⊆ Σ its homotopy type .Let Σ be a connected surface. A curve C ⊆ Σ is contractible if one of the components of Σ − C

is homeomorphic to an open disc. Otherwise it is non-contractible . C is separating if Σ − C is not connected. A non-separating, non-contractible curve is called essential or genus reducing .The following is a simple result on the number of homotopy classes of non-contractible curves on a given surface that can be realised simultaneously 4. In [JMM96], Juvan et al. prove similar results for closed curves disjoint from the boundary. See e.g. Lemma 3.2 in that paper. 

Lemma 3.2. Let Σ be a connected surface of genus g with b ≥ 1 boundary components. Let L

be a set of pairwise disjoint simple curves on Σ whose endpoints are all on the boundary of Σ.Then the number of homotopy types realised by curves in L is ≤ 1 in case b = 1 and g = 0 , it is 

≤ 3 in case b = 2 and g = 0 , and otherwise it is bounded by 4b + 6 g − 6.

Proof. In the special case that b = 1 and g = 0 Σ is a disc and L is a set of simple curves with all endpoints on the boundary. Thus all curves in L are null-homotopic and therefore there is only one homotopy type that is realised by L.The other special case we need to consider is b = 2 and g = 0 . In this case Σ is a cylinder and there are at most three possible homotopy types that can be realised simultaneously: one type for each of the two cuffs realised by curves with both endpoints on that cuff and the third type realised by a curve with both endpoints on different cuffs. Note that there is an unbounded number of pairwise non-homotopic curves with endpoints on different cuffs, which are essentially determined by the number of times they wind around the cylinder. But no two of these can be realised at the same time in L.So we may now assume that b > 1 or g > 0. Let L ∈ L be a curve with both endpoints a, b on the same boundary component C of Σ. We say that L is null-homotopic if L together with one of the two components of C − { a, b } bounds a disc on Σ. Clearly any two null-homotopic curves with endpoints on the same boundary are homotopic. Thus there are at most b null-homotopic curves in L which realise different homotopy types in Σ.Let M ⊆ L be a maximal subset of L such that no two curves in M are homotopic and furthermore no curve in M is null-homotopic in the sense above. Let Σ′ be obtained from Σ by closing each cuff C with a disc ∆( C). For each C let T (C) be the endpoints of curves in M on 

C. For each cuff C choose a point v = v(C) ∈ int (∆( C)) and for each u ∈ T (C) a curve Lu in 

∆( C) between v and u such that for u̸ = u′ ∈ T (C) these curves are disjoint except for v. We can then extend each curve L ∈ M with endpoints a, b by the curves La, L b to obtain a new 

> 4This lemma in different formulation is almost certainly found in standard topology texts. Our use for it is related to [Maz13] (see Claim 7 in said paper for comparison.)

19 set M ′ of curves whose endpoints are all contained in {v(C) : C a cuff of Σ}. Without loss of generality we assume that M ′ is maximal in the sense that there is no non-null-homotopic curve with endpoints in {v(C) : C a cuff of Σ} that is internally disjoint from each curve in M ′ and is not homotopic to a curve in M ′.As M ′ does not contain any null-homotopic curve, there is a triangulation N of Σ′ such that 

M ′ ⊆ N . See e.g. [FM12]. By Euler’s formula [Eul58], |V (N )| − | E(N )| + |F (N )| = 2 − 2g, 

where V (N ) denotes the set of vertices of N, E (N ) the set of arcs, and F (N ) the set of faces of the embedding N . By construction, |V (N )| = b and |E(N )| = |N | ≥ | M ′|. Let f := |F (N )|.Thus we obtain |N | = b + f + 2 g − 2.On the other hand, as N triangulates Σ′, we obtain 2|N | = 3 f . Combining the two equations yields |N | = 3 b + 6 g − 6.Thus, together with the at most b null-homotopic curves we removed above, the maximal number of pairwise non-homotopic curves in L is ≤ 4b + 6 g − 6.

Embeddings. Given a graph G, we say that G has an embedding ψ into a surface Σ, if ψ is a function with domain V (G) ∪ E(G) such that i) ψ(v) is a point of Σ for each v ∈ V (G),

ii) ψ(u)̸ = ψ(v) for all distinct u, v ∈ V (G),

iii) ψ(uv ) is a simple curve in Σ with the ends ψ(u), ψ (v) for all uv ∈ E(G),

iv) ψ(uv ) ∩ ψ(wx ) ⊆ { ψ(u), ψ (v)}, for all uv, wx ∈ E(G), and v) ψ(uv ) ∩ ψ(w) = ∅ for all uv ∈ E(G) and w ∈ V (G) \ { u, v }.We let ψ(G) be the union of all points and curves in its image and call the connected components of Σ \ ψ(G) the faces of ψ. If every face of ψ is homeomorphic to an open disc, we say that ψ is a 2-cell-embedding .Given a sequence of cycles C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ in G we call these cycles concentric (with respect to ψ) if there is a disc ∆ ⊆ Σ such that ψ(⋃ C) ⊆ ∆ and ψ(Ci) bounds a disc ∆i such that 

∆1 ⊊ . . . ⊊ ∆s.We will generally work with graphs with a fixed embedding and thus identify the elements of G

with the points and curves in Σ which ψ maps to each other. The minimum number of vertices of G that is intersected by any non-contractible curve in Σ meeting G only in vertices is called the representativity (or face-width ) of ψ. We consider the representativity of an embedding in the sphere to be infinite. 

3.2 Definitions from Graph Minors 

We continue by briefly introducing some key concepts for handling the general structure of 

H-minor-free graphs. 

Separations and tangles. Let G be a graph and k be a positive integer. Let t ≥ 1. A sequence of separations ⟨(Ai, B i)⟩i∈[t] of G is laminar if Ai ⊆ Aj and Bj ⊆ Bi for every pair 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ t.We denote by Sk(G) the collection of all separations (A, B ) of order less than k in G.An orientation of Sk(G) is a set O such that for all (A, B ) ∈ S k(G) exactly one of (A, B ) and 

(B, A ) belongs to O. A tangle of order k in G is an orientation T of Sk(G) such that for all 

(A1, B 1), (A2, B 2), (A3, B 3) ∈ T , it holds that G[A1] ∪ G[A2] ∪ G[A3]̸ = G. If T is a tangle and 

(A, B ) ∈ T we call A the small side and B the big side of (A, B ).20 Let G be a graph and T and D be tangles of G. We say that D is a truncation of T if D ⊆ T .Let G and H be graphs as well as T be a tangle in G. We say that a minor-model μ of H in G

is controlled by T if there does not exist a separation (A, B ) ∈ T of order less than |V (H)| and an x ∈ V (H) such that μ(x) ⊆ A \ B.Let T be a tangle of order k in a graph G and let S ⊆ V (G) be a set of size at most k − 2. Notice that every separation (A, B ) of order at most k − | S| − 1 in G − S corresponds to a separation 

(A ∪ S, B ∪ S) of order at most k − 1 in G. It follows that TS := {(A, B ) : (A ∪ S, B ∪ S) ∈ T } 

is a tangle of order k − | S| in G − S. Now suppose that G − S has components G1, . . . , G p. Then there exists i ∈ [p] such that V (Gi) ⊆ B for all separations of order 0 in TS . We call Gi the 

T -big component of G − S.

Meshes. Let n, m be integers with n, m ≥ 2. A (n × m)-mesh is a graph M which is the union of paths M = P1 ∪ · · · ∪ Pn ∪ Q1 ∪ · · · ∪ Qm where  

> •

P1, · · · , P n are pairwise vertex-disjoint, and Q1, · · · , Q m are pairwise vertex-disjoint.  

> •

for every i ∈ [n] and j ∈ [m], the intersection Pi ∩ Qj induces a path,  

> •

each Pi is a V (Q1)-V (Qm)-path intersecting the paths Q1, · · · Qm in the given order, and each Qj is a V (P1)-V (Pm)-path intersecting the paths P1, · · · , P h in the given order. We say that the paths P1, · · · , P n are the horizontal paths , and the paths Q1, · · · , Q m are the 

vertical paths . The union P1 ∪ Pn ∪ Q1 ∪ Qm is a cycle called the perimeter of M . The unique cycle in the union Pi ∪ Pi+1 ∪ Qj ∪ Qj+1 , where i ∈ [n − 1] and j ∈ [m − 1] , is called a brick 

of M . A mesh M ′ is a submesh of a mesh M if every horizontal (vertical) path of M ′ is a subpath of a horizontal (vertical) path of M, respectively. We write n-mesh as a shorthand for an (n × n)-mesh. Let r ∈ N with r ≥ 3, let G be a graph, and M be an r-mesh in G. Let TM be the orientation of Sr such that for every (A, B ) ∈ T M , the set B \ A contains the vertex set of both a horizontal and a vertical path of M . We call B the M -majority side of (A, B ). Then TM is the tangle 

induced by M . If T is a tangle in G, we say that T controls the mesh M if TM is a truncation of T .

Paintings in surfaces. A painting in a surface Σ is a pair Γ = ( U, N ), where N ⊆ U ⊆ Σ, N is finite, U \ N has a finite number of arcwise-connected components, called cells of Γ, such that each cell is homeomorphic to an open disc in Σ, cl (c) is a closed disc in Σ, and cl (c) ∩ cl (c′) ⊆ N

for two distinct cells. We define NΓ(c) := cl (c) ∩ N . If |NΓ(c)| ≥ 4, the cell c is called a vortex .We further let N (Γ) := N, let U (Γ) := U, and let C(Γ) be the set of all cells of Γ.Any given painting Γ = ( U, N ) defines a hypergraph with N as its vertices and the set of closures of the cells of Γ as its edges. Accordingly, we call N the nodes of Γ.

Σ-renditions. Let G be a graph and Σ be a surface. A Σ-rendition of G is a triple ρ = (Γ , σ, π ),

where  

> •

Γ is a painting in Σ, 

> •

for each cell c ∈ C(Γ) , σ (c) is a subgraph of G, and  

> •

π : N (Γ) → V (G) is an injection, such that

R2 for all distinct c, c ′ ∈ C(Γ) , the graphs σ(c) and σ(c′) are edge-disjoint, 21 R3 π(NΓ(c)) ⊆ V (σ(c)) for every cell c ∈ C(Γ) , and 

R4 for every cell c ∈ C(Γ) , we have V (σ(c) ∩ ⋃

> c′∈C(Γ) \{ c}

(σ(c′))) ⊆ π(NΓ(c)) .We write N (ρ) for the set N (Γ) , let Nρ(c) = NΓ(c) for all c ∈ C(Γ) , and similarly, we lift the set of cells from C(Γ) to C(ρ). If it is clear from the context which ρ is meant, we will sometimes simply write N (c) instead of Nρ(c), and if the Σ-rendition ρ for G is understood from the context, we usually identify the sets π(N (ρ)) and N (ρ) along π for ease of notation. Given a graph G with a Σ-rendition ρ and a subgraph G′ ⊆ G, we define the rendition ρ′ of G′

induced by ρ by letting the nodes of ρ′ be N (ρ) ∩ V (G′), letting the cells of ρ′ be the cells c of ρ

such that (σρ(c) ∩ G′) − N (ρ) is non-empty, and adjusting σ appropriately. Note that every embedding ψ of a graph G in a surface Σ defines a rendition ρψ of G in Σ where the graph of every cell corresponds to a unique edge of G. The cell itself can be an open disc with the two endpoints of the edge in its boundary. Thus all definitions for renditions naturally transfer to embeddings. 

Blank renditions and renditions of annotated graphs. Let ρ = (Γ , σ, π ) be a Σ-rendition of an annotated graph (G, R ). By slightly abusing notation, we call a cell c of ρ a vortex if it is either a vortex of Γ or V (σ(c)) ∩ R̸ = ∅. If 

π(N (ρ)) ∪ ⋃{V (σ(c)) : c ∈ C(ρ) and c is not a vortex }

is disjoint from R, we call ρ a blank rendition (of (G, R )).

Societies. Let Ω be a cyclic ordering of the elements of some set. Given that Ω = ⟨v1, . . . , v r⟩,

then we denote the set {v1, . . . , v r} by V (Ω) . A society is a pair (G, Ω) , where G is a graph and Ω is a cyclic ordering with V (Ω) ⊆ V (G). For a given set S ⊆ V (Ω) an element s ∈ S is an endpoint of S if there exists an element t ∈ V (Ω) \ S that immediately precedes or succeeds 

s in Ω. We call S a segment of Ω if S has two or less endpoints. We call a segment proper if 

V (S)̸ = V (Ω) . The ordering of S induced by Ω is the restriction of Ω to S.Let (G, Ω) be a society and let Σ be a surface with one boundary component B homeomorphic to the unit circle. A rendition of (G, Ω) in Σ is a Σ-rendition ρ of G such that the image under 

πρ of N (ρ) ∩ B is V (Ω) and Ω is one of the two cyclic orderings of V (Ω) defined by the way the points of πρ(V (Ω)) are arranged in the boundary B.

Traces of paths and cycles. Let ρ be a Σ-rendition of a graph G. For every cell c ∈ C(ρ) with 

|Nρ(c)| = 2 , we select one of the components of bd (c) \ N (c). This selection will be called a 

tie-breaker in ρ, and we assume that every rendition comes equipped with a tie-breaker. Let G be a graph and ρ be a Σ-rendition of G. Let Q be a cycle or path in G that uses no edge of σ(c) for every vortex c ∈ C(ρ). We say that Q is grounded if it uses edges of σ(c′) and σ(c′′ )

for two distinct cells c′, c ′′ ∈ C(ρ), or Q is a path with both endpoints in N (ρ). If Q is grounded we define the trace of Q as follows. Let P1, . . . , P k be distinct maximal subpaths of Q such that 

Pi is a subgraph of σ(c) for some cell ci. Fix i ∈ [k]. The maximality of Pi implies that its endpoints are π(n1) and π(n2) for distinct nodes n1, n 2 ∈ N (ρ). If |Nρ(ci)| = 2 , let Li be the component of bd (ci) selected by the tie-breaker, and if |Nρ(ci)| = 3 , let Li be the component of 

bd (ci) \ (N (ci) ∩ V (Pi)) that is disjoint from Nρ(ci). We define L′ 

> i

by pushing Li slightly so that it is disjoint from all cells in C(ρ), while maintaining that the resulting curves intersect only at a common endpoint. 22 The trace of Q is defined to be ⋃

L′
