. If Q is a cycle, its trace is the homeomorphic image of the unit circle, and otherwise, it is an arc in Σ with both endpoints in N (ρ).

Linear decompositions of societies. Let (G, Ω) be a society. A linear decomposition of (G, Ω) is a labelling v1, v 2, . . . , v n of V (Ω) such that v1, v 2, . . . , v n appear in Ω in the order listed, together with sets ordered as ⟨X1, X 2, . . . , X n⟩ such that i) Xi ⊆ V (G) and vi ∈ Xi for all i ∈ [n],

ii) ⋃ 

> i∈[n]

Xi = V (G) and for every uv ∈ E(G) there exists i ∈ [n] such that u, v ∈ Xi, and iii) for every x ∈ V (G) the set {i ∈ [n] : x ∈ Xi} forms an interval in [n].The adhesion of a linear decomposition is max {| Xi ∩ Xi+1 | : i ∈ [n − 1] }. The width of a linear decomposition is max {| Xi| : i ∈ [n]}. We write α = (⟨v1, . . . , v n⟩, ⟨X1, . . . , X n⟩) for the respective linear decomposition. Let 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n and S = [ i, j ]. Then we call (⟨vi, . . . , v j ⟩, ⟨Xi, . . . , X j ⟩)

the S-restriction of α.

Aligned curves and grounded subgraphs. Let G be a graph and let ρ = (Γ , σ, π ) be a Σ-rendition of G. We say that a 2-connected subgraph H of G is grounded (in ρ) if every cycle in H is grounded and no vertex of H is drawn by Γ in a vortex of ρ. A curve in Σ is called 

ρ-aligned if it only intersects Γ in nodes. In turn, a disc in Σ is called ρ-aligned if its boundary is 

ρ-aligned and we call it vortex-free if its interior is disjoint from all vortices in ρ. If H is planar, we say that it is flat in ρ if there exists a vortex-free, ρ-aligned disc ∆ ⊆ Σ which contains all cells c ∈ C(ρ) with E(σ(c)) ∩ E(H)̸ = ∅.For any ρ-aligned disc ∆, we call the subgraph of G that is the union of σ(c) for all cells c of 

ρ in ∆ the crop of G by ∆ (in ρ). Furthermore, the restriction δ′ of ρ by ∆ is defined as the 

∆-rendition that consists of the restriction of both Γ, σ, and π to ∆.This allows to define a society associated with ∆ as follows. Let V (Ω ∆) be the set of all vertices whose corresponding nodes are drawn in the boundary of ∆ and let Ω∆ be the cyclic ordering of 

V (Ω ∆) obtained by traversing along the boundary of ∆ in the anticlockwise direction. Now, let 

G∆ be the crop of G by ∆. We call the society (G∆, Ω∆) the ∆-society (in ρ). If ρ is clear from the context, we do not mention it. We also call the restriction of ρ by ∆, the restriction of ρ to 

(G∆, Ω∆).

Let P be an X-Y -linkage in G such that X ∩ V (G∆) ⊆ V (Ω ∆) and Y ⊆ V (G∆) and assume that each path in P is grounded in ρ. Then we define the ∆-truncation (in ρ) of P to be the 

V (Ω ∆)-Y -linkage in G∆ which consists of the minimal V (Ω ∆)-Y -subpaths of the paths in P.Let ρ be a rendition of a society (G, Ω) in a surface Σ that is not the sphere. Given a cycle 

C ⊆ G that is grounded in ρ we define the C-disc (in ρ) as the unique ρ-aligned disc ∆ ⊆ Σ

bounded by the trace of C in ρ. We also use the terms C-society (in ρ) to denote the ∆-society in ρ and C-truncation (in ρ) to denote the ∆-truncation in ρ of an appropriately defined linkage in G.

Transactions and cylindrical renditions. Let (G, Ω) be a society. A transaction in (G, Ω) is an 

A-B-linkage for disjoint segments A, B of Ω consisting of V (Ω) -paths. A rendition of (G, Ω) in the disc with a unique vortex c0 is called a cylindrical rendition of (G, Ω) around c0.

Nests and radial linkages. Let ρ be a rendition of a society (G, Ω) in a surface Σ other than the sphere. Further, let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ be a sequence of pairwise disjoint cycles in G, such that each of them is grounded in ρ and we have ∆1 ⊊ . . . ⊊ ∆s ⊆ Σ, where ∆i is the Ci-disc for 23 each i ∈ [s]. We say that the cycles in C are concentric (in ρ) and call C1 the inner cycle and 

Cs the outer cycle of C in ρ.A nest C (in ρ) is a set of concentric cycles with the inner cycle C such that every vortex of ρ is found in the C-disc. Moreover, we call a V (Ω) -V (C)-linkage R a radial linkage (in ρ) for C if all paths in R are grounded in ρ and internally disjoint from V (Ω) .If (G, Ω) is a society with a nest C in a rendition ρ of (G, Ω) in a disc, we say that a radial linkage R for C is orthogonal to C if for all C ∈ C and all R ∈ R the graph C ∩ R is a path. 

Inner and outer graphs of a cycle. Let (G, Ω) be a society with a Σ-rendition ρ. Further, let C be a grounded cycle whose trace bounds a disc ∆C and the ∆C -society (G′, Ω′). We call 

G′ ∪ C the inner graph of C (in ρ) and call G′ itself the proper inner graph of C (in ρ). Let B =

π(N (ρ) ∩ bd (∆ C )) . We define the proper outer graph of C (in ρ) as G′′ := G[B ∪ (V (G) \ V (G′))] 

and call G′′ ∪ C the outer graph of C (in ρ). 

Depth of vortices. Let G be a graph and ρ be a Σ-rendition of G with a vortex cell c0. Notice that c0 defines a society (σ(c0), Ωc0 ), where V (Ω c0 ) is the set of vertices of G corresponding to 

Nρ(c0). The ordering Ωc0 is obtained by traversing along the boundary of the closure of c0 in counterclockwise direction. We call (σ(c0), Ωc0 ) as obtained above the vortex society of c0.

We define the depth of a society (G, Ω) as the maximum cardinality of a transaction in (G, Ω) .The depth of the vortex c0 is thereby defined as the depth of its vortex society. It easily follows that every society with a linear decomposition of adhesion at most k has depth at most 2k. What interests us here is the reverse statement which was shown by Robertson and Seymour in [RS90]. 

Proposition 3.3 ( [RS90]) . Let k be a non-negative integer and (G, Ω) be a society of depth at most k. Then (G, Ω) has a linear decomposition of adhesion at most k. 

Finally, we define the width of a Σ-rendition of a graph G as the minimum non-negative integer 

w such that every vortex society of ρ admits a linear decomposition of width at most w. Further, we define the breadth of ρ as the number of vortex cells of ρ and the depth of ρ as the maximum depth of its vortex societies. 

Flat Meshes. Let n ≥ 2 be an integer. Let G be a graph, and let M ⊆ G be an n-mesh. We say that M is a flat mesh in G if there exists a rendition ρ of G on the sphere with a single vortex c0 such that M is flat in ρ and the trace of the perimeter of M in ρ separates all vertices 5

in N (ρ) ∩ V (M ) from c0. We say that ρ witnesses the flatness of M. 

Cylindrical meshes. In some settings it will be easier to work with a version of meshes that is cylindrical. Let m, n be positive integers, let M be a graph, and let C1, . . . , C m be cycles and P1, . . . , P n be paths in M such that the following holds for all i ∈ [m] and j ∈ [n]: 

> •

C1, . . . , C m are pairwise vertex-disjoint, P1, . . . , P n are pairwise vertex-disjoint, and M =

C1 ∪ · · · ∪ Cm ∪ P1 ∪ · · · ∪ Pn. 

> •

Ci ∩ Pj is a path, and if i ∈ { 1, m } or j ∈ { 1, n }, then Ci ∩ Pj has exactly one vertex, 

> 5Note that this may mean that non-nodes belonging to the perimeter of the mesh may be drawn in cells outside of the vortex.

24 • when traversing Ci starting from an endpoint of P1 ∩ Ci, then either the paths P1, . . . , P n

are encountered in the order listed or the next Pj one encounters is Pn and from here the paths are encountered in the order Pn, . . . , P 1, and  

> •

Pj has one end in C1 and the other in Cm, and when traversing Pj starting from its endpoint on C1, the cycles C1, . . . , C m are encountered in the order listed. If the above conditions hold for M, we call M an (n×m)-cylindrical mesh . The cycles C1, . . . , C m

are called the concentric cycles , or cycles , of M and the paths P1, . . . , P n are called the radial paths , or rails , of M . We also call (n × n)-cylindrical meshes n-cylindrical meshes .Let n, m ≥ 3 be integers, G be a graph, and M be an (n × m)-cylindrical mesh in G. Similarly to meshes, let TM be the orientation of Sr, where r = min {n, m }, such that for every (A, B ) ∈ T M ,

the set B \ A contains the vertex set of both a concentric cycle and a radial path of M, we call 

B the M -majority side of (A, B ). Then TM is the tangle induced by M . If T is a tangle in G, 

say that T controls the mesh M if TM is a truncation of T .

3.3 Manipulating vital linkages 

At several points in our proofs we will want to delete vertices, edges, and decompose graphs at separations while preserving the existence of vital linkages. Most of these operations have been considered already by Robertson and Seymour in [RS09] and we will reuse several of their results on these operations. 

Proposition 3.4. Let (G, T ) be an annotated graph and L a vital T -linkage in G. Let (A, B )

be a separation in G and let S := A ∩ B. Let TB := T ∩ B. Then (G[B], T B ∪ S) is an annotated graph admitting a vital (S ∪ TB )-linkage L′ such that each path in L′ is a subpath of a path in L.

Proposition 3.5. Let (G, T ) be an annotated graph and L a vital T -linkage in G. Let 

v ∈ V (G) \ V (T ) and G′ := G − v. Let u, w ∈ V (G) be the two vertices adjacent to v such that uv, vw ∈ E(L) for some L ∈ L . Let T ′ := T ∪ { u, w }. Then (G′, T ′) is an annotated graph admitting a vital T ′-linkage L′ such that each path in L′ is a subpath of a path in L.If v ∈ V (G) is an isolated vertex, then v ∈ T and there is some path L ∈ L consisting solely of v. Further, (G − v, T \ { v}) is an annotated graph with a vital linkage L′ such that L′ ⊆ L .Let G, H be graphs with H ⊆ G and let L be a linkage in G, then we define L ∩ H to be 

{P : L ∈ L and P is a component of H ∩ L}. The following is an immediate consequence of (2.3) in [RS09] 6.

Proposition 3.6. Let G, H be graphs with H ⊆ G and let L be a linkage in G. If L is vital in 

G, then L ∩ H is vital in H.A key tool for us will be [RS09, Theorem 2.5]. This is essential for our later proof of Theorem 6.4 analysing the number of “non-trivial types” a vital linkage can “induce” on the separators given a sequence of laminar separations. The following is a transcription to our setting. 

> 6Note that in Robertson and Seymour’s setting separations are defined as tuples of subgraphs that intersect only in vertices.

25 Proposition 3.7 ([RS09, Theorem 2.5]) . Let k, d ∈ N and let f (k, d ) := (2 k+1)( k+d+1) 2( k+d)+1 .Let L be a vital linkage of order k in a graph G, and for i ∈ [f (k, d )] let (Ai, B i) be a separation of G of order d, such that i) Ai ⊆ Aj and Bj ⊆ Bi for 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n, and ii) for 1 ≤ i < n, there is a linkage Mi in G[Bi] ∩ G[Ai+1 ] of order d, each path of which has one end in Ai ∩ Bi and the other in Ai+1 ∩ Bi+1 .Then there exists i ∈ [n] such that ⋃ L ∩ G[Bi] ∩ G[Ai+1 ] = ⋃ Mi.

3.4 Colorful graphs 

To later deal with the folio-problem, we will need definitions for graphs which feature several different colours. We define these here since we will briefly deal with these graphs and the folio-problem in Section 8 as well. We use the patently incorrect spelling of colour when defining these colorful graphs to stay in line with the name given to them in [PTW25]. Let q be a non-negative integer. A q-colorful graph is a pair (G, χ ) where G is a graph and 

χ : V (G) → 2[q]. For a vertex v ∈ V (G) we call χ(v) the palette of v. A q-colorful graph (G, χ )

where χ(G) ⊊ [q] is called restricted and a q-colorful graph (G, χ ) with χ(G) = ∅ is called blank .A q-colorful graph (G, χ ) is said to be rainbow if χ(v) = [ q] for all v ∈ V (G). Annotated graphs correspond directly to 1-colorful graphs. Let (G, χ ) and (H, ψ ) be colorful graphs. We say that a minor model φ of H in G is a colorful minor model of (H, ψ ) in (G, χ ) if for every v ∈ V (H) and every i ∈ ψ(v) there is some vertex 

u ∈ φ(v) such that i ∈ χ(u). To simplify notation, we may simply say that (H, ψ ) is a colorful minor of (G, χ ). Accordingly, in a 1-colorful graph G that has H as a red minor, H is also a rainbow (colorful) minor. 

3.5 The Local Structure Theorem for annotated graphs 

One of the key results we will use is a variant of the Local Structure Theorem (first proven in [RS03] as (3.1)) recently provided by Gorsky, Protopapas, and Wiederrecht [GPW26]. Their statement of this theorem (see Theorem 5.1 in [GPW26]) is quite a bit more technical than what we use here. We simplify their formulation in three ways. First, we assume that we are working on an annotated graph with bounded dimensionality, which gets rid of one of the options in their statement. Secondly, we will not require the complicated structure that their 

layout -definition provides and thus will simply extract the parts of their structure that are of use to us. (Accordingly the proposition we state here is quite a bit weaker than what they actually prove.) Third, we reformulate their first outcome by digging only mildly into their proof (which uses Proposition 8.2 to guarantee this point) to respect a blank Kt-minor model instead of the mesh. 

Proposition 3.8 (Gorsky, Protopapas, and Wiederrecht [GPW26]) . There exist functions 

apex 3.8, depth 3.8 : N2 → N and mesh 3.8 : N3 → N such that for all integers t ≥ 1, r ≥ 4, and 

w ≥ 3, every annotated graph (G, R ) with bidim (G, T ) ≤ r, and every mesh 3.8(r, t, w )-mesh 

M ⊆ G one of the following holds. i) (G, R ) has a blank K⌊5t/2⌋-minor model ψ and a separation (X, Y ) of order at most t − 1

such that the Tψ-big component of G − (X ∩ Y ) is blank, 26 ii) (G, R ) has a red Kt-minor model controlled by M, or iii) there exists a set A ⊆ V (G) with |A| ≤ apex 3.8(t, r ) and a surface Σ of genus less than 9t2 such that (G − A, R \ A) has a blank Σ-rendition ρ with breadth at most 

> 3

/2(t − 1)(3 t − 4) + r(r − 1) − 3, depth at most depth 3.8(t, r ), and there exists a vortex-free, 

ρ-aligned disc ∆ ⊆ Σ such that the restriction of ρ to ∆ contains a flat w-wall. Moreover, it holds that apex 3.8(t, r ), depth 3.8(t, r ) ∈ poly (t + r) and mesh 3.8(t, r, w ) ∈ poly (t +

r) + O(t2w).

# 4 Taming linkages in surfaces 

This subsection develops the structural framework needed to control how linkages interact with nested cycles in surface embeddings. The central objective is to enforce a controlled interaction between a family of paths and a system of concentric cycles drawn in a disc, ensuring that paths penetrate the cycles in a well-organised and hierarchical manner. This machinery has been inspired by the result of Frédéric Mazoit in [Maz13] who proved a single exponential bound for the linkage function on surface embeddable graphs. This result is based on an earlier result of Geelen, Huynh, and Richter in [GHR18] on topological linkages. The treatment presented here can be viewed as a streamlined reconstruction of the ideas of [Maz13], adapted to our setting and developed with an emphasis on clarity and structural transparency. 

4.1 Preparing the linkage 

This subsection is dedicated to establishing that we can force a set of paths interacting with a set of concentric cycles on a disc to intersect in an orderly manner. What we present here is a version of a fairly standard approach (see for example cheap linkages in [AKK +11] and valleys 

in [Maz13]) adapted to our setting. Let s be a positive integer. An s-well W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C, P) is a society (G, Ω) with a vortex-free, cylindrical rendition ρ in a disc ∆ such that G = ⋃ C ∪ ⋃ P for a set of pairwise internally disjoint V (Ω) -paths P and C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ is a sequence of concentric cycles in ∆. Thus for each P ∈ P , we may let T be the trace of P in ρ and there exists a unique disc ∆P in ∆ − T

such that the intersection of ∆P and the trace of C1 is empty. We call the closure of ∆P the 

interior of P . See Figure 2 for an example of a 4-well. We say that an s-well W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C, P) is drained if either |P| ≤ 1 or  

> •

for each P ∈ P we have that for all i ∈ [s − 1] , if P ∩ Ci is non-empty, then there exists 

Q ∈ P \ { P } such that the trace of Q ∩ Ci+1 is non-empty and found in ∆P , and  

> •

for all i ∈ [s], no Ci-path P ′ in P that is disjoint from V (C \ { Ci}) is contained in a cell 

c ∈ C(ρ) such that σ(c) contains an edge of Ci.In drained wells the vortex-free rendition tells us that any path that moves deeply into a drained well implies the existence of many other paths that also go deep. This is captured in the following observation which we will apply implicitly whenever we work with drained wells. 

Observation 4.1. Let W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C, P) be a drained s-well and let P ∈ P be a path such that Ci ∩ P is non-empty for some i ∈ [s]. Then there exist (s − i) + 1 distinct paths 27 P ∆

> C3
> C4

Figure 2: A 4-well with a single path drawn in violet. The disc ∆1 ⊆ ∆ bounded by C1 forms the “bottom” of the well and is illustrated in gray. As drawn, it is possible for the outermost cycle to intersect the boundary of the disc onto which the well is embedded in nodes. 

Ps, P s−1, . . . , P i+1 , P i = P such that Pj ∩ Cj is non-empty for all j ∈ [i, s ] and we have ∆Ps ⊊

. . . ⊊ ∆Pi+1 .We start by proving that we can drain any given well by rearranging the paths within it without changing the underlying set of cycles that make up the well. 

Lemma 4.2. Let s be a positive integer and let W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C, P) be an s-well. Then there exists an s-well W′ = ( G′, Ω, ρ ′, C, P′) that is drained and such that G′ ⊆ G, ρ ′ is the rendition of 

G′ induced by ρ, |P| = |P ′|, and for each P ∈ P there exists a P ′ ∈ P ′ with the same endpoints. 

Proof. Suppose the statement is false and that W is a minimal counterexample with respect to 

|E(C ∪ P )|. Let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩, where ∆i is the Ci-disc in ρ for each i ∈ [s], and let P ∈ P 

intersect some cycle Ci with i ∈ [s − 1] .We first establish the second property in the definition of being drained. Observe that if there exists a cell c ∈ C(ρ) such that σ(c) contains both an edge of Ci and a Ci-path P ′ ⊆ P with endpoints u, v, then we may simply replace P ′ in P with the unique u-v-path in Ci ∩ σ(c). This would contradict the minimality of W.Towards the first property, suppose that Ci+1 is not intersected by any path whose trace lies in 

∆P . Since ρ is vortex-free, we know that Ci+1 ∩ P is non-empty. Then let a, b be the first and last vertex we see of P within Ci+1 ∩ P when traversing P from one endpoint to the other. If there exists a grounded a-V (P )-path L in Ci+1 − E(P ) whose trace is found in ∆P (see the left side of Figure 3), then according to our assumptions on P and Ci+1 , no other path in P

whose trace lies in ∆P intersects L. However, this would allow us to shortcut P along Ci+1 ,

contradicting the minimality of W.Let P ∗ ⊆ P be the first Ci+1 -Ci-path or Ci+1 -path with a trace in ∆i+1 that we see when traversing P starting in a and heading towards b. Note that such a path must exist, since 

P ∩ Ci is non-empty and it must be grounded, according to our prior observation. Let a′ be the endpoints of P ∗ closest to a on P .28 P P

As there does not exist a grounded a-V (P )-path in Ci+1 − E(P ) whose trace is found in ∆P and due to our choice of P ∗ (see the right side of Figure 3), there now exists a grounded a′-V (P )-path 

L′ in Ci+1 − E(P ) whose trace is found in ∆P and we arrive at a contradiction exactly as we did earlier with L.For some applications the above lemma will be sufficient, but we will also need wells that act much more orderly but provide stronger assumptions on the structure of the cycles. Let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ be a sequence of concentric cycles in a ∆-rendition ρ of a graph G in a disc ∆ and let ∆i be the Ci-disc in ρ for each i ∈ [s], with ∆0 = ∅. We say that C is tight (in 

ρ) if for all i ∈ [s] there does not exist a grounded Ci-path whose trace is found in ∆i \ ∆i−1.Given a vortex c with c ⊆ ∆1, we say that C is tight around c (in ρ) if there does not exist a grounded C1-path whose trace is found in ∆1. In general, we call a set of concentric cycles C′

tight in a rendition ρ′ (around a vortex) if we can find a disc such that the restriction of ρ′ to 

∆′ allows us to call C′ tight. A well W = ( G′, Ω, ρ ′, C′, P) tight if C′ is tight in ρ′.Finding tight concentric cycles is an easy exercise and many articles in the literature provide algorithmic proofs that turn a concentric set of cycles into a tight, concentric set of cycles (see the discussion of tight cycles in [AKK +11] and cozy nests in [GSW25] for example). Thus we state the following result without proof. 

Proposition 4.3. Let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ be a set of concentric cycles in a ∆-rendition ρ of a graph G in a disc ∆ with ∆s being the Cs-disc in ρ. Then there exists a tight concentric set of cycles C′ = ⟨C′

> 1

, . . . , C ′

> s

⟩ such that the trace of each cycle in C′ lies in ∆s.Under the assumption that we are working with a tight well, we can actually guarantee a much stronger property than simply being drained. We say that a well W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C =

⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩, P) is dry if W is drained and for all P ∈ P we have that i) the graph C1 ∩ P is either empty or a single path, ii) there exists a unique i ∈ [s] such that Ci ∩ P is a single path, iii) for all j ∈ [i − 1] the graph Cj ∩ P is empty, and iv) for all j ∈ [i + 1 , s ] the graph Cj ∩ P consists of exactly two paths. It turns out that guaranteeing the existence of a dry well once given a tight well is quite easy, since we already proved Lemma 4.2. 29 Lemma 4.4. Let s be a positive integer and let W = ( G, Ω, ρ, C, P) be a tight s-well. Then there exists a tight, dry s-well W′ = ( G′, Ω, ρ ′, C, P′) such that G′ ⊆ G, ρ ′ is the rendition of G′

induced by ρ, |P| = |P ′|, and for each P ∈ P there exists a P ′ ∈ P ′ with the same endpoints. 

Proof. Suppose the statement is false and that W is a minimal counterexample with respect to |E(C ∪ P )|. Thanks to Lemma 4.2, we may assume that W is already drained and we let 

C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩, where ∆i is the Ci-disc in ρ for each i ∈ [s].Thanks to the second property of being drained, all paths we discuss from this point forward can be assumed to have a trace. Furthermore, we observe that, due to the tightness of C, no path in P may have a C1-path whose trace intersects the interior of ∆1.Suppose now that there exists a path P ∈ P and an i ∈ [s] such that P contains a Ci-path 

P ′ with the endpoints a, b whose trace is found outside of ∆i \ bd (∆ i). Then we choose P to maximise i and let L be the a-b-path in Ci such that the P ′ ∪ L-disc is disjoint from ∆i \ bd (∆ i).Due to the maximality of i and the fact that ρ is vortex-free, we conclude that L is internally disjoint from V (P). Thus by replacing P ′ with L in P we produce a contradiction to the minimality of W.As a consequence of the observations we have just made, for any path P ∈ P such that C1 ∩ P

is non-empty, the graph C1 ∩ P must be a path. This guarantees the first property of being dry. Suppose now that there instead exists a path P ∈ P such that for no i ∈ [s] the graph Ci ∩ P

consists of a single component. We choose P such that the value i ∈ [s] for which Ci ∩ P is non-empty is maximum and for all Q ∈ P such that the interior ∆Q of Q contains P there does actually exist a j ∈ [s] such that Cj ∩ Q is a path. Clearly, the fact that Ci ∩ P is comprised of at least two components implies the existence of a Ci-path P ′ in P . As we have observed earlier, 

P ′ must have its trace in ∆i. But since i is maximum and P is chosen to be the “outermost” path violating the second property at i, P ′ must be disjoint from all other cycles in C and thus the tightness of C prohibits its existence. This ensures that the second property of dryness holds. The third property is then easily derived from the fact that C is concentric and ρ is vortex-free. So we may finally suppose that the fourth property is violated and that there exists a P ∈ P 

such that Ci ∩ P consists of more than two components for some i ∈ [s]. By again choosing P

and i to be maximal as in the previous paragraph, we arrive at another contradiction, completing our proof. We note at this point that while the ideas behind our considerations in this subsection play an important role in the upcoming discussion of the bounded genus case of the Vital Linkage Theorem, we need somewhat stronger minimality assumptions on the type of “wells” that will appear there. Nonetheless our results in this subsection and the remainder of this section are cut from similar cloth and we will require wells – both drained and dry – at several points throughout the paper, as they form a basic type of configuration we encounter. 

4.2 Linkages in graphs on a surface 

In 2013 Mazoit proved a single exponential upper bound for the irrelevant vertex theorem for graphs embedded into a fixed surface. More precisely, he proved the following [Maz13, Theorem 1]. Let G be a graph embedded into a surface Σ of genus g ≥ 0. Suppose that G contains a sequence ⟨C1, . . . , C t⟩ of pairwise disjoint cycles such that each Ci bounds a disc ∆( Ci) ⊆ Σ

30 with ∆( Ci) ⊆ int (∆( Ci+1 )), for all 1 ≤ i < t . Let L ⊆ G be a linkage of order k in G

such that no path L ∈ L has an endpoint in ∆( Ct). In this setting Mazoit proved that if 

t ≥ f (g, k ) := 20 9 · k · c3( g−1)+2 k, where c = 3 e 10 3e is a small constant, then there is a linkage 

L′ ⊆ G with τ (L) = τ (L′) and L′ is disjoint from G ∩ int (∆( C1)).To prove the theorem, Mazoit first performs some preprocessing steps to reduce the input instance to a simpler form that he calls a reduced instance which corresponds to what we call a wedding cake below. See Definition 4.8 for details. The technical core of his argument is to show that in such a reduced instance the vertices deep in the middle of a separating set of concentric cycles is irrelevant. Mazoit made his proof available as a preprint on arXiv [Maz13], but to date it has not appeared in a peer-reviewed venue. His proof in [Maz13] is at some places very condensed and not always completely polished and therefore occasionally a bit hard to follow. Therefore, in this section, we reprove the part of his result that we need in full detail and also adapt it to the notation in the rest of this paper. Before we state the theorem we prove we first need to introduce some notation. 

Wedding cakes We first define the kind of surfaces we work with. 

Definition 4.5. For a number g ≥ 0, a disc with g strips is a surface Σ obtained from a closed disc ∆ as follows. Let d11, d 21, . . . , d 1

> g

, d 2 

> g

be pairwise disjoint closed and connected subsets of 

bd (∆) . Let S1, . . . , S g be closed discs and, for all 1 ≤ i ≤ g, let s1 

> i

and s2 

> i

be disjoint connected closed subsets of bd (Si). Then Σ is obtained from ∆ by gluing, for all 1 ≤ i ≤ g, s 1 

> i

onto d1 

> i

and 

s2 

> i

onto d2 

> i

in any of the two possible orientations. We say that Σ is generated by (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g) and denote this by Σ ˆ =(∆ , S 1, . . . , S g).We refer to the discs S1, . . . , S g as the strips of Σ and to ∆ as its base . The curves s1 

> i

and s2 

> i

are the sides of Si.The segments of Σ are the components of bd (∆) ∩ bd (Σ) . A pre-segment of Σ is either a segment of Σ or a side of a strip. In this section we will only work with graphs G embedded into a surface Σ generated by 

(∆ , S 1, . . . , S g). It is therefore convenient for us to assume that G ⊆ Σ, i.e. the vertices of G

are points on Σ and the edges are simple curves connecting their endpoints. Our goal is to show that any vertex in G that is separated from a set of terminals by a sufficient number of concentric cycles will be irrelevant for the existence of a linkage connecting the terminals. In the next definition we formally define what we mean by a set of concentric cycles. 

Definition 4.6. Let C := ⟨C1, . . . , C t⟩ be a sequence of pairwise disjoint cycles in G. We say that C is a canonical sequence of concentric cycles if each Ci, for 1 ≤ i < t, bounds a closed disc 

∆( Ci) ⊆ ∆ such that ∆( Ci) ⊆ int (∆( Ci+1 )). The number t is the order of C.As mentioned in the introduction of this section, we will only prove the irrelevant vertex theorem for instances of a very special form. In the next definition we introduce notation for linkages that we need to formally define the special form of instances we work with later on. Recall that the segments of Σ ˆ =( δ, S 1, . . . , S g) are the components of bd (∆) ∩ bd (Σ) .31 S1 S2Figure 4: A wedding cake of genus 2, order 4, and depth 4. The 4 segments are marked by the grey areas on the outer cycle. 

Definition 4.7. Let Σ be a disc with g strips generated by (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g). Let G ⊆ Σ be a graph and let C be a canonical sequence of concentric cycles of order t embedded into ∆ such that bd (∆) = Ct.Let L ⊆ G be a linkage whose endpoints are all contained in the segments of Σ, i.e. in bd (Σ) ∩

bd (∆) .A component L of L ∩ ∆ is a valley if there is d ≤ t such that the vertices of L, as they appear, are ⟨v0, . . . , v 2d⟩ with vi, v 2d−i ∈ V (Ct−i), for all 0 ≤ i ≤ d. We call d the depth of the valley L.The linkage L is clean (w.r.t. Σ and C) if each component L of L ∩ ∆ is a valley and, if g ≥ 1,

then the two endpoints of L are contained in different pre-segments of Σ.A property of valleys that is implicit in their definition is that every edge of a valley has its endpoints on different consecutive cycles of C. We will need this property below. We are now ready to define the special form of instances for the irrelevant vertex problem we will work with. See Figure 4 for an illustration. 

Definition 4.8. Let Σ be a disc with g strips generated by (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g). Let G ⊆ Σ be a graph, C = ⟨Ct, . . . , C 1⟩ be a canonical set of concentric cycles of order t, and let L ⊆ G be a linkage of order k. The tuple (Σ , G, C, L) is a wedding cake of depth t, genus g, and order k if it meets the following conditions. (i) L is clean (w.r.t. Σ and C), the endpoints of the paths in L are contained in the segments of Σ, and otherwise L is disjoint from bd (Σ) .(ii) ∆( Ct) = ∆ .(iii) V (G) = V (L) = V (C), E (C) ∩ E(L) = ∅, and E(G) = E(C) ∪ E(L).Given a wedding cake (Σ , G, C, L), we say that a vertex v ∈ V (G) is irrelevant if G−v contains a linkage L′ with τ (L′) = τ (L).Finally, we fix an orientation of bd (∆) which also orients all cycles in C in the same way. This allows us to say that a vertex u in a pre-segment s of Σ comes before or after a vertex v on s.Before stating the main theorem of this section we collect a few useful properties of wedding cakes we use below. 

Lemma 4.9. Let W := (Σ ˆ= (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g), G, C, L) be a wedding cake of depth t, genus g, and order k.32 Let p ⊆ bd (∆) be a pre-segment and let P = {P0, . . . , P ℓ} be the paths in L ∩ ∆ with an endpoint in p ordered so that the endpoints p0, . . . , p ℓ of P0, . . . , P ℓ appear in this order on bd (∆) w.r.t. to the orientation of bd (∆) .Then for each 0 ≤ j ≤ ⌈ ℓ 

> 2

⌉ the paths Pj and Pℓ−j both contain a subpath linking their endpoint on p and a vertex on Ct−j .

Proof. Suppose the claim was false and let j be minimal such that Pj or Pℓ−j does not contain a subpath linking their endpoint on p with a vertex on Ct−j . W.l.o.g. we assume that Pj does not contain such a subpath. It is easily seen that j > 0. By our choice of j, the paths Pj−1 and Pℓ+1 −j both contain subpaths 

Ij−1 ⊆ Pj−1 and Iℓ+1 −j ⊆ Pℓ+2 −j linking their endpoints pj−1 and pℓ+1 −j on p, resp., to some vertex qj−1 and qℓ+1 −j , resp., on Ct−(j−1) . But if Pj is disjoint from Ct−j , then p ∪ Ij−1 ∪ Iℓ+1 −j

together with the subpath C′ ⊆ Ct−j between qj−1 and qℓ+1 −j bounds a closed disc D that contains Pj with bd (D) ∩ bd (Σ) = p. But this is a contradiction to the requirement of valleys that the other endpoint of Pj is not on p.Given a segment p in a wedding cake we will refer to the set of paths I1, . . . , I ℓ constructed in the previous lemma as a pyramid over p below. We are now ready to state the main technical theorem in Mazoit’s paper. 

Theorem 4.10 (see [Maz13, Theorem 2]) . Let f4.10 (g, k ) := 20 9 k · (3 e 10 3e )g and let 

(Σ ˆ= (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g), G, C, L) be a wedding cake of depth t ≥ f4.10 (g, k ), genus g, and order 

k. Then there is a linkage L′ with τ (L′) = τ (L) which is disjoint from int (∆( C1)). That is, every vertex of G inside int (∆( C1)) is irrelevant for the existence of a linkage in G realising τ (L).In the remainder of this section we prove the previous theorem. So far we have worked with linkages L as sets of paths in G. For some of our arguments we will work with topological linkages which are simply sets of disjoint curves on Σ. We introduce the required notation next. 

Definition 4.11. Let G be a graph and let A ⊆ V (G). A pattern on A is a set τ ⊆ {{a, b } :

a, b ∈ A} of pairs of vertices of A (not necessarily disjoint). If A = V (G) we simply say that τ is a pattern. Let Σ be a surface. A topological linkage T in Σ with pattern τ is a set of pairwise disjoint simple curves such that T contains for each pair {a, b } ∈ τ a curve with endpoints a and b. We denote the pattern of T by τ (T ).A pattern τ is realisable (in G) if there is a linkage L in G with τ (L) = τ and it is topologically feasible (in Σ) if there is a topological linkage T in Σ with τ (T ) = τ .Obviously if τ is feasible then it must also be topologically feasible. The converse is not true in general. We call a topological linkage T realisable in G if there is a linkage L ⊆ G such that 

τ (L) = τ (T ).Throughout this part we will use L (possibly with subscripts) to denote linkages in G and T

(again possibly with subscripts) to denote topological linkages in Σ.We first need the following easy lemma which proves a special case to which we reduce in the sequel. 33 Lemma 4.12. Let G be a graph embedded on a disc ∆ and let C := ⟨C1, . . . , C t⟩ be a canonical sequence of concentric cycles such that Ct = bd (∆) . Furthermore, let P be a V (Ct)−V (C1)-linkage of order 2k in G such that |V (P ∩ Cj )| = 1 for each P ∈ P and 1 ≤ j ≤ t. Let τ be a pattern of order k on V (Ct) ∩ V (P).If t ≥ k and τ is topologically feasible then it is realisable in G. Furthermore, τ can be realised in G \ int (∆( C1)) ⊆ Σ.

Proof. We prove the lemma by induction on k. Let P := {P1, . . . , P 2k} and, for 1 ≤ j ≤ 2k, let 

pj be the endpoint of Pj on Ct. As τ is topologically feasible, there must be a pair {a, b } ∈ τ

such that Ct contains a subpath C(a, b ) with ends a and b that does not contain any other vertex occurring in an element of τ . Let A ⊆ τ be the set of such pairs. Thus, |A| ≥ 1.We construct a pattern τ ′ as follows. For {a, b } ∈ τ \ A let Pa, P b be the paths in P with endpoints a, b, resp., and let a⋆ be the vertex in Pa ∩ Ct−1 and let b⋆ be the vertex in Pb ∩ Ct−1.Let P ⋆a and P ⋆b be the subpaths of Pa and Pb between a and a⋆ and b and b⋆, resp. Then 

τ ′ := {{ a⋆, b ⋆} : {a, b } ∈ τ \ A}.By construction, τ ′ is a pattern of order k′ for some k′ < k and all vertices occurring in 

τ ′ are contained in V (Ct−1). Let C′ := ⟨C1, . . . , C t−1⟩ and let P′ := P ∩ ∆( Ct−1). Thus, 

G′ := G ∩ ∆( Ct−1), P′, C′, and τ ′ satisfy the assumptions of the induction hypothesis and hence there is a linkage L′ ⊆ G′ \ int (∆( C1)) ⊆ Σ with τ (L′) = τ ′. For each L′ ∈ L ′ with endpoints 

a⋆ and b⋆ let L = P ⋆a ∪ L ∪ P ⋆b be the extension of the path L′ ∈ L to the cycle Ct and let 

L := {L : L′ ∈ L ′} ∪ { C(a, b ) : {a, b } ∈ A}. Then L ⊆ G \ int (∆( C1)) ⊆ Σ and τ (L) = τ as required. We also need the following lemma in later sections which can be proved in a similar way. 

Lemma 4.13. Let G be a graph embedded into a disc ∆ and let C := ⟨C1, . . . , C t⟩ with t ≥ 2k

be a canonical sequence of concentric cycles such that Ct = bd (∆) and int (∆( C1)) = ∅. Let P

be a V (Ct)−V (C1)-linkage of order 2k in G such that |V (Ci) ∩ V (P )| = 1 , for all 1 ≤ i ≤ t and all P ∈ P . Finally, let τ be a pattern of order k on (V (Ct) ∪ V (C1)) ∩ V (P).If τ is topologically feasible then it is realisable in G.

Proof. A pair {a, b } ∈ τ is local if {a, b } ⊆ V (C1) or {a, b } ⊆ V (Ct). We prove the lemma by induction on the number of local pairs in τ .We first consider the case where τ does not have a local pair. Fix an orientation of ∆ which implies a consistent orientation of Ct and C1. Let τ := {{ai, b i} : 1 ≤ i ≤ k} such that all 

ai ∈ V (Ct) and all bi ∈ V (C1), numbered such that a1, . . . , a k occur in this order on Ct. As G is planar and τ is topologically feasible, b1, . . . , b k must also appear in this order in C1.Let P (a1) ∈ P be the path containing a1 and let c1 be the other endpoint of P (a1) on C1. Let 

S1 be the subpath of C1 starting at c1 and ending at b1 in the direction of C1 and let S2 be the other subpath of C1 starting at b1 and ending at c1. Let P (b1) ∈ P be the path with endpoint 

b1.At least one of S1, S 2 contains ≥ k1 endpoints of paths in P \ { P (a1), P (b1)}. W.l.o.g. we assume that this is S2.Let a′ be the unique vertex in V (P (a1)) ∩ C⌊ t  

> 2⌋

and let b′ be the unique vertex of V (P (b1)) ∩ C⌊ t 

> 2⌋

.Let S be the subpath of C⌊ t  

> 2⌋

between a′ and b′ in the same direction as S1. Let L be the union 34 of S, the subpath of P (a1) between a1 and a′, and the subpath of P (b1) between b′ and b1. By construction, L links a1 and b1.Let L′ be a linkage of order k − 1 between A := {a2, . . . , a k} and B := {b2, . . . , b k} in G − L.Such a linkage exists, for otherwise, by Menger’s theorem, there would be a separation (A′, B ′)

of G − L of order < k − 1 with A ⊆ A′ and B ⊆ B′. But this is impossible because there are 

k − 1 disjoint paths in P \ { P (a1), P (b1)} with an endpoint in B which intersect each of the cycles Cj with 1 ≤ j ≤ ⌊ t 

> 2

⌋ − 1, there are k − 1 paths in P \ { P (a1), P (b1)} with an endpoint in 

A which intersect each of the cycles Cj with t ≥ j ≥ ⌊ t 

> 2

⌋ + 1 and, by our choice of S1, there are 

k − 1 paths in P \ { P (a1), P (b1)} linking S2 ⊆ C1 to Ct. As |V (A′) ∩ V (B′)| ≤ k − 2, the union of these paths and cycles contains an A−B-path disjoint from V (A′) ∩ V (B′), a contradiction. Thus the A−B-linkage L′ exists. As G − L is a planar graph with A and B contained in disjoint subpaths of the outer cycle, L′ must link ai to bi, for all 2 ≤ i ≤ k. Thus L := {L} ∪ L ′ is a linkage realising τ . This concludes the induction base. Now suppose that τ does contain a local pair {a, b }. We can now argue exactly as in the proof of Lemma 4.12. In particular, as τ is topologically feasible, τ must contain a local pair {a, b }

such that one of the two subpaths of the cycle C ∈ { Ct, C 1} containing a, b between a and b

contains no other terminal from τ . Let P ⊆ τ be the set of all such pairs. Then we can link these pairs directly by these subpaths of the cycle containing them without intersecting any other terminal pair. Let τ ′ := τ \ P . For each terminal a ∈ V (Ct) occurring in τ ′ let P (a) ∈ P 

be the path in P containing a and let a′ be the unique vertex in V (P (a) ∩ Ct−1). Analogously, for each b ∈ V (C1) occurring in τ ′ let P (b) ∈ P be the path containing b and let b′ be the unique vertex of P (b) ∩ V (C2). Let P′ be the set of subpaths of paths in P between Ct−1 and C2. Let 

G′ := P′ ∪ ⋃{C2, . . . , C t−1}. By induction hypothesis, there is a linkage L′ in G′ realising τ ′.This can easily be extended to a linkage L in G realising τ .

4.3 Proof of Theorem 4.10 

We are now ready to start proving Theorem 4.10. The main idea is as follows. Let 

(Σ ˆ= (∆ , S 1, . . . , S g), G, C, L) be a wedding cake. We now consider the subpaths of L within a single strip Si, i.e. the linkage L ∩ Si. If L crosses Si only a bounded number of times, we can remove Si from Σ and treat the vertices in L ∩ bd (Si) as new terminals. If this can be done for every strip then we arrive at a planar instance embedded into ∆ with all terminals on bd (∆) .This is the simplest case that we prove in Lemma 4.15. Otherwise we can take a strip Si which L crosses sufficiently often. We then apply the following theorem by Geelen, Huynh, and Richter [GHR18], which is the topological engine of Mazoit’s argument. 

Theorem 4.14. [GHR18, Theorem 2.6] Let Σ be a surface and let P be a non-separating bd (Σ) -path in Σ. For any linkage L in Σ whose ends are disjoint from P, there is a bd -homeomorphism 

ϕ : Σ → Σ such that each path of ϕ(L) intersects P at most twice. This will allow us to show that the pattern τ (L) can be realised by a topological linkage Ti

which crosses Si only a bounded number of times. Repeating this for each strip we obtain a topological linkage T which realises the pattern τ (L) but crosses each strip only a bounded number of times. The difficult step in Mazoit’s proof is to show that T can be realised by a 35 linkage in G which also crosses each strip only a bounded number of times. Once this is done we are in the case that we already discussed above. We now give the details of this proof strategy. We start with the base case which essentially settles the case where L crosses each strip only a bounded number of times. 

Lemma 4.15. Let (Σ := ∆ , G, C, L) be a wedding cake of depth t ≥ 2k, genus 0, and order k.Then there is a linkage L′ with τ (L′) = τ (L) which is disjoint from int (∆( C1)). That is, every vertex of G in int (∆( C1)) is irrelevant for the existence of a linkage in G to realise τ (L).

Proof. The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma 4.12. As Σ has no strips, the instance is planar with all terminals on the outer cycle Ct. Thus, there must be a pair {a, b } ∈ τ (L) such that Ct contains a subpath P (a, b ) with ends a and b which is internally disjoint from L (in fact, by the definition of wedding cakes, a = b, but this does not matter here). As in the proof of Lemma 4.12 we add P (a, b ) to L′ for all such pairs and apply induction on the remaining pairs and the sequence ⟨C1, . . . , C t−1⟩ of cycles. We now prove the main lemma that establishes the inductive step. For 0 ≤ i ≤ g we define 

Σi := Σ \ ((S1 ∪ . . . S i) \ bd (∆) ). That is, Σ0 = Σ and, for 1 ≤ i ≤ g, Σi is obtained from the disc ∆ by attaching only the strips Si+1 , . . . , S g. For 0 ≤ i ≤ g, aGiven a linkage L, we define the size of a strip Si, denoted by |Si|, as the number of components in Si ∩ L .Recall that, by definition of a wedding cake, L is clean.

# 5 Linking vortices up to some gap 

This section is dedicated to the proof of a version of [DKMW12, Lemma 15] suitable for our needs: We want to change our rendition slightly in order to guarantee that the graphs of vortices are d-linked up to some bounded gap . Intuitively speaking, this means that there is a linear decomposition of some segment of the vortex society, that “covers” all but a few society vertices, and such that the adhesion of that linear decomposition is d and there is a linkage of order d

that “links” the first to the last bag of the decomposition. We will need this to apply a variant of Proposition 3.7 in a later section. The main idea to achieve linkedness being that, if we find d + 1 concentric cycles around a vortex of depth d, we may try to “push” the cycles into the vortex as deeply as possible in order to “calibrate” regions of the vortex that are of depth lower than d by bloating these up artificially. Unfortunately, we cannot easily guarantee all the assumptions needed for the referenced theorem; more precisely, we may not be able to find 41 Figure 8: The setting for linking vortices. Red cells denote vortices, each of which come with two layers of concentric cycles: purple areas are schematic representations of (d + 1) 

concentric cycles and yellow areas are schematic representations of the remaining concentric cycles. The cycle whose outer-layer is yellow is the vortex one currently tries to link, where the inner purple layer is used to push cycles deeply into the vortex. “private” nests of “large enough” size for every vortex. Worse even, it may happen that the nest of one vortex is contained in the disc bounded by a cycle of the nest of another vortex – this will be one outcome of what we will call stacked – without yielding immediate contradictions to any “distance” assumptions between vortices; see Figure 8 for a schematic representation of the setting. It turns out that this is, however, not really a problem when trying to link vortices; it may simply happen that one vortex is “absorbed” by another without increasing the depth of the former by too much. 

5.1 Linked linear decompositions 

We will need renditions where vortices are linked up to some bounded gap ; we transcribe the following definition of [DKMW12] to our setting. 

Definition 5.1. Let d ∈ N and m ≥ 1. Let (H, Ω) be a society. Let α =(⟨u1, . . . , u m⟩, ⟨U1, . . . , U m⟩) be a linear decomposition of (H, Ω) .Let 1 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ m and let S = [ p, q ]. Let αS be the S-restriction of α. Let Zi := ( Ui ∩ Ui+1 ) \

V (Ω c) for i ∈ [p, q − 1] . We call αS d-linked if i) for every i ∈ [p, q ], u i ∈ Ui and Ui ∩ V (Ω) ⊆ { ui−1, u i}, where u0 := u1

ii) for every i ∈ [p, q − 1] , |Zi| = d, and iii) there is a Up-Uq-linkage M in G[⋃qi=p Ui] \ V (Ω) of order d.Note that by definition for every i ∈ [p, q − 1] , it holds Ui ∩ Ui+1 ⊆ Zi ∪ { ui}. We say that M

witnesses that (H, Ω) is d-linked .If additionally the following holds iv) there is a path P ∗ ⊆ G disjoint from M such that Nρ(P ∗) = V (Ω) ,

then we call α d -stuffed . We say that M ∪ { P ∗} witnesses that (H, Ω) is d-stuffed . Finally, we say that α is properly d-linked if M ∪ { P ∗} ⊆ G[⋃qi=p Ui].42 If (H, Ω) = ( σ(c), Ω) is a vortex society for some vortex c ∈ C(ρ) given some Σ-rendition of some graph G in a surface Σ, then we say that M witnesses that c is d-linked and M ∪ { P ∗}

witnesses that c is d-stuffed , respectively, for simplicity. While properly d-linkedness can in general not be easily guaranteed – we work with renditions and paths cannot be easily “tucked” into vortices – we will need it for technical reasons in Section 7 where we essentially reduce to an embedded instance. For completeness, we give the definition here. We extend the definition of (properly) linked and stuffed linear decompositions, to linear decompositions with gaps. 

Definition 5.2. Let d ∈ N, and ℓ, m ≥ 1. Let (H, Ω) be a society. Let u−(ℓ−1) , . . . , u 0, u 1, . . . , u m

be the vertices of V (Ω) appearing in Ω in the order listed together with a sequence of sets

ii) ⋃

iii) for every x ∈ V (H), the set {i ∈ [m] : x ∈ Xi} forms an interval in [m] and the set 

{i ∈ [−(ℓ − 1) , 0])] : x ∈ Xi} forms an interval in [−(ℓ − 1) , 0] ,

iv) let X− := ⋃0

> (

⟨u−(ℓ−1) , . . . , u 0, u 1, . . . , u m⟩, ⟨U−(ℓ−1) , . . . , U 0, U 1, . . . , U m⟩

> )

, then we call α a linear decomposition of (H, Ω) with gap ℓ.Let −(ℓ − 1) ≤ p ≤ q ≤ m and let S = [ p, q ]. We call αS = (⟨up, . . . , u q⟩, ⟨Up, . . . , U q⟩) the

α is (properly) d-linked, respectively d-stuffed. Note that linear decompositions with gaps are special cases of so called cyclic decompositions; see for example [DJKK25]. Given some vortex c in some Σ-rendition ρ, we may say that a linear decomposition of (σ(c), Ωc)

is a d-linked linear decomposition with gap ℓ of c for simplicity, not stating the exact choice of Ωc (there are two up to cyclic rotation). We say that (σ(c), Ωc) or c is (properly) d-linked, respectively d-stuffed, with gap ℓ if it admits a (properly) d-linked, respectively d-stuffed, linear decomposition with gap ℓ, and so on. Furthermore, we extend the definition of concentric cycles to more general renditions as follows. 

Definition 5.3. Let Σ be a surface, s ≥ 1 and ρ a Σ-rendition of some graph G. Let ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩

be a sequence of disjoint cycles in G, then we call them concentric in ρ if there exists a ρ-aligned disc ∆ ⊆ Σ such that ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ are concentric in the restriction ρ∗ of ρ by ∆. Further, for every i ∈ [s] we let ∆Ci denote the respective Ci-disc in ρ∗ (and hence in ρ). Note that whenever s ≥ 2 the Ci-disc is unambiguously defined via the order ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ even if Σ is the sphere. 43 Finally, we slightly extend the definition of crop of a graph by a disc as follows. 

Definition 5.4. Let Σ be a surface and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of some graph G. Let C ⊆ G be a grounded cycle and let ∆C be a ρ-aligned disc bounded by the trace of C. We define G[ρ, ∆C ]

as the extended crop of G by ∆C obtained from the crop G∆C of G by ∆C , by adding to it the cycle C, i.e., G[ρ, ∆C ] := G∆C ∪ C.If ∆C is uniquely defined by C or is clear from context, we write G[ρ, C ] for simplicity, and refer to it as the extended crop of G by C.Note that if Σ is not the sphere and the trace of C bound a disc in Σ, then the extended crop of 

G by C is unambiguously defined. 

5.2 Kissing vortices 

As a first lemma will need an adaptation of [DKMW12, Lemma 15]; the main difference being that we do not want to introduce new (major) apices when changing the rendition. Following their proof yields a possible major vertex u say 7. This can easily be mitigated by introducing a “small gap” where we do not impose as strict restrictions on the behaviour inside the graph of the vortex by simply redrawing u on the resulting vortex boundary and adding a bag to the linear decomposition that takes care of all the formerly introduced apices. While the modification is easy – put all the deleted vertices into a single bag – we provide a proof for completeness. Note that the main part of the proof simply follows the proof of [DKMW12, Lemma 15], thus we omit some details by transcribing relevant claims from their setting to ours. Furthermore, note that they work with what they call “properly attached cells”; this is mitigated in our setting by working with grounded paths and nests. We define the following, giving a name to a set of paths “pushed tightly into a vortex”. 

Definition 5.5. Let Σ be a surface and ρ a Σ-rendition of depth d of some graph G. Let c ∈ C(ρ)

be a vortex. Let s ≥ 1 and let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ be concentric cycles in ρ such that c ⊆ ∆C1 .Let G[ρ, ∆Cs ] be the extended crop of G by ∆Cs .Let B = {B1, . . . , B s} and P = {P1, . . . , P s} be linkages in G[ρ, ∆Cs ] such that for distinct 

i, j ∈ [s] the paths Pi, B j are internally disjoint and {Bi ∪ Pi | i ∈ [s]} is a set of s disjoint cycles where Bi ⊊ Ci for every i ∈ [s]. We call B a base of P in C.Let C∗ ⊆ G[ρ, ∆Cs ] be a grounded cycle in ρ and let ∆∗ be a disc bounded by the trace of C∗

containing c. We say that C∗ ρ-envelopes P at B if P ∪ B is contained in the extended crop of 

G by ∆C∗ and additionally C∗ = Ps ∪ Bs; we call (Ps, B s) the perimeter of (P, B) and ∆∗ the 

C∗-disc (in ρ containing c). We say that (P, B) ρ-kisses c in C if for every linkage P′ of order s such that B is a base of P′ in 

C and such that C′ ρ-envelopes P at B, it holds that ∆C∗ is contained in the respective C′-disc. Note that in general, C-discs in ρ are only defined if Σ is not the sphere: This is because if it is a sphere there are two natural choices. We may however talk about the C-disk containing 

X ⊆ Σ which then unambiguously defines it, i.e., if the trace of C bounds a disc ∆ in Σ such that X ⊆ ∆, then we call ∆ the C-disc (in ρ) containing X.

> 7Note that all but one of the resulting apices in their proof have all their original neighbours in the graph of the newly constructed vortex, hence they are minor apices.

44 (a) (P, B) kissing a vortex. (b) The resulting linked vortex.

Non-linked vortices are red together with purple regions marking (d + 1) concentric cycles and yellow regions marking the remaining concentric cycles. Linked vortices are green together with their remaining set of cycles in orange. All vortices are stacked. On the left hand side a (d + 1) linkage P (purple) together with a base B (green) ρ-kissing a vortex are depicted. On the right hand side, the resulting linked vortex is depicted, where some vortices have been absorbed. We have the following version of [DKMW12, Lemma 15].

Claim 5.6.1 . Let P be an X-Y -linkage in G′. Then B is a base for P in G.

Proof of claim. This is immediate from the fact that B is internally disjoint from V (G′) and every path in B shares its endpoints with a unique path in P. ⊣

The following summarises Equation (4) and its implications as given in the proof of [Die10, Lemma 15] transcribed to our setting; again the paths being grounded in ρ take over the role of properly attached cells in their setting. 

Claim 5.6.2 . For every set P of d + 1 disjoint X–Y -paths in G′ whose traces are contained in ∆,

5.3 Linking stacked vortices 

In light of the discussion above, we make precise what we mean by “stacked vortices”; see Figure 8 for a schematic illustration. 

Definition 5.7. Let s1, s 2 ≥ 1. Let Σ be a surface, and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of a graph G.Let ∆10, ∆20 be two distinct vortices in ρ. Let C1 = ⟨C11 , . . . , C 1 

> s1

⟩ and C2 = ⟨C21 , . . . , C 2 

> s2

⟩ be two sequences of concentric cycles in ρ around ∆10 and ∆20, respectively. Let ∆ij for i ∈ [2] and j ∈ [si]

be the Cij -disc in ρ containing ∆i 

> 0

such that ∆i 

> 0

⊊ ∆i 

> 1

⊊ . . . ⊊ ∆isi , and Ci is tight around ∆i 

> 0

for both i ∈ [2] . Then we say that (C1, ∆10) and (C2, ∆20) are stacked with respect to each other if we either have that ∆1 

> s1

and ∆2 

> s2

are disjoint, or there exists an i ∈ [2] and a j ∈ [si] such that 

∆3−is3−i is found in ∆ij − (bd (∆ ij ) ∪ ∆ij−1), i.e., in the interior of the annulus bounded by traces of 

Cij and Cij−1.The following is the main theorem of this section. The main idea is that, given two vortices 

(C1, ∆10) and (C2, ∆20) as in Definition 5.7 with enough concentric cycles – meaning b(d + 1) many where b is the number of vortices – and after contracting the graph of the first vortex together with the (b − 1)( d + 1) inner cycles of C1 into a single vertex x and subsequently applying Lemma 5.6 resulting in a new linked vortex ˜∆20, we can guarantee that x will not be part of the adhesion of the “ d-linked part” of ˜∆20. This is possible since Lemma 5.6 results in a “tight” set 

P of d paths witnessing linkedness – this is made precise by the notion of ρ-kissing – which, due to the presence of d + 1 concentric cycles around the contracted vertex x are “deflected” from x

by the choice of “tightness” for P. See the left hand side of Figure 9 for an illustration of this, where c2 may be thought of as the focused vortex with the purple region close around it – these are the (d + 1) cycles needed to link it – and c1 is a vortex inside the purple region of c2, where 48 the yellow region is to be thought of as the (b − 1)( d − 1) cycles that are “protected” by the purple region of (d + 1) -layers needed to deflect the paths. After linking c2, there are more than 

(d + 1) cycles around it left – see the right hand side Figure 9 where these cycles are marked by the orange region – that can be used to deflect paths in a next iteration step. More precisely, the whole vortex ∆10 will either be contained in one of the bags of the resulting linear decomposition of ˜∆20, i.e., it was absorbed by the second vortex, or ∆10 together with the inner (b − 1)( d + 1) cycles of C1 will lie outside of ˜∆20. Note that once a vortex is absorbed, it will no longer be a cell, and thus no longer be a vortex in the resulting rendition; in particular no vortex is absorbed more than once. If the first vortex was not absorbed, we continue linking it in a second iteration. Note that this does not result in an exponential blow-up, as the number of cycles needed to link a vortex is only (d + 1) and similarly only (d + 1) concentric cycles are needed to guarantee that a vortex can be safely absorbed. Therefore, we have enough cycles left to continue linking ∆10 possibly absorbing other vortices, and after having linked ˜∆20, it has enough concentric cycles left around it so that it can be safely absorbed in a later step. For technical reasons we will work with (b + 1)( d + 1) many concentric cycles, in order to easily witness that the resulting rendition remains stretched , i.e., vortex boundaries are disjoint, and since we will need “disjoint” d-stuffed linear decompositions, this easily guarantees that the resulting linkages witnessing this are too. We continue with a formal formulation and proof of the above.

# 6 The apex-free case 

Throughout this section we work with renditions in surfaces, in particular we are apex-free . The main goal of this section is to prove that, given an instance (G, T ) with a vital T -linkage L and a blank Σ-rendition of (G, T ), then there exists another instance (G′, T ′) with a vital T ′-linkage L′

such that |T ′| ∈ poly (|T |) · 2poly (b) where b = bidim (G, T ) and such that (G, T ) can be embedded in a surface Σ′ with all terminals drawn on boundary components of Σ′, where the surfaces ˆΣ′

and ˆΣ obtained by “capping their holes with discs” are homeomorphic. We will formulate and prove the relevant results in a more general setting as it is more natural. We need specific renditions throughout this section; we call a rendition ρ stretched if for any pair of distinct vortices c, c ′ ∈ C(ρ) it holds N (c) ∩ N (c′) = ∅. Recall the Definition 5.3 of concentric cycles in ρ, and that, given a sequence ⟨C1, . . . , C s⟩ of concentric cycles in ρ, we let ∆Ci denote the respective Ci-disc in ρ satisfying ∆C1 ⊊ . . . ⊊ ∆Cs .

Definition 6.1. Let Σ be a surface. Let m ≥ 1. Let (G, T ) be an annotated graph. Let ρ

be a blank and stretched Σ-rendition of (G, T ) and let C∗ ⊆ C(ρ) be its set of vortices. Let 

M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩ be a sequence of concentric cycles in ρ such that ∆Cm is vortex-free 9. Then we call (ρ, M ) an m-locus (of (G, T ) in Σ). We refer to Ci as a cycle of M for every i ∈ [m].We call (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) exhausted if

Definition 6.2. Let Σ be a surface, G a graph and ρ a Σ-rendition of G. Let c ∈ C(ρ) be a cell with N (c) = {ac, b c} such that σ(c) consists of the single edge acbc. Then we call c dead 10 .We call ρ a Σ-skeleton of G if every non-vortex cell is dead. Given a Σ-rendition, we will classify different homotopy types of paths whose traces have both endpoints in vortex-boundaries: These will be loops, if both endpoints lie on the society of a common vortex, and links otherwise.

54 Definition 6.3. Let Σ be a surface and ρ a Σ-rendition of some graph G. Let c, c ′ ∈ C(ρ) be vortices. Let P ⊆ G be grounded such that both its endpoints are in N (c) ∪ N (c′). If c̸ = c′ we call P a c-c′ link or simply link . If c = c′, we call P a loop (at c).Given a loop P at c, let γ ⊊ Σ be the trace of P . If there exists an arc-wise connected component

Theorem 6.4. Let t, ξ, g, k ∈ N and d ≥ 1. Let Σ be a surface of genus g. Then there exist functions terminals : N4 → N, cycles : N3 → N fat : N2 → N satisfying the following. Let (G, T ) be a k-annotated graph such that

there exists a vital T -linkage in G.Then there exists a terminals (k, ξ, d, g )-annotated graph (G′, T ′), and a t-locus (ρ′, M ′) of (G′, T ′)

in Σ together with a vital T ′-linkage L′ in G′ such that i) ρ′ is a Σ-skeleton with at most ξ vortices, and (G′, T ′), L′, (ρ′, M ′) is exhausted, ii) all vortices c ∈ C(ρ′) satisfy σ(c) = ( N (c), ∅),

iii) let L ∈ L ′, then L is either a non-simple loop or a link; in particular it is internally disjoint from Nρ′ (c) for every vortex cell c ∈ C(ρ′),

iv) let P ∈ L ′ be a non-simple loop or a link in G′, and let L′(P ) ⊆ L ′ be the maximum size linkage of paths ρ-homotopic to P . Then |L ′(P )| ≤ fat (k, d ).Moreover, terminals (k, ξ, d, g ) ∈ poly (k + ξ + g) · 2poly (d), cycles (k, d, t ) ∈ poly (k + t) · 2poly (d),

6.1 Exhausting the instance 

In order to prove Theorem 6.4 we need several technical steps; the first one – marking the main result of this subsection – allows us to reduce an instance (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) where (ρ, M ) is some 

(m + 1) -locus of (G, T ) in a surface Σ, to that of an exhausted instance (G′, T ), L′, (ρ′, M ′) on the same set of annotations where (ρ′, M ′) is an m-locus of (G′, T ′) in Σ; recall Definition 6.1. In particular, the instance we are left with comes with a Σ-skeleton, and thus it is “quasi-embedded”, meaning that it is essentially embedded up-to the vortex cells. The main techniques employed to prove this result expand on existing techniques [RS09, CKK24, AKK +17]. Unfortunately, we cannot easily transcribe the proposed results from the literature as we need to take care of further technicalities, and thus we give a full proof dedicated to our setting and needs for completion. The main idea of the proof being that no two paths in L can use non-node vertices of the graph of a common non-vortex cell, for the graphs of non-vortex cells can be separated from the rest by separations of order ≤ 3. This can be exploited in order to “throw away” cells with ≤ 3 nodes via simply replacing them with single edges respecting the linkage L. Continuing 55 (a) P ∗        

> iand forbidden configura-tions.

In a next instance, one then “throws away” vertices and contracts edges until left with an exhausted instance maintaining a large locus; recall the definitions from Section 4.1. There is one more technicality that we need to add to the above sketch, which we briefly discussed in Section 5: When given a d-stuffed vortex with gap 1, one of the (d + 1) paths witnessing that the vortex is d-stuffed, may partially “live” outside of the vortex, due to the fact that we work with renditions. We need to carefully “tuck” that path into the graph of the vortex when exhausting the instance, as we want the adhesion of our linear decomposition to match the size of the linkage inside the graph of the vortex “linking” the separators; compare with Proposition 3.7. To this extent, recall Definitions 5.2 and 5.9 of properly d-linked renditions with gap at most ℓ.

Claim 6.5.1 . Let c ∈ C(σ) be a non-vortex cell. Then, either i) c is dying, or ii) there is a unique path L(c) ∈ L such that E(L(c)) ∩ E(σ(c)) ̸ = ∅ and |V (L(c)) ∩ Nρ(c)| ≥ 2.

Proof of claim. By assumption c is not a vortex cell. If c is dead ii ) is true and if it is dying i)

is true. Henceforth, assume that E(σ(c)) ̸ = ∅ as well as V (σ(c)) ̸ = Nρ(c), whence there exist a vertex u ∈ V (σ(c)) \ Nρ(c) and an edge e ∈ E(σ(c)) incident to u. Note that such an edge must exist since L is vital implying that u ∈ V (L) for some L ∈ L and since ρ is a blank rendition and c is not a vortex cell, u must be adjacent to some edge. Further, since u is not a node of the cell and ρ is a rendition, E(σ(c)) must contain at least one edge adjacent to u. Since ρ

is blank and |N (c)| ≤ 3 – for it is not a vortex – there is at most one path L ∈ L such that 

E(L) ∩ E(σ(c)) ̸ = ∅. To see this note that both terminals of L lie in V (G) \ V (σ(c)) since ρ is blank, in particular N (c) separates T from σ(c) with E(σ(c)) ∩ E(σ(c′)) = ∅ for all cells c′̸ = c.Thus, since |N (c)| ≤ 3 and paths in L are pairwise vertex-disjoint, at most one path in L may use an edge of σ(c). Similarly, since Nρ(c) separates u from T, at least one path of L satisfies 

E(L) ∩ E(σ(c)) ̸ = ∅. Combining both observations, such a path exists and is unique, and since both endpoints of the path are in T, it must have at least two common vertices with Nρ(c). This concludes the proof; see Figure 10 for a schematic illustration of the general setting. ⊣

Claim 6.5.3 . There is an m-locus (ρ′, M ) of (G, T ) satisfying (⋆⋆ ) such that for every non-vortex cell c ∈ C(ρ) with Nρ(c) = {u1, u 2, u 3} for distinct vertices and every separation (A, B ) in σ(c)

with A ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3}̸ = ∅ and B ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3}̸ = ∅, it holds |A ∩ B| ≥ 2.58 Proof of claim. The proof is by inductively refining ρ as follows. Let c ∈ C(σ) be a cell such that Nρ(c) = {u1, u 2, u 3} for distinct vertices and let (A, B ) be an order ≤ 1 separation in σ(c)

with A ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3}̸ = ∅ and B ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3}̸ = ∅: call such a cell bad . We prove how to refine the rendition by reducing the number of bad cells inductively. First note that if the separation is of order 0, then A, B partition {u1, u 2, u 3}. Thus, we may define ρ′ from ρ by removing the cell c and adding two new disjoint cells cA, c B ⊊ c ⊊ Σ by letting σρ′ (cA) = σρ(c)[ A] and σρ′ (cB ) = σρ(c)[ B] where cA has as nodes A ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3} and 

cB has as nodes B ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3}; both have ≤ 2 nodes. Then (ρ′, M ) is an m-locus of G in Σ

and P1, . . . , Pb still witness that it is d-stuffed with gap at most ℓ; the details are easy and left to the reader. In particular the number of bad cells in ρ′ is strictly less than the ones in ρ by construction. Thus let the separation be of order 1 and let x ∈ A ∩ B be the unique vertex (possibly 

x ∈ { u1, u 2, u 3}). Then construct ρ′ from ρ by deleting c and introducing new disjoint cells 

cA, c B ⊊ c ⊊ Σ with nodes A ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3} ∪ { x} and B ∩ { u1, u 2, u 3} ∪ { x} respectively, by letting σρ′ (cA) = σρ(c)[ A] and σρ′ (cB ) = σρ(c)[ B], and drawing x as a node inside c away from its boundary. One easily verifies that (ρ′, M ) is an m-locus of (G, T ) in Σ (after possibly fixing a tiebreaker if |Nρ′ (cA)| = 2 or |Nρ′ (cB )| = 2 ). To see this note that if some cycle C of M has an edge in E(σ(c)) , then the trace of C in ρ′ is still equal to its old trace except for its subsets of the boundary of cA and cB . Since cA, c B ⊆ c by construction, one can easily choose a tiebreaker (if necessary) such that the trace of C in ρ′ is as desired. Note however, that both cells cA and cB may have exactly three nodes each, and the nodes may still be separable by separators of order 1. In particular the number of bad cells may have increased. But one may now inductively continue with the cells cA and cB by refining the rendition ρ′ further at possible 1 separations. Since in each step we further decompose the graph 

σ(c), this procedure eventually stops, and we effectively reduced the number of bad cells. By repeating the above for every bad cell of ρ we conclude the proof (note that (⋆⋆ ) is still satisfied by construction). ⊣

Henceforth, we assume that (G, T ) and (ρ, M ) are as given by Claim 6.5.3; L is still a vital linkage in G.

Claim 6.5.4 . Let c ∈ C(ρ) be a non-vortex cell with Nρ(c) = {u1, u 2, u 3} for distinct vertices. If 

c is not dying, then |V (L(c)) ∩ Nρ(c)| = 2 .

Proof of claim. Assume that |V (L(c)) ∩ Nρ(c)) | = 3 , i.e., the path visits all three vertices. Fix an orientation of the path via a start and endpoint of the path and let ⟨v0, . . . , v m⟩ be a linear order on its vertices with respect to that orientation. Assume that ⟨u1, u 2, u 3⟩ are visited in that order by L(c) and let L′(c) ⊆ L(c) be the respective subpath starting in u1 and ending in u3. By Claim 6.5.3 we derive that there is a {u1}-{u3}-path P in σ(c) − u2. In particular 

P̸ = L(c)′. But since no path of L∗ := L \ { L(c)} uses an edge or vertex in V (σ(c)) – for L(c)

visits all three vertices {u1, u 2, u 3} by assumption – the path P is vertex-disjoint from every path in L∗. Let L be obtained from L(c) by replacing L′(c) with P, then L is a path and it has the same endpoints as L(c). By construction then L∗ ∪ { L} is a linkage in G with pattern τ (L)

contradicting the fact that L was vital. ⊣

Combining the above claims, one easily kills dying cells as follows. 59 Claim 6.5.5 . There exists a graph G′ ⊆ G, such that (G′, T ) is an annotated graph admitting a vital T -linkage L′ with τ (L′) = τ (L) and an m-locus (ρ′, M ) where ρ′ has no dying cells, and it agrees with ρ on all cells of ρ that are not dying, in particular the instance satisfies (⋆⋆ ).

Proof of claim. Let c ∈ C(ρ) be a dying cell. If E(σ(c)) = ∅ then simply delete c from the rendition; this does not have any effect on M, i.e., for every cycle C of M, C ⊆ G′ remains grounded in the resulting rendition, and no cell containing an edge of E(C) is altered. Henceforth assume that V (σ(c)) = Nρ(c) and E(σ(c)) ̸ = ∅. If |Nρ(c)| = 2 , then c is dead as a contradiction to it being dying; thus let Nρ(c) = {u1, u 2, u 3} for distinct vertices. By Claim 6.5.3 we derive that σ(c) is isomorphic to K3. We define ρ′ from ρ by deleting c and adding three new cells 

c1, c 2, c 3 /∈ C(σ) and defining σρ′ (cj ) to consist of the edge {ui, u k} for {i, j, k } = {1, 2, 3}. One easily verifies that ρ′ is a blank rendition of G still satisfying the assumptions of the claim, in particular, every cycle of M remains grounded in the new rendition. Note that this may affect at most one cycle of M, say C, since the cycles are pairwise disjoint. Furthermore, the cycles remain concentric, since the trace of C in the new rendition remains effectively equal when choosing the new cells carefully and setting the tie-breaker for the trace of the new cells accordingly. Finally, c1, c 2 and c3 are dead by definition. Repeating this argument for every dying cell concludes the proof. ⊣

Next, we kill cells away from cycles in M .

Claim 6.5.6 . There is an m-locus (ρ′, M ) of (G, T ) such that ρ′ agrees with ρ on all cells that contain an edge of a cycle in M . Further, all other non-vortex cells of ρ′ are dead, and the instance satisfies (⋆⋆ ).

Proof of claim. We proceed by induction, showing how to “kill” cells away from M . Let c ∈ C(ρ)

be a non-vortex cell that is not dead and such that it does not contain an edge of a cycle in M .By Claim 6.5.5 we may assume that it is not dying. Thus, by (⋆⋆ ) some path of L uses an edge of σ(c). Since c is not dying, Claim 6.5.1 implies that there is a unique path L(c) ∈ L using an edge in σ(c). Combining Claim 6.5.3 and Claim 6.5.4 we derive that |V (L(c)) ∩ Nρ(c)| = 2 . Let L′(c) be the maximum length subpath of L(c) contained in σ(c), and let u and v be its endpoints. Then u, v ∈ Nρ(c). Since no cycle of M admits an edge in σ(c) by assumption, we derive by (⋆⋆ ) that σ(c) = L(c). Thus, replace the cell c by introducing dead cells, one for each edge of L(c).By induction we can proceed as above until all non-vortex cells not containing an edge of a cycle of M are dead, never producing new non-dead cells, i.e., the induction stops after r ∈ N steps where r is the number of non-dead cells in C(ρ). Clearly the instance still satisfies (⋆⋆ ) for we did not alter the rendition on vortices. This concludes the proof of the claim. ⊣

Combining Claim 6.5.5 and Claim 6.5.6 we may assume that every non-vortex cell of ρ whose graph has no edge in common with a cycle in M is dead – by switching to the respective subgraph and rendition – and there are no dying cells left. Let M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩ and fix 

C = {C1, . . . , C m}.

Claim 6.5.7 . Let c ∈ C(ρ) be a non-dead cell such that E(σ(c)) ∩ E(⋃ C)̸ = ∅. There exists a graph G′ that is a minor of G, such that (G′, T ) is a k-annotated graph having a vital T -linkage 60 L′ with τ (L′) = τ (L) and an m-locus (ρ′, M ) where ρ′ agrees with ρ on all cells c′̸ = c, and all other cells of ρ′ are dead; in particular the instance satisfies (⋆⋆ ).

Proof of claim. Let c be as in the claim and let e ∈ E(σ)∩E(⋃ C) and let C ∈ C be the respective cycle with e ∈ E(C). There are two cases to consider: |Nρ(c)| ∈ { 2, 3}.

Case 1; |Nρ(c)| = 2 : Let Nρ(c) = {u1, u 2} for distinct vertices. Every edge of E(⋃ C) is part of a grounded cycle. Thus, since C is a set of disjoint cycles σ(c) contains edges of exactly one cycle in C, hence C, and in particular it must contain a subpath of length at least 2 of 

C since e ∈ E(C) ∩ E(σ(c)); let PC ⊆ C be maximal such that E(Pc) ⊆ E(σ(c)) . Since C

is a grounded cycle we derive that PC must be a path with endpoints u1, u 2.Further, since c is not dying, Claim 6.5.1 implies that L(c) exists. Let L′(c) be the maximum subpath of L(c) contained in σ(c) with endpoints u1, u 2, then L′(c) by vitality of L we derive that L′(c) = PC , else we could reroute L(c) by replacing L′(c) with PC

contradicting that L was the unique linkage of its pattern. Furthermore, since L is vital we derive that V (σ(c)) ⊆ V (L′(c)) . But then E(σ(c)) = E(L′(c)) , for any other edge could be used to reroute L′(c) inside σ(c) again refuting vitality of L. Claim 6.5.8 . Let (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) satisfy (⋆⋆ ) and such that M is tight. Then there exists a minor G′ of G and an m-locus (ρ′, M ′) of (G′, T ) in Σ together with a vital T -linkage L′ with

Finally M ′ is tight, for otherwise M was not—simply reroute the cycles of M accordingly by “decontracting” the edge if necessary—as a contradiction to the above assumption. Since the resulting instance satisfies (⋆⋆ ), by repeating the above iteratively reducing the amount of edges in E(⋃ L) ∩ E(⋃mi=1 Ci) with each iteration concludes the proof of the claim. ⊣

Applying Claim 6.5.8 to (G′, T ), L′, (ρ′, M ∗) we conclude the proof. Note that C∗ is still the set of vortices of ρ′ and Nρ′ (c) = Nρ(c) for all c ∈ C∗ by construction. 

6.2 Taming the instance 

In this subsection, we gather various technical lemmas that leverage Lemma 4.4 on dry wells, in order to tame the behaviour of the vital linkage L inside the Cm-disc ∆Cm of the outer cycle Cm

of M, given that (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) is exhausted. Let the segments of L be the maximal subpaths with ends in vortex boundaries that are otherwise disjoint from the graphs in vortices. We will adapt several ideas of [CKK24] to our setting, in order to lift the derived structure of the paths “inside” ∆Cm to the segments of L, which requires a non-trivial amount of work: Note that while 62 Figure 11: A schematic representation of the interaction of segments of a vital linkage with a large set of concentric cycles in a disc. The four segments are highlighted in different colours, and the vortices are marked by red regions. The restriction of the paths to the cycles is highlighted by red paths exhibiting a tamed behaviour. The four segments however exhibit “untamed” behaviour, where a single segment produces many “stacked” paths in the disc. the paths inside of ∆Cm may exhibit tamed behaviour, this is a priori not guaranteed for the segments of L; see Figure 11. To this extent, we define the following, which is essentially the restriction of the linkage L to a vortex-free ρ-aligned disc. 

Definition 6.6. Let Σ be a surface, and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of some annotated graph G. Let 

∆ ⊆ Σ be a vortex-free ρ-aligned disc. Let L be a linkage in G such that every endpoint of a path in L is contained in the graph of some cell c not contained in ∆. Let (G∆, Ω∆) be the 

∆-society in ρ. We define L(∆) as the set of paths obtained from L as follows. For every L ∈ L 

let P ⊆ L be a maximal subpath such that P has its endpoints in V (Ω ∆), the trace γP of P is contained in ∆, and no interior point of P is part of V (Ω ∆), then P ∈ L (∆) . We call L(∆) the 

restriction of L to ∆.Note that the above is not the same as L ∩ G∆ where G∆ denotes the crop of G by ∆, since 

L ∩ G∆ may contain paths with internal vertices on the boundary of ∆.One easily verifies that L(∆) is a set of pairwise internally disjoint paths; see the red paths in Figure 11 for an illustration. We extend the definition of patterns of linkages to sets of pairwise in-ternally disjoint paths P in the obvious way, i.e., τ (P) := {{ s, t } | P ∈ P has endpoints s and t}.Note that by definition, every path P ∈ L (∆) has all its vertices in graphs of cells of the rendition ρ′ obtained by restricting ρ to ∆. The following is an easy observation following from the definition of L(∆) (using the notation of Definition 6.6). 

Observation 6.7. Let P1, P 2 ∈ L (∆) be distinct such that V (P1) ∩ V (P2)̸ = ∅, then they intersect in a single vertex x with x ∈ V (Ω ∆) and P1, P 2 ⊊ L for some common L ∈ L . Further, 63 x is an endpoint of both, say that P1 ends in x and P2 starts in x, and the concatenation P1 ◦ P2

is a subpath of P ∈ L .

Proof. This is clear by construction: There exist L1, L 2 ∈ L (possibly equal) such that P1 ⊆ L1

and P2 ⊆ L2. If L1 = L2 we derive that |V (P1) ∩ V (P2)| ≤ 1 and if V (P1) ∩ V (P2)̸ = ∅ then their concatenation is clearly a subpath of L1. By definition of L(∆) , if they intersect they must do so in a vertex of V (Ω ∆). If L1̸ = L2 then V (P1) ∩ V (P2) = ∅ since L is a linkage. This concludes the proof. By definition of wells and exhausted instances, one can easily extract the former from the latter as follows. Given an exhausted instance (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) with outer cycle Cm of M and let ∆Cm

be the respective Cm-disc. Recall Definition 5.4 and define G(M, L) := G[ρ, C m] as the extended crop of G by Cm and let ΩCm be a cyclic orientation of V (Cm) respecting the cycle, i.e., two consecutive vertices with respect to Ωcm share an edge in Cm. Then (G(M, L), ΩCm ) is a society and it admits a ∆Cm -rendition ρ∆m . By construction ⋃ M ⊆ G(M, L) and ⋃ L(∆ Cm ) ⊆ G(M, L)

where L(∆ Cm ) is a set of internally disjoint paths with its endpoints in V (Ω Cm ); the resulting graph and L(∆ Cm ) are essentially given as in Figure 11, where the thick cycle highlights the outer cycle Cm and the red paths illustrate L(∆ Cm ).

Observation 6.8. Let Σ be a surface. Let m ≥ 1. Let (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) be exhausted where 

(G, T ) is an annotated graph, L is some vital T -linkage in G and (ρ, M ) is an m-locus of (G, T )

in Σ. Then W := ( G(M, L), Ω∆Cm , ρ ∆m , M, L(∆ Cm )) is an m-well. And if M is tight, then W

is tight. Note that the reason we add back Cm in the construction of G(L, M ) is purely for aesthetic reasons: Otherwise, we could only guarantee an (m − 1) -well obtained from M by additionally deleting the outer cycle, for it may not be part of the initial ∆Cm -society. Thus, given an annotated graph (G, T ) with m-locus (ρ, M ) and a vital T -linkage L, we write (

G(M, L), Ω∆Cm , ρ ∆Cm , M, L(∆ Cm )

> )

to mean the m-well obtained by Observation 6.8. Note that it is uniquely defined up to reversing Ω∆Cm . It is not difficult to see that if (G, T ), L, (ρ, M )

is exhausted and M is tight, then (G(M, L), Ω∆Cm , ρ ∆Cm , M, L(∆ Cm )) is dry.
