Lemma 6.9 brings to light a clear pattern for how a vital linkage interacts with a sequence of concentric cycles M inside the disc bounded by its outer cycle. It turns out that we can leverage the above, to tame the behaviour of L “outside” of the cycles of M as we discuss next. To this extent, we introduce segments of a linkage given a blank rendition, which we define as follows; see Figure 11 for a schematic illustration. 

Definition 6.10. Let Σ be a surface and ρ a blank Σ-rendition of an annotated graph (G, T ).Let P be a path in G. A segment of P (in ρ) is a grounded subpath P ′ ⊆ P with both endpoints in vortex-boundaries such that no internal vertex of P ′ is part of a vortex-boundary. Note that by definition, every segment is a loop or a link; recall Definition 6.3. The following is an easy observation. 

Observation 6.11. Let Σ be a surface and ρ a blank Σ-rendition of some annotated graph (G, T ).Let L be a T -linkage and S the set consisting of all segments of paths in L. Then S is a set of internally disjoint paths and every endpoint of a path in S is part of at most two paths in S.Recall that, given a disc ∆, a sequence of concentric cycles ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩ in some ∆-rendition ρ

and a grounded path P whose trace γP is contained in ∆ with its endpoints on the boundary of 

∆ but internally disjoint from the boundary of ∆, we denote by ∆P the unique disc of ∆ − γP ,

after taking the topological closure of the component, that does not contain the trace of C1.We refine on this definition as follows. 

Definition 6.12. Let Σ be a surface. Let m ≥ 1. Let (ρ, M ) be an m-locus of some annotated graph (G, T ) such that ρ is a Σ-skeleton and M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩. Let L be a vital T -linkage. Let 

P ∈ L (∆ m) with trace γP . Then we define ∆MP to be the unique disc of ∆Cm − γP , after taking the topological closure of the component, that does not contain the trace of C1. We write ∆P

for simplicity if M is clear from context. Note that ∆P is ρ-aligned by construction. The following is an easy observation. 

Observation 6.13. Let Σ be a surface. Let m ≥ 1. Let (ρ, M ) be an m-locus of some annotated graph (G, T ) such that ρ is a Σ-skeleton and M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩. Let L be a vital T -linkage. Let P, P ′ ∈ L (∆ Cm ). Then either ∆P and ∆P ′ are internally disjoint or one is contained in the other. 65 Proof. By Observation 6.7, P and P ′ are internally disjoint. Since ρ is a Σ-rendition, if the trace γP of P is not internally disjoint from ∆P ′ then γP ⊊ ∆P ′ . To see this note that γP is internally disjoint from the trace of Cm as well as from the trace of P ′. One easily verifies that 

∆P ⊆ ∆P ′ . Switching the roles of P and P ′ concludes the proof. The following lemma proves that, given an exhausted instance (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) such that M is tight, no subpath of L ∈ L whose trace is contained in ∆Cm may intersect Cm in more than two components, i.e., the traces of the paths do not “bounce off” the outer cycle while staying inside the disc. In particular, together with Observation 6.7 this implies that L(∆ Cm ) is a linkage (as opposed to internally disjoint). 

Lemma 6.14. Let Σ be a surface. Let m ≥ 2. Let (ρ, M ) be an m-locus of some annotated graph (G, T ) such that M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩ is tight and ρ is a Σ-skeleton. Let L be a vital 

T -linkage. Let L ∈ L and let S ⊆ L be a segment. Let P ⊆ S be a subpath such that its trace is contained in ∆Cm and its endpoints lie on the boundary of ∆Cm . Then P ∩ Cm consists of at most 2 components, each of which is a single vertex of Cm.In particular, there is a unique path P∆m ∈ L (∆ m) that is a subpath of P and L(∆ m) is a linkage. 

Proof. Note that the first part of the lemma implies the second, i.e., if P ∩ Cm consists of at most two components then, since P was arbitrary, we derive that L(∆ m) is a linkage, since, a priori, if two paths of L(∆ m) intersect, then they belong to a common segment and intersect at a common endpoint by Observation 6.7. Since P ∩ Cm consists of at most two components this is impossible; we are left to prove the former.

for i = 1 , 2.

Proof of claim. Clearly ℓ > 1, since otherwise P would be internally disjoint from V (Cm) and thus P ∩ Cm = {x1, x 2}. Similarly, 

Pℓ is not a single vertex for else Pℓ−1 would be longer again contradicting its existence. This implies that ℓ ≤ 2 since otherwise the concatenation P ′ = P1 ◦ P2 ⊆ P would be a shorter path satisfying the assumptions of the lemma and P ′ ∩ Cm consists of at least 3 components by Observation 6.7. To see that |V (Pm) ∩ V (Cm)| = 3 , note that we already established that P1, P 2 have their endpoints on V (Cm). Since neither of the two paths consist of a single vertex, and neither has an edge in common with E(Cm), we derive that |V (P1)∩V (Cm)| = 2 as well as |V (P2)∩V (Cm)| = 2 .Finally, since they share exactly one common endpoint we derive that |V (P ) ∩ V (Cm)| = 3 . ⊣

Thus let x ∈ V (P ) ∩ V (Cm) \ { x1, x 2}. Let Ω∆Cm = ⟨v1, . . . , v m⟩ be a linear order on V (Cm)

By Lemma 6.9 and the assumption of the theorem, we derive that W :=(G(M, L), Ω∆Cm , ρ ∆Cm , M, L(∆ Cm )) is dry.

Intuitively speaking, the following lemma proves that given an exhausted instance (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) such that M is tight, the trace of a subpath of L ∈ L that lies outside of ∆Cm with both endpoints on its boundary has to be an essential curve. This marks the main result of this section. 

Theorem 6.17. Let Σ be a surface and m ≥ 2. Let (ρ, M ) be an m-locus of some annotated graph (G, T ) in Σ such that M = ⟨C1, . . . , C m⟩ is tight and ρ is a Σ-skeleton. Let L be a vital 

T -linkage in G and let (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) be exhausted. Let S(L) be the set of segments of L.Let ∆ ⊆ Σ be a vortex-free ρ-aligned disc such that for each segment S ∈ S (L) its trace is either contained in ∆ or is internally disjoint from ∆. Let S ∈ S (L) be such that its trace is contained in ∆. Let L(∆ Cm ) be the restriction of L to ∆Cm . Let P1, P 2 ∈ L (∆ m) be distinct such that 

P1, P 2 ⊊ S and both contain at least one edge each. Then ∆P1 ∩ ∆P2 = ∅.

Proof. Recall that since ρ is a Σ-skeleton, all paths in L(∆ m) are grounded, and every cell represents a single edge of the graph. Since (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) is exhausted and M is dry, Lemma 6.9 implies that

Claim 6.17.1 . It holds ∆P1 ⊊ ∆P2 or ∆P2 ⊊ ∆P1 .

Proof of claim. This follows immediately from Observation 6.13 together with the fact that they are not internally disjoint. ⊣

By Claim 6.17.1 we may assume without loss of generality that ∆P1 ⊊ ∆P2 ; otherwise rename

In particular, the trace of P ∗ is contained in 

∆; see Figure 14 for a schematic illustration. To this extent, let P ⊆ P ∗ with one endpoint x1, and the other endpoint x ∈ V (Cm) \ { x1} such that P is otherwise internally disjoint from V (Cm). To see that this exists, note that E(P ∗) ∩ E(Cm) = ∅

by our assumption that (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) is exhausted and further |V (P ∗) ∩ V (Cm)| ≥ 2. By construction, the trace γP of P satisfies the following: 

(⋆) either γP ⊆ ∆Cm or γP ⊆ cl (Σ \ ∆Cm ).

Claim 6.17.2 . γP is not contained in ∆Cm .72 Proof of claim. Assume the contrary. Let Q = P1 ∪ P ⊆ S, then Q is a path and its trace is contained in ∆Cm with both its endpoints on the boundary of ∆Cm . But u1, u 2, x are pairwise distinct, and {u1, u 2, x } ⊆ V (Q) ∩ V (Cm). In particular |V (Q) ∩ V (Cm)| ≥ 3 as a contradiction to Lemma 6.14. ⊣

Claim 6.17.3 . γP is not contained in cl (Σ \ ∆Cm ).

Proof of claim. Assume the contrary. Then by Definition 6.15 together with the fact that γP ⊆ ∆

with ∆ vortex-free, we derive that P is a Cm-boundary jump; note that it admits at least one edge by construction. This is a contradiction to Lemma 6.16. ⊣

Combining both claims we derive a contradiction to (⋆), concluding the proof. 

6.3 Controlling vortices 

This subsection is devoted to the proof of technical lemmas needed to show that, given an exhausted instance (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) in some surface Σ with a “large enough” locus with respect to the depth of the vortices, the number of non-simple loops and links in S(L) is bounded in d

and the genus of the surface Σ, whereas simple loops are guaranteed to not penetrate “deep” into M . The main result consists of a rather technical analysis on the interaction of L with vortices, combining Proposition 3.7 and Theorems 5.8 and 6.17; recall the relevant definitions. We will need T -normal linear decompositions which we define as follows. 

Definition 6.18. Let (G, T ) be an annotated graph and let α = (⟨u1, . . . , u n⟩, ⟨U1, . . . , U n⟩) be a linear decomposition (possibly with gap) of some society (H, Ω) with H ⊆ G. Let 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n

and let α′ be the [i, j ]-restriction of α. We call α′ T -normal if for every t ∈ T either t ∈ ⋂jp=i Up

or t / ∈ ⋃jp=i Ui.For ease of notation we define what we mean by “strips” bounded by segments of vortices; see Figure 15 for an illustration. 

Figure 15: Two examples of (F1, F 2)-strips, highlighted in orange, one between two vortices, drawn in red, and one on a single vortex. 

Definition 6.19. Let Σ be a surface and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of some graph G. Let c1, c 2 ∈ C(ρ)

be vortices (possibly equal) with respective vortex societies (σ(c1), Ωc1 ) and (σ(c2), Ωc2 ). For 

i = 1 , 2, let Fi be a segment of Ωci with endpoints si, t i such that F1 ∩ F2 = ∅. Let γ1, γ 2 be 

ρ-aligned curves such that for i = 1 , 2 the curve γi has endpoints si, t i and such that γ1 ∪ γ2

together with the boundary segments η1, η w of c1, c 2 that contain exactly the nodes of F1, F 2,

respectively, bound a vortex-free disc ∆. Then we call ∆ an (F1, F 2)-strip (guarded by γ1, γ 2).73 Note that the (F1, F 2)-strip is ρ-aligned as the boundary of every cell is ρ-aligned by definition. The following is a folklore topological fact transcribed to our setting. 

Observation 6.20. Let Σ be a surface and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of some graph G. Let 

c1, c 2 ∈ C(ρ) be vortices (possibly equal) with respective vortex societies (σ(c1), Ωc1 ) and 

(σ(c2), Ωc2 ). Let S(c1, c 2) be a set of at least two ρ-homotopic paths, each with one endpoint in 

N (c1) and the other in N (c2). For i = 1 , 2 let Fi be segments of Ω( ci) such that S(c1, c 2) is an 

F1-F2-linkage. Then there exist S, S ′ ∈ S (c1, c 2) with traces γ, γ ′ and disjoint segments F ∗ 

> i

⊆ Fi

such that γ, γ ′ guard an (F ∗ 

> 1

, F ∗

)-strip ∆, and such that S(c1, c 2) is an F ∗ 

> 1

-F ∗ 

> 2

-linkage, and the trace of each path in S(c1, c 2) is contained in ∆.

The following is the main technical lemma of this section. It is a powerful tool that, given a vital linkage L, leverages Proposition 3.7 in order to “control” the number of homotopic non-simple loops and homotopic links of L, as well as to quantify “how much” simple loops can interact with the concentric cycles of some large locus given that the vortices of the instance are properly 

d-linked; recall Definitions 5.1 and 6.10 and the fact that if a linear decomposition is properly 

d-linked, then the adhesion is (d + 1) and it admits a linkage of order (d + 1) between the bags. This “off-by-one” definition is the reason why the following lemma is stated as is.

Lemma 6.21. Let ℓ′, k ′, d 1, d 2 ≥ 1. Let Σ be a surface. There exists a function g6.21 : N → N

such that the following holds. Let ρ be a blank and stretched Σ-rendition of some annotated 74 graph (G, T ). Let c1, c 2 ∈ C(ρ) be vortices (possibly equal). Let α1 = (⟨u1, . . . , u ℓ′ ⟩, ⟨U1, . . . , U ℓ′ ⟩)

be a linear decomposition of the vortex society (σ(c1), Ωc1 ) of adhesion at most d1, and let 

α2 = ( ⟨w1, . . . , w k′ ⟩, ⟨W1, . . . , W k′ ⟩) be a linear decomposition of the vortex society (σ(c2), Ωc2 )

of adhesion at most d2. Let 1 ≤ ℓ1 < ℓ 2 ≤ ℓ′ and 1 ≤ k1 < k 2 ≤ k′ be such that the [ℓ1, ℓ 2]-restriction of α1 is T -normal and properly (d1 − 1) -linked and the [k1, k 2]-restriction of α2 is 

T -normal and properly (d2 − 1) -linked. Further assume that for every i ∈ [k1, k 2] and every 

j ∈ [ℓ1, ℓ 2] it holds Ui ∩ Wj = ∅.Let L be a vital T -linkage such that G = ⋃ L ∪ σ(c1) ∪ σ(c2). Let S be a set of ρ-homotopic segments of L such that every P ∈ S has one endpoint in {uℓ1 , . . . , u ℓ2 } and the other in 

{wk1 , . . . , w k2 }. Then |S| < g 6.21 (d1 + d2).Furthermore, g6.21 (d) ∈ 2poly (d).

Proof. Let f := f3.7 and let d := d1 + d2. Finally fix g(d) := 12 f (d, d ) + 14; we claim that 

g satisfies the theorem; g(d) ∈ 2poly (d) by definition. Note that if c1 = c2 we may still have 

Ωc1̸ = Ω c2 ; then the second is the “reverse” order of the first. Towards a contradiction, assume that for our choice of g the theorem is wrong, and let S be as in the theorem with |S| ≥ 12 f (d, d ) + 14 . Let S(L) be the set of all segments of L; in particular S ⊆ S (L).Without loss of generality, by renaming the vertices accordingly with respect to the cyclic order Ωci for i = 1 , 2, respectively, we may assume that ℓ1 = 1 and let ℓ := ℓ2 as well as k1 = 1 

and let k := k2. Note that if c1 = c2 then d1 = d2, but it may still be true that α1̸ = α2, in particular the linear decompositions are allowed to have differing bags. Furthermore, note that since ρ is stretched, the additional assumption {ui | i ∈ [ℓ1, ℓ 2]} ∩ { wi | i ∈ [k1, k 2]} = ∅ is only relevant if c1 = c2. It turns out that the additional assumption makes the proof in the single vortex case analogous to the one with two vortices. This is intuitively easy to see, since there is no tangible way to differentiate between both: If the graph σ(c) were to consist of two disjoint components one could easily split the single vortex into two vortices. In addition, without loss of generality, we may assume that u1, w 1, u ℓ, w k are endpoints of some paths in S since otherwise we may restrict the segments of Ωc1 and Ωc2 accordingly. Let 

U := {u1, . . . , u ℓ} and W := {w1, . . . , w k}.Since L is a linkage, S is a set of pairwise internally disjoint paths. And since the paths in S

are grounded and pairwise ρ-homotopic, there exists a ρ-aligned vortex-free disc ∆ ⊆ Σ that intersects N (ρ) exactly in all of U ∪ W such that for every P ∈ S its trace is contained in ∆. This follows from Observation 6.20, but we provide an exemplary construction for completion. To see this note that the traces of any two ρ-homotopic paths P, P ′ ∈ S bound a disc ∆P,P ′ together with the respective segments of vortex boundaries between them by definition of homotopy, call these segments F1, F 2 for c1, c 2 respectively. That is, given the traces γP , γ P ′ of P, P ′,

there exists an (F1, F 2)-strip ∆P,P ′ guarded by γP , γ P ′ ; in particular that disc is ρ-aligned by construction. Call two paths P, P ′ ∈ P consecutive if the trace of no other path P ∈ S is contained in ∆P,P ′ . Note here that there may still be P ∗ ∈ S (L) \ S such that the trace of 

P is contained in ∆P,P ′ but then P ∗ needs to be a simple loop with both ends either in U or both in W ; if c1 = c2 then P and P ′ may also be (simple) loops, but they still have endpoints in the different segments. Finally, one can construct ∆ by gluing the discs ∆P,P ′ of pairwise 75 consecutive paths carefully – possibly omitting one such disc if the resulting surface would be no disc – until the resulting disc contains the traces of all paths in S.Note that since ∆ is a disc and all the paths in S have their traces in ∆ with both endpoints on its boundary, we derive that – after possibly reversing the order of ⟨w1, . . . , w k⟩ and renaming – for any two paths P1, P 2 ∈ S with ends ui1 , w j1 and ui2 , w j2 we have 

(⋆) if 1 ≤ i1 ≤ i2 ≤ ℓ then 1 ≤ j1 ≤ j2 ≤ k and vice versa. Let P∆, P ∆ be the paths in S with endpoints u1, w 1 and uℓ, w k, respectively, and let ˜P ⊊ S be the at most six paths with endpoints in u1, w 1, u ℓ, w k including P ∆, P ∆11 . Let S′ := S \ ˜P, in particular |S ′| ≥ 12 f (d, d ) + 8 , then for every path in S′ its trace is contained in the interior of 

∆, in particular ⋃ S′ is contained in the crop of G by ∆; see the left hand figure in Figure 16, where the paths ˜P are highlighted by purple and blue tubes in the right hand figure. of size ≥ 6f (d, d ) + 4 such that no two paths in S′′ end in the same vertex of U . Applying the pigeonhole principle again, we derive that there exists a set S∗ ⊆ S ′′ of size ≥ 3f (d, d ) + 2

Our main goal is to generalise Lemma 6.21 to a more general setting. The first step in that direction is an easy combinatorial result that guarantees the existence of T -normal segments in linear decompositions of sufficient length. 

Lemma 6.22. Let ℓ∗, ℓ, k ≥ 1 and t ≥ 2. Let Σ be a surface. Let ρ be a Σ-rendition of some 

k-annotated graph (G, T ). Let c1 ∈ C(ρ) be a vortex. Let α = ( ⟨u1, . . . , u ℓ∗ ⟩, ⟨U1, . . . , U ℓ∗ ⟩) be a linear decomposition of c. Let J ⊆ [ℓ∗] be a set of indices such that |J| = ℓ. If ℓ > (2 k + 1)( t + 1) ,

then there exist j1, j 2 ∈ J with |[j1, j 2] ∩ J| ≥ t, and such that the [j1, j 2]-segment of α is 

T -normal. 

Proof. Let T = {t1, . . . , t k} be non-empty for some k ≥ 1. For every t ∈ [k] let 1 ≤ st ≤ ft ≤ ℓ∗

be such that t ∈ ⋂ft 

> p=st

Up and t / ∈ Uq for all q / ∈ [st, f t]; this exists since α is a linear decomposition. Let X = {st, f t | t ∈ [k]}. Then |X| ≤ 2k. Let X = {x1, . . . , x m} for some m ∈ [2 k] such that 79 x1 ≤ . . . , ≤ xm. We derive the existence of (m + 1) intervals I0, . . . , I m of [ℓ∗] satisfying the following i) |I0 ∩ X| = 1 as well as |Im ∩ X| = 1 and I0 = [0 , x 1] (where x1 = 0 is possible) as well as 

Im = [ xm, ℓ ] (where xm = ℓ is possible) ii) for every i ∈ [1 , m − 1] it holds |Ii ∩ X| = 2 and Ii = [ xi, x i+1 ].In particular, note that for 0 ≤ i < j ≤ m and every x ∈ Ii and y ∈ Ij it holds x ≤ y and if 

Ii ∩ Ij̸ = ∅, then j = i + 1 and Ii ∩ Ii+1 = {xi+1 }.Note that ℓ > (2 k + 1)( t + 1) implies ℓ > (m + 1)( t + 1) . By the pigeonhole principle, there is p ∈ [0 , m ] such that Ip is an interval of length at least (t + 2) containing at least (t + 2) 

elements of J. In particular since Ip = [ xp, x p+1 ] we derive the xp+1 ≥ xp + t + 2 . Hence 

I := [ xp + 1 , x p+1 − 1] is a non-empty interval containing at least t elements of J. Thus, there exist xp + 1 ≤ j1 < j 2 ≤ xp+1 with and j1, j 2 ∈ J and |[j1, j 2] ∩ J| ≥ t.

Claim 6.22.1 . Let i ∈ I and t ∈ T ∩ Ui, then t ∈ ⋂ 

> j∈I

Uj .

Proof of claim. First note that by i) and ii ), we derive I ∩ X = ∅. In particular st, f t /∈ I. By construction of st, f t we derive that t ∈ Uj if and only if j ∈ [st, f t]. Since t ∈ Ui we derive that 

st ≤ i ≤ ft and in particular since I ∩ X = ∅ we derive st ≤ xp + 1 ≤ xp+1 − 1 ≤ ft and finally 

t ∈ Uj for all j ∈ I. This concludes the proof. ⊣

Claim 6.25.1 . Every path in S is a subpath of some path in L∗. Further every path in L∗ has at least one vertex in two of {Usu , U tu , W sw , W tw }.

86 6.4 Taming simple loops 

In a first instance, we prove the main ingredient to prove iii ) of Theorem 6.4, i.e., given an exhausted tuple (G, T ), L, (ρ, M ) in some surface Σ, such that the locus is “large enough”, simple loops cannot intersect cycles “deeply nested” in M, whence we will be able to remove them completely from the instance. We outsource the following lemma, as we will use the same technique to tame non-simple loops and links. The lemma is an easy combinatorial filtering result. 

Lemma 6.26. Let (G, Ω) be a society and d, ℓ ≥ 1. Let S1, S 2 ⊊ V (Ω) be disjoint non-empty segments. Let gap 1, gap 2 ⊆ V (Ω) be segments (not necessarily disjoint) with |gap i| ≤ ℓ for 

i = 1 , 2 and such that either gap 1 = gap 2 or Si ∩ gap j = ∅ for {i, j } = {1, 2}. Let u1, . . . , u m be a labelling of V (Ω) such that they appear in that order in Ω for m := |V (Ω) |. Let S be a set of 

16 d + 4 ℓ internally disjoint paths in G such that every path in S has one endpoint in S1 and the other in S2, and no vertex in S1 ∪ S2 is part of more than two paths in S.Then there exist disjoint non-empty intervals F1, F 2 ⊊ [m] such that i) for i = 1 , 2 it holds {uj | j ∈ Fi} ⊆ Si and |Fi| ≥ d, 

ii) {ui | i ∈ F1 ∪ F2} ∩ (gap 1 ∪ gap 2) = ∅, and iii) there is S′ ⊆ S of size d such that every path in S′ has one endpoint in {ui | i ∈ F1} and the other in {ui | i ∈ F2}.

Proof. We start by defining linear orders on S1, S 2 “induced” by Ω.

i = 1 , 2, then since Si is a proper segment we derive the existence of x ∈ V (Ω) \ V (Si) such that either si, x, t i or ti, x, s i appear in the order listed in Ω. Without loss of generality assume the latter and call si the start of Si and ti the end of Si. Then, again since Si is a segment, we may define a linear order x ⪯i y on Si by saying x ⪯i y ⇐⇒ si, x, y, t i

appear in the order listed in Ω. In particular si ⪯i ti.Next we filter S by removing all the paths that have an endpoint in one of gap 1, gap 2

Note that while Theorem 6.27 bounds how “deep” simple loops may interact with a locus, it does not bound the overall number of homotopic simple loops, simply because ρ-homotopic 89 simple loops at some common vortex c may bound disjoint discs. There is no way to “efficiently” bound the number of such simple loops, but fortunately we will not have too. 

6.5 Controlling homotopic links and non-simple loops 

In a second instance, we prove iv ) of Theorem 6.4, i.e., given an exhausted tuple (G, T ), L, (ρ, M )

in some surface Σ, such that the locus is “large enough”, there are not “too many” links or non-simple loops of the same homotopy type. The proof is easier than that of Theorem 6.27 as it does not rely and is independent of the size of the locus.

6.6 Embedding the instance 

We are ready to prove Theorem 6.4 by combining Theorems 6.5, 6.27 and 6.28. For simplicity we extend the definition of vitality of linkages to sets of internally disjoint paths as follows. Let G be a graph and S a set of internally disjoint paths in G. Recall that 

τ (S) := {(u, v ) | a path in S had endpoints u and v} is a multiset. Then we call S vital in G if for every set of internally disjoint paths S∗ with τ (S∗) = τ (S) it holds S∗ = S.

# 7 Eliminating apices and building nests 

While Proposition 3.8 already guarantees both a large wall grounded in the rendition together with private nests for each vortex, those nests are not large enough for us to apply Theorem 5.8. This is fundamentally a consequence of the way Proposition 3.8 – and more generally the main results in [GSW25] – is proven and how the vortex nests are created. The goal of this section is to show that we are able to surround the vortices with disjoint nests of essentially arbitrary size, with the depth of their vortices being our goal size. The main tools in this section are inspired by results due to Diestel, Kawarabayashi, Müller, and Wollan [DKMW12]. Since their results produce several annoying problems for us, which we will explain in detail in a moment, we have to do quite a bit of work to fix them up for our purposes. 

Representativity and distance in a rendition. We start by generalising the notion of represen-tativity to renditions in a natural way and introduce a measure for distance between nodes of a rendition. Let Σ be a surface and let ρ be a Σ-rendition of a graph G. Then we let Gρ be the graph on the vertex set N (ρ) and with the following edges  

> •

for each non-vortex cell c ∈ C(ρ), add all edges between distinct vertices in N (c), and  

> •

for each vortex, with (H, Ω) being its society and with Ω = ⟨v1, . . . , v k⟩, we introduce the edges vivi+1 for all i ∈ [k − 1] and the edge vkv1.16 

We then define an embedding ψρ of Gρ on Σ by drawing N (ρ) onto Σ exactly as ρ does and for each edge uv ∈ E(Gρ) choosing a cell c ∈ C(ρ) such that c ∈ N (c), letting T be the closure of a component of bd (c) − { u, v } for which T is disjoint from N (c) \ { u, v } but contains both u and 

v, and setting ψ(uv ) = T . The representativity of ρ is then simply the representativity of ψρ.Furthermore, given two distinct vertices u, v ∈ N (ρ), the distance between u and v in ρ is the minimum number of nodes of ρ that a ρ-aligned curve ending in u and v contains. Given two vortices c1, c 2 with vortex societies (H1, Ω1), (H2, Ω2), the distance between c1 and c2 in ρ is the minimum distance between a vertex in V (Ω 1) and a vertex in V (Ω 2) in ρ.Given an embedding ψρ derived from a rendition ρ of a graph G in a surface Σ, we want to translate a curve T in Σ that meets ψρ only in the vertices of G into what should be a ρ-aligned curve. It is easy to see that T can be shifted in Σ such that it meets none of the cells of ρ.However, if T passes through the interior of a vortex c of ρ, there may be no way to simply shift 

T to avoid c. Nonetheless, we want to use such a curve T to cut apart our surface in this section.   

> 16 We delete all parallel edges after this process to turn Gρinto a simple graph.

95 We call a curve in Σ that meets ρ only in nodes and the interior of vortices of ρ a ρ-conformal curve . The following is immediate from our discussion. 

Observation 7.1. Given an embedding ψρ of G with representativity at most r derived from a rendition ρ of G in a surface Σ, then for any curve T that meets ψρ only in V (G) there exists a ρ-conformal curve T ′ such that T ∩ V (G) = T ′ ∩ V (G), the curve T ′ intersects none of the non-vortex cells of ρ, and T ′ intersects a vortex of ρ if and only if T intersects said vortex. We will need a small technical result on curves witnessing low representativity from [DKMW12]. For a graph G with an embedding ψ in a surface Σ, a face f of ψ, and a closed curve C, let 

comp (C, f ) denote the number of components of C ∩ f .

Proposition 7.2 (Diestel et al. [DKMW12]) . Let G be a graph with an embedding ψ in a surface Σ of positive genus and let F be the set of faces of ψ. For a positive integer r, consider all genus-reducing curves C that meet ψ only in ψ(V (G)) and such that |ψ(V (G)) ∩ C| < r . Let 

C be chosen such that ∑  

> f∈F

comp (C, f ) is minimal. Then comp (C, f ) ≤ 1 for all f ∈ F .What we would love to do at this point is to simply import the Lemmas 14 and 16 from [DKMW12] which first fuse together vortices that are close together and then increase the representativity of the surface our rendition is working in. However, both of their lemmas introduce vertices that must be deleted – commonly called apices – which have neighbours outside of the vortices of the established rendition. Such apices are called major apices .17 This causes immense problems for us, because we desperately need all newly introduced apices to only have neighbours in vortices. As such, we will have to go through a much more considerable effort than Diestel et al. to establish our versions of their lemmas. In particular, we make the following adjustments: We will only work on graphs which already host a linkage whose vitality we are trying to preserve. In the course of both of our modifications, we will actually change the graph, the rendition, and the linkage we are working on to produce a new instance with more convenient properties for us. The vortices we create throughout will be more complicated than what Diestel et al. find, but in exchange we will have more control over the apices that get created. 

7.1 Joining close vortices 

Our first goal will be to join together vortices in a rendition which are not very far apart. This is dealt with in Lemma 14 of [DKMW12], where the nodes on the curve that shows that the two vortices are not far from each other are simply taken to be apices. For us this is not an option and we will have to make some more involved modifications to our graph. 

Lemma 7.3. Let b, d, r be positive integers. Let Σ be a surface, let ρ be a Σ-rendition with depth d and breadth b of a graph G, let L be a linkage in G, and let c1, c 2 be two distinct   

> 17 Notably we are being a bit vague here by what we mean by “neighbours outside of vortices”. Our requirements are actually slightly more lax than what is usually required. In the broader context of Graph Minor Structure Theory an apex is considered major if it sees any node of the rendition or has a neighbour in a non-vortex cell of said rendition. In our case, we will actually allow apices that have neighbours on the boundary of vortices, which are nodes of the rendition.

96 vortices in ρ such that there exists a ρ-aligned bd (c1)-bd (c2)-curve T that is otherwise disjoint from all vortices of ρ such that |T ∩ N (ρ)| ≤ r.Then there exists a set A ⊆ V (G) with |A| ≤ 2d, a set B with |B| = 2 |T ∩ N (ρ)|, a graph G′, alinkage L′ in G′, a Σ-rendition ρ′ of G′ with breadth b − 1, and a ρ′-aligned disc ∆ such that i) V (G′) = ( V (G) ∪ B) \ (A ∪ (T ∩ N (ρ))) ,

Since we effectively only added a single vertex to both σρ(c1) and σρ(c2) it is easy to see that the depth of (σρ(∆) , Ω′) is at most the sum of the depths of c1 and c2 plus 2, as the largest transaction in (σρ(∆) , Ω′) at worst consists of a large transaction in (G1, Ω1), another large transaction in (G2, Ω2), and two extra paths that may appear due to us copying vh and vℓ.The first four items of our lemma are now easy to confirm according to our construction with G∗

being the final graph we return together with the rendition ρ∗. Note that our construction also immediately shows that the breadth of ρ∗ is one less than the breadth of ρ. The fact that all new endpoints that L∗ added lie in V (σρ∗ (∆)) is also an easy consequence of our construction. Finally, the last point was already explicitly guaranteed earlier during the construction of L∗.

7.2 Pumping up the representativity 

Our second goal of this section is to pump up the representativity of the rendition we are dealing with. This process is taken care of by Lemma 16 in [DKMW12]. As noted earlier, we will also have to modify this lemma and its proof quite a bit to make it work for our purposes. In the statement we discuss the existence of two surfaces independent of what kind of genus-reducing curve we find, but we will only need more than one surface if the genus-reducing curve is separating. For the other cases we will let the pairs of objects be equal. 

Lemma 7.4. Let k, t, r, r ′, b, d, w with r′ ≥ r and w ≥ 2r be positive integers. Let Σ be a surface of positive genus, let G be a graph with a Σ-rendition ρ with depth d, breadth b, and the property that any two vortices have distance at least r′, and let L be a linkage in G. Furthermore, there exists a closed, ρ-aligned disc ∆M ⊆ Σ and a flat w-mesh M in G such that the trace of M is found in ∆M and ∆M is disjoint from all vortices in ρ.If the representativity of ρ is less than r, then there exist graphs G1, G 2, surfaces Σ1, Σ2, linkages 

L1, L2, a Σ1-rendition ρ1 of G1, and a Σ2-rendition ρ2 of G2, such that for both i ∈ [2] :i) the genus of Σi is less than the genus of Σ,

ii) the breadth of ρi is at most b + 2 ,

iii) the depth of ρi is at most d + 2 ,

iv) |L i| ≤ |L| + r + 6 d, 

v) if L is vital, then Li is vital, and 99 vi) there exists a j ∈ [2] , a (w − 2r)-mesh M ′ ⊆ M, and a closed, ρj -aligned disc ∆′ ⊆ Σj ,

such that M ′ is a flat mesh in ρj , the trace of M ′ in ρj is contained in ∆′, and all vortices of ρj are disjoint from ∆′.

Proof. As ρ has representativity less than r, we may let T be a closed, ρ-conformal, genus-reducing curve such that |T ∩ N (ρ)| is minimal. Note that thanks to Proposition 7.2, we know that T intersects each vortex of ρ in at most one component. Furthermore, since r′ ≥ r, we know that T intersects at most one vortex c of ρ and if T intersects Nρ(c), then either |Nρ(c) ∩ T | = 1 

and T does not intersect c itself – it thus just “grazes” c – or |Nρ(c) ∩ T | = 2 and T ∩ c consists of a single component. We note that for the sake of this proof, we can ignore the first option. We now have a few cases to distinguish: First of all, we are interested in whether T is a separating curve or a non-separating curve. If T is non-separating, we distinguish further between the cases in which Σ − T has two open holes and the case in which it has only one open hole. 

T is separating. We first suppose that there exists a vortex c in ρ such that T ∩ c is non-empty and thus consists of a single component. The other case in which T intersects no vortices essentially just uses a subset of the arguments we will now present and thus we will not discuss it explicitly. 7.3 Building nests in renditions with large distances and high representativity 

Now that we can ensure that the representativity of our renditions is high and that any two vortices in them are at large distance with respect to each other, we need some tools to build nests around each of these vortices that will end up being stacked with respect to each other. We start with what can be considered the base case in which we have a rendition in the sphere. 

Lemma 7.5. Let d, k, s be positive integers with s being even. Let G be a graph with a sphere-rendition ρ such that any two vortices of ρ lie at distance at least sd + 3 and let c1, . . . , c k

be the vortices of ρ. Further, let there exists a disc ∆0 such that there exists a flat (2 sd )-wall W

in ρ whose trace is contained in ∆0 and ∆0 is disjoint from all vortices of ρ.Then there exist k sets of sd /2 tight, concentric cycles C1, . . . , Ck, with ∆i being the closed disc bounded by the trace of the inner cycle of Ci in ρ for each i ∈ [k], such that ci ⊆ ∆i and (Ci, c i)

and (Cj , c j ) are stacked for all distinct i, j ∈ [k].

Proof. Due to the presence of W, we can definitely find at least k sets of s′ := sd /2 concentric cycles C1, . . . , Ck, with ∆i defined as in our statement, in ρ such that ci ⊆ ∆i. In particular, we may choose all of these sets such that they are tight in ρ. For each i ∈ [k] let Ci = ⟨Ci

> 1

, . . . , C is′ ⟩,

which Ci 

> 1

being the innermost cycle of Ci.Suppose that there exist distinct i, j ∈ [k] and hi, h j ∈ [s′] such that V (Cihi ) ∩ V (Cjhj )̸ = ∅. Then let v ∈ V (Cihi ) ∩ V (Cjhj ) be a node of ρ and note that the tightness of both Ci and Cj implies that there exist ρ-aligned curves γi, γ j such that for both x ∈ { i, j } the curve γi ends in v and Nρ(cx)

and intersects at most hx + 1 nodes of ρ. Thus γi ∪ γj contain a ρ-aligned curve intersecting at most hi + hj + 2 ≤ 2sd /2 + 2 nodes of ρ and thus contradict the fact ci and cj lie at distance at least sd + 3 in ρ.Therefore, for any two distinct i, j ∈ [k] we have V (Ci) ∩ V (Cj ) = ∅. The fact that all our sets are now pairwise stacked then follows directly from standard considerations for the topology of the sphere. 103 Now that we have proven this base case, we note that the following result due to Mohar and Thomassen lifts the above lemma to graphs with a rendition in a surface of positive genus and high representativity. 

Proposition 7.6 (Mohar and Thomassen [MT01]) . Let d be a positive integer. Let G be a graph with an embedding ψ in a surface of positive genus and representativity at least 2d + 2 .Then for every face F of ψ there exists a set of d cycles C1, . . . , C d in G such that the Cd-disc ∆

contains f and C1, . . . , C d−1 are concentric in ∆.

Corollary 7.7. Let d, k, s be positive integers with s being even. Let G be a graph with a 

Σ-rendition ρ such that Σ has positive genus, any two vortices of ρ lie at distance at least sd + 3 ,

and ρ has representativity at least sd + 2 , and let c1, . . . , c k be the vortices of ρ.Then there exist k sets of sd /2 tight, concentric cycles C1, . . . , Ck, with ∆i being the closed disc bounded by the trace of the inner cycle of Ci in ρ for each i ∈ [k], such that ci ⊆ ∆i and (Ci, c i)

and (Cj , c j ) are stacked for all distinct i, j ∈ [k].

7.4 Reduction to graphs without apices 

Before we can prove the main result of this section, we need a way to produce a large mesh if our graph has high treewidth. The standard reference for this is the grid theorem [RS86,CC16,CT21]. Since we also exclude a clique as a minor, we can actually use slightly more optimised bounds from [GSTW26]. 

Proposition 7.8 (Gorsky, Stamoulis, Thilikos, and Wiederrecht [GSTW26]) . There exists a function grid 7.8 : N2 → N with grid (t, k ) ∈ O(t2k + t2304 ) such that the following holds. Let k, t be positive integers. Any graph excluding both a (k × k)-grid and Kt as a minor has treewidth at most grid 7.8(t, k ).Having now gathered all of our tools, we are finally ready to gather them up to prove the main theorem of this section which reduces an instance of a graph with a vital linkage that is already excluding a clique-minor of a certain size to an instance which has a rendition with some convenient properties. Most importantly, the rendition is for the entire instance and does not require the deletion of apices. 

Theorem 7.9. There exist functions clique 7.9, breadth 7.9, depth 7.9 : N → N, paths 7.9 : N2 → N and

s + r).Let r, t, s be positive integers. G′ as follows. For each a ∈ A and i ∈ [2] , if uai̸ ∈ N (ρ) there exists a unique c ∈ C(ρ) such that uai ∈ V (σρ(c)) , then we simply add ai and its incident edge to σρ(c). Note that if c is a vortex, this does not increase its depth, as we may simply add ai to one of the bag of its linear decomposition which also includes uai . Thus we may instead assume that uai ∈ N (ρ). In this case we introduce a new cell ca ⊆ Σ that intersects no other cell of ρ (or any of the cells we have added thus far) and intersects N (ρ) exactly in uai . and note that ca is a vortex despite having very short length, since it contains a terminal of our linkage and thus one of the coloured vertices of our annotated graph. G. Furthermore, ρ1 is blank and we have added at most 2|A| “vortices” to ρ1 in comparison to ρ, which means that the breadth of ρ1 is safely within poly (t + b′) and similarly, if we let

> √

apex 3.8(t2, t ) + 1 to find an r1-wall W1 ⊆ W0 contained in a ρ′-aligned disc that is entirely disjoint from all of our vortices, by dividing our wall up into 

apex 3.8(t2, t ) + 1 pairwise disjoint r1-walls, where 

r1 = r + (9 t4(4 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1) + 4) + breadth 7.9(t)(4 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1) + 6) + 2 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1)) .

Finding a rendition with high distance and representativity. Now our goal is to get to an instance of a graph with a vital linkage embedded on some surface of high representativity (or on the sphere) and with high distance between any two vortices. We will accomplish this as follows. First, we increase the distance between any two vortices Lemma 7.3. Then, we check if the representativity of our rendition is high enough and if it is not, we apply Lemma 7.4. However, whilst this reduces the genus of the instance we are interested in, this does introduce up to two more vortices that may now actually be close to some of the existing vortices. Thus, whenever we are forced to apply Lemma 7.4, we return to the first step and iterate. Since Lemma 7.4 decreases the genus of the surface we work on, this will terminate. In total, Lemma 7.4 will produce a number of vortices proportional to the genus of Σ and Lemma 7.3 decreases the number of vortices by one with each application whilst only adding at most 2 to the depth of the rendition. Thus, if b1 is the breadth of ρ1 and g is the genus of Σ, we will at worst generate b2 := b1 + 2 g vortices in this process. In particular, since each application of Lemma 7.3 fuses two of these vortices whose depth simply gets added together with 2, the final depth of our vortices is at most d2 := ( d + 2)( b2 − 1) + 2 g, since Lemma 7.3 can only be applied at most b2 − 1 times and Lemma 7.4 can only applied g times. Let us now be more precise about the numbers with which we apply both of these lemmas. First, consider Lemma 7.3, for which we try to ask for distance 2s(d2 + 1) + 3 between the vortices of the current rendition. Any modification that results from this lemma does not change the surface, results in a vital linkage with at most 4s(d2 + 1) + 6 new endpoints (and thus the annotated graph created by taking these endpoints as the red vertices still has bounded bidimensionality), 106 all of these endpoints are found in vortices, and outside of the addition of the new fused vortex the rendition is not changed. Thus, if the old rendition contained a flat wall of order w′, our new rendition preserves the existence of a (w′ − (4 s(d2 + 1) + 6)) -wall. On the other hand, we apply Lemma 7.4 asking for representativity 2s(d2 + 1) + 2 . If this cuts apart our surface into two components, we choose the w′-wall in our original instance to orient ourselves, since the new instance still contains a flat (w′ − (4 s(d2 + 1) + 4)) -mesh which is draw on either of the two new surfaces. This allows us to choose the surface containing the remains of our flat wall. We then note that this also creates a vital linkage with at most 2s(d2 + 1) + 2 + 6 d2

endpoints (again ensuring that the bidimensionality of the corresponding annotated graph is still bounded), all of which are found in vortices. Furthermore, both the breadth and the depth of our rendition increases at most by 2. Thus, after this process we will arrive at a graph G2 containing a vital linkage L2 with a 

Σ2-rendition ρ2 such that i) the genus of Σ2 is at most the genus of Σ,

ii) |L 2| ≤ |L 1| + g(2 s(d2 + 1) + 2) + b2(2 s(d2 + 1) + 2 + 6 d2),

iii) all endpoints of L2 are found in vortices of ρ2,

iv) the depth of ρ2 is at most d + d2,

v) the breadth of ρ2 is at most b2,

vi) there exists a flat (r1 − (g(4 s(d2 + 1) + 4) + b2(4 s(d2 + 1) + 6))) -wall W2 in ρ2,

vii) the vortices of ρ2 pairwise lie at distance at least 2s(d2 + 1) + 3 , and viii) if Σ2 has positive genus, the representativity of ρ2 is at least 2s(d2 + 1) + 2 .From this we can easily derive an annotated graph (G2, R 2) by letting R2 be the set of endpoints of the paths in L2.

Building nests and making the rendition blank. By simply applying Lemma 7.5 or Corollary 7.7, we can now find the desired pairwise disjoint nests of order s(d2 + 1) around the vortices of 

ρ2. These nests may intersect W2, but this at worst causes us to lose the outermost 2s(d2 + 1) 

horizontal and vertical paths of W2 and thus we can find an r2-wall W3 ⊆ W2, with r2 =(r1 − (g(4 s(d2 + 1) + 4) + b2(4 s(d2 + 1) + 6))) − 2s(d2 + 1) , whose perimeter is disjoint from the trace of all cycles of all nests we have just constructed. We note that 

r1 − (9 t4(4 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1) + 4) + breadth 7.9(t)(4 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1) + 6) + 2 s(depth 7.9(t) + 1)) ≥ r. 

Finally, we still have to turn ρ2 into a blank rendition of (G2, R 2). Note that the only problem here is that some vertices of R2 may be placed on the boundary of one of our vortices. Thus for each v ∈ R2 with Nρ2 (c) for some vortex c of ρ2, we create a copy v′̸ ∈ V (G2), make v adjacent to v′ and add v′ to the vortex c. Note that this does not increase the depth of c and thus does not increase the depth of ρ2. Furthermore, we then elongate the path in L2 that uses v by adding v′ and the edge vv ′ to it and replacing v in R2 with v′. This clearly preserves the vitality of the linkage. Repeating this for every vertex in R2 yields a blank rendition of a graph with a vital linkage having the desired properties. This completes our proof. 107 8 What to do with a clique? 

One of the most fundamental parts of our proof and the Graph Minor Algorithm we will present later is the problem of dealing with the existence of large clique-minors in our graph, since we would really like them to be absent to apply the tools from Graph Minor Structure Theory. Luckily there is a well-established set of tools which allow us to see that these cliques are actually quite helpful for us. This gives us permission to focus on graphs that exclude a clique-minor. The following is (6.1) from [RS95], where we note that the original statement confusingly discusses digraphs . However the proof by Robertson and Seymour in particular shows the following statement for undirected graphs. To get the algorithmic part of the statement derives from algorithm (6.2) in [RS95]. 

Proposition 8.1 (Robertson and Seymour [RS95]) . Let G be a graph, let ℓ, d ∈ N be non-negative integers, and let Z ⊆ V (G) with |Z| ≤ ℓ. Further let k = ⌊5/2 · ℓ⌋ + 3 d2 + 1 , let φ be a minor model of Kk in G, and let (A, B ) be a separation in G such that i) A ∩ φ(v) = ∅ for some v ∈ V (Kk),

ii) Z ⊆ A, 

iii) subject to item i) and item ii), (A, B ) is of minimum order, and iv) subject to item i), item ii), and item iii), A is maximal. Then for any v ∈ B \ A, v is irrelevant to the d-folio of G relative to Z.In particular, given G, Z, and φ as above, such a vertex v can be found in poly (ℓ + d)|| H|| -time. We will also require the following result from [PTW25] which refines some of the tools from [RS95]. 

Proposition 8.2 (Protopapas, Thilikos, and Wiederrecht [PTW25]) . Let q, t, k ∈ N be positive integers with k ≥ ⌊ 3/2 · qt ⌋ + t. Let (G, χ ) be a q-colorful graph such that G contains a minor model φ of Kk. Then one of the following is true: i) There exists a colorful minor model ψ of a rainbow Kt in (G, χ ) such that Tψ is a truncation of Tφ, or ii) There exists a set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most qt − 1 such that the Tφ-big component of 

G − S is restricted. Furthermore, there exists an algorithm that takes as input t, (G, χ ), and φ as above and finds one of the two outcomes in time poly (q + t)|| G|| .We combine these two results into a simple lemma that will later on allow us to efficiently find irrelevant vertices for the folio-problem in Section 10. 

Lemma 8.3. Let k, b, d ∈ N be positive integers, let (G, Z ) be an annotated graph with bidimensionality less than b and |Z| ≤ k, and let φ be a Kt-minor-model in G with t ≥⌊5/2⌋b2 + 3 d2 + 1 .Then G contains an irrelevant vertex for the (k, d )-folio of (G, Z ), which can be identified in 

poly (k + b + d)|G|-time. 

Proof. We view (G, Z ) as a colorful graph (G, χ ) with one colour which is assigned by χ exactly to the vertices in Z. If there existed a colorful minor model ψ of a rainbow Kb2 in (G, χ ),

then this would directly contradict bidim (G, Z ) < b . Thus, as t ≥ ⌊ 5/2⌋b2 + 1 ≥ ⌊ 3/2 · b2⌋ + b2,

108 Proposition 8.1 tells us that there exists a set S ⊆ V (G) of order b2 − 1 such that the Tφ-big component of G − S is restricted and thus contains no terminals from Z.In particular, this means that there exists a separation (A, B ) in G with A ∩ B = S such that 

A ∩ φ(v) = ∅ for some V (Kt) and we have Z ⊆ A. This allows us to apply Proposition 8.1, which confirms that any vertex in B is irrelevant, completing our proof. Of course to do something with a clique-minor, we first need to find one. To do this efficiently, we take a tool from Reed and Wood [RW09], who state that their algorithm actually takes a running time dependent on both the number of vertices and the number of edges of G. However as Kawarabayashi, Kobayashi, and Reed [KKR12] point out, by only considering 2t−3|G| edges of the graph, this algorithm ends up finding the clique-minor in a running time only depending on |G| and the size of the clique we search for. 

Proposition 8.4 (Reed and Wood [RW09]) . Let t be a positive integer and let G be a graph with || G|| ≥ 2t−3|G|. Then one can find a Kt-minor model in G in O(t|G|)-time. 

# 9 The Vital Linkage Theorem 

In the previous sections we have shown that  

> •

graphs with large cliques contain irrelevant vertices for vital linkages (see Section 8),  

> •

graphs with high treewidth and excluding a clique-minor yields a graph with a well-structured rendition without apices (see Section 7),  

> •

graphs hosting a vital linkage that have a well-structured rendition without apices can be reduce down until they no longer have vortices without introducing many terminals (see Section 6), and  

> •

graphs containing a vital linkage embedded on a surface with bounded genus and a bounded number of holes hosting terminals of the linkage cannot have large treewidth (see Section 4.2). We will now apply these insights in the given order to prove our first main theorem, which gives the first explicit bound for the Linkage Function, showing that it is polynomial in the number of terminals and singly exponential only in the bidimensionality of these terminals. 

Theorem 9.1. There exists a function β9.1 : N2 → N such that for every annotated graph 

(G, T ) with bidim (G, T ) ≤ b, if there exists a vital T -linkage of order at most k in G, then 

tw (G) ≤ β9.1(k, b ). Moreover, β9.1(k, b ) ∈ 2poly (b) · poly (k).

Proof. For future use, we let 

r := 2 cycles 6.29 (b + 2 paths 7.9(b, breadth 7.9(b)) , depth 7.9(b),f4.10 (breadth 7.9(b) + 9 b4, terminals 6.29 (k

+ 2 paths 7.9(b, breadth 7.9(b)) , breadth 7.9(b), depth 7.9(b), 9b4))) .

We will prove this theorem by setting β9.1(k, b ) := wall 7.9(b, breadth 7.9(b), r ). Let (G0, T 0) be a counterexample to our statement, meaning that there exists a vital T0-linkage of order at most 

k in G0, we have bidim (G0, T 0) ≤ b, and tw (G0) > β 9.1(k, b ).109 We now first apply Lemma 8.3 to exclude the presence of a Kt-minor in G0 with t = ⌊5/2⌋b2 + 1 ,

whilst noting that the linkage problem is simply the folio problem with detail 0. This follows, since the presence of an irrelevant vertex for the linkage would imply that G0 is in fact not a minimal counterexample. Together with the large treewidth of G0, this allows us to now apply Theorem 7.9, which yields an annotated graph (G1, T 1), a vital T1-linkage L1 in G1, and a blank, stretched Σ1-rendition ρ1 with breadth b1 ≤ breadth 7.9(b) and depth at most depth 7.9(b) on a surface Σ1 of genus at most 9b4, such that i) bidim (G1, T 1) ≤ b + 2 paths 7.9(b, breadth 7.9(b)) and |L 1| ≤ k + paths 7.9(b, breadth 7.9(b)) ,

which confirms the bound claimed in our statement as b ≤ k.

# 10 Tackling the Folio Problem 

The previous sections were all concerned almost entirely with proving Theorem 9.1. However, establishing good bounds for the Linkage Function is merely the first step towards reigning in the galactic parametric dependencies in the running time of the Graph Minor Algorithm. Indeed, in order to prove Theorem 2.2 we need to prove Theorem 2.5 which is the statement that guarantees the existence of an irrelevant vertex in all instances of large enough treewidth. In this section and the following one we take on the task to deal with the algorithmic consequences of Theorem 9.1 for the (k, d )-Folio problem. The main goal of this section is to show that within a large “homogeneous” flat wall W avoiding the set R of annotated vertices, it is always possible to redraw a minor model invading the compass of W in a way such that it avoids the centre of the wall. 

10.1 Quickly combing an annulus 

A key result in the algorithm theory of graph minors is the so-called “Annulus Combing Lemma” of Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [GST23a]. This result is a slight strengthening of similar lemmas originally proven by Robertson and Seymour [RS12] and essentially says the following: Given a Σ-rendition ρ of a graph together with some disc ∆ ⊆ Σ such that the restriction of ρ

to ∆ is a vortex-free rendition containing a large grounded cylindrical wall W together with a pattern of order k, say Π, in G and a Π-linkage L, then there exists a still big cylindrical subwall 

W ′ of W, another Π-linkage L′ and a subset R of radial paths in W ′ such that the intersection of L′ with the compass of W ′ is a subset of the paths in R. Here, the compass of a cylindrical wall W grounded in ρ is the union of all graphs σ(c) over the cells c contained in the annulus defined by the traces of the outer- and inner-most cycle of W . Similarly, the compass of a wall 

W ∗ grounded in ρ is the union of all graphs σ(c) over the set of all cells c contained in the disc defined by the trace of the perimeter of W ∗. Notice that, whenever we have a graph G with an embedding Γ in some surface Σ, one may obtain a trivial Σ-rendition of G from Γ by defining, for each edge xy ∈ E(G) a cell cxy obtained by choosing an ε-disc ∆xy around the drawing of 

xy with ε > 0 such that x, y ∈ bd (∆ xy ), ∆xy intersects Γ only in x, y, and xy, and the curve 

xy \ { x, y } is contained in the interior of ∆xy .The notions of “large” and “big” in the statement above depend on k and the Linkage Function from Theorem 9.1. In fact, we prove a slightly more general statement for which we require some further definitions. 111 Figure 20: A railed annulus with 5 circles and 7 rails. 

A partially embedded railed annulus. Let w, r ≥ 0 be two integers. A (w, r )-railed annulus is a graph Aw,r such that there exist pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles C1, . . . , C w, called the circles ,and pairwise vertex-disjoint paths R1, . . . R r, called the rails , such that i) Aw,r = ⋃ 

> i∈[w]

Ci ∪ ⋃ 

> i∈[r]

Ri is a planar graph, ii) Ri has one endpoint on C1, the other on Cw for all i ∈ [r], and is otherwise disjoint from 

C1 ∪ Cw,

iii) for all i ∈ [r], j ∈ [w], R i ∩ Cj is a path, and iv) for each i ∈ [r], R i visits the cycles C1, . . . , C w in the order listed when traversing along 

Ri starting from its endpoint on C1.We call C1 and Cw the boundary circles of Aw,r . We say that a graph A is a railed annulus if there exist r and w such that A is a (w, r )-railed annulus. See Figure 20 for an example. Now let Σ be a surface and G be a graph with a Σ-rendition ρ. We say that a railed annulus 

A ⊆ G is separating in ρ if i) A is grounded in ρ, 

ii) if A has at least two circles and at least two rails, then there exists an annulus ⊚ ⊆ Σ such that the traces of the boundary circles of A coincide with the two boundary components of ⊚, and the trace of every rail of A is contained in ⊚,

iii) ⊚ is disjoint from the closures of the vortices of ρ, and iv) for every circle C of A, the trace of C is a separating curve in Σ.See Figure 21 for some examples. We call the annulus ⊚ above the domain of A in ρ. The crop of G by ⊚ is the union of all graphs σ(c) whose cell c of ρ is contained in ⊚. Finally, if (G, T ) is an annotated graph with a Σ-rendition ρ and a separating railed annulus A with domain ⊚, we say that A is blank if the crop of G by ⊚ does not contain any vertices from T .Let p ≥ 0 and q ≥ 2 be integers. Let A = A2p+q,r be a railed annulus. The p-trimming of A is the railed annulus Aq,r obtained from A by removing the first and last p circles and restricting the rails to their minimal Cp+1 -Cp+q-subpaths. 112 Figure 21: Two separating annuli in different surfaces. The separating annulus on the left bounds a disc in its interior, while the separating annulus on the right is genus-reducing for its surface. In this paper we only care about the situation on the left, however, Theorem 10.1 holds also in the situation on the right. 

Combed linkages. Let Σ be a surface, G be a graph with a Σ-rendition ρ, and let A ⊆ G be a separating railed annulus in G with domain ⊚ in ρ. Let H be the crop of G by ⊚. A linkage L

in G is combed in A if the intersection of ⋃ L and H is a subgraph of the union of the rails of A.The main theorem of this subsection now reads as follows. 

Theorem 10.1. There exist functions f10 .1, h 10 .1, g 10 .1 : N2 → N such that for every annotated graph (G, T ) with bidim (G, T ) ≤ b, every surface Σ, and every Σ-rendition ρ of G, the following holds: If A = Aa,r is a blank and separating railed annulus with a ≥ f10 .1(k, b ) and r ≥ g10 .1(k, b ) in G

and ρ and L is a T -linkage of order at most k, then there exists a T -linkage L⋆ in G such that i) τ (L) = τ (L⋆),

ii) L⋆ is combed in the h10 .1(k, b )-trimming of A, and iii) if ⊚ is the domain of A and G′ is the crop of G by ⊚, then ⋃ L⋆ − G′ ⊆ ⋃ L − G′.Moreover, f10 .1(k, b ), h 10 .1(k, b ), g 10 .1(k, b ) ∈ 2poly (b) · poly (k).We will need the following classic theorem for lower bounds on the treewidth of graphs (see for example [ST93, RS12]). Let G be a graph. A bramble in G is a collection of connected subgraphs B of G such that for all B1, B 2 ∈ B either V (B1) ∩ V (B2)̸ = ∅ or there exists an edge with one end in B1 and the other in B2. A hitting set for a bramble B is a set S ⊆ V (G) such that S ∩ V (B)̸ = ∅ for all 

B ∈ B . The order of a bramble B is the minimum size of a hitting set for B.

Proposition 10.2 (Reed [Ree97]) . Let k ≥ 0 be an integer and G be a graph. If G has a bramble of order k + 1 , then tw (G) ≥ k.113 Proof of Theorem 10.1. Let us start by defining our functions, to this end let β be the Linkage Function from Theorem 9.1. We set 

f10 .1(k, b ) := 16 β(k, b ) + 16 

h10 .1(k, b ) := 8 β(k, b ) + 8 , and 

g10 .1(k, b ) := 6 β(k, b ) + 6 .

The claimed bounds now follow directly from Theorem 9.1. Let C = ⟨C1, . . . , C a⟩ be the circles of A and let G′′′ := ⋃ L ∪ ⋃ C. In the following we assume that L is chosen to minimise |E(L) \ E(C)| among all linkages Q in G with τ (Q) = τ (L). This is a fair assumption since the assertion allows us to make changes to the original linkage. Let ⊚ be the domain of A in Σ. Let S be the set of all (V (C1) ∪ V (Ca)) -subpaths of the paths in L which are contained in the crop of G by ⊚. A path in S is called a river if it is a 

V (C1)-V (Ca)-path, a mountain if it is a V (C1)-path, and a valley if it is a V (Ca)-path. See Figure 22 for an illustration. Let now G′′ be obtained from G by iteratively picking a vertex v of degree exactly 2 and contracting one of its two incident edges. In a second step, let now G′ be obtained from G′′ 

by contracting every remaining edge which belongs to some circle of A and some path in L.Notice that G′ still contains a sequence C′ = ⟨C′

> 1

> a

contains a linkage L′ such that τ (L′) = τ (L). This is true in particular since the construction step to go from G′′ to G′ only contracted edges that already belonged to L and thus ensured that the paths in L remain paths. Finally, notice that, since G′ is a minor of G, we may inherit a Σ-rendition ρ′ of G′ from the restriction of ρ to G′′′ .

Let T ′ := V (G′) ∩ T .

Claim 10.2.2 . We have that bidim (G′, T ′) ≤ b.

Proof of claim. The claim follows directly from the fact that bidimensionality is monotone under taking red minors and (G′, T ′) is indeed a red minor of (G, T ). ⊣

We say that two rivers J1 and J2 are consecutive , if deleting their traces from ⊚ leaves one component ζ′, whose closure is homeomorphic to a disc, that does not intersect the trace of any other river in S. Note that we may define what it means for two rails of A′ to be consecutive in the same way. We call the closure ζ = cl (ζ′) of such a disc a slice of ⊚. Notice that the rivers in 

S decompose ⊚ into |S| slices ζ1, . . . , ζ ℓ where ℓ is the number of rivers in S. Moreover, for every mountain or valley P in S there exists a unique i ∈ [ℓ] such that the trace of P is contained in 

ζi. For each i ∈ [ℓ] let us denote by Mi the set of all mountains whose trace is contained in ζi

and by Vi the set of all valleys whose trace is contained in ζi.In what follows, we wish to argue that rivers and valleys cannot enter deeply into the circles. For this, the goal is to apply – among others – Lemma 4.2 which requires us to confine the curves of mountains and valleys into wells. Since the arguments for dealing with valleys is analogous to those for mountains, we only discuss the mountain case below. 

Draining a well in a mountain. Fix any i ∈ [ℓ]. Let Gi be the graph ⋃ Mi ∪ C ′. Then Gi

is a planar graph with a plane embedding Γi inherited from ρ through Theorem 6.5 and we may declare Ωi to be the cyclic ordering of the vertices of C′ 

> 1

> 1

in clockwise order. Then (Gi, Ωi, ρ, C′, Mi) is a well and by Lemma 4.2 we may assume this well to be drained. We call (Gi, Ωi, ρ, C′, Mi) the well associated with Mi. This is, because the new collection of paths returned by Lemma 4.2 must still have all of its traces contained in ζi and is therefore guaranteed to stay disjoint from the rivers of S.In particular, any mountain in Mi encloses a disc with the boundary of ζi which cannot contain the trace of any valley from Vi. This observation allows us to apply the same treatment to the valleys and assume that their respective wells are also drained.

10.2 Redrawing topological minors models in partially disc-embedded graphs 

With Theorem 10.1 we now have a way to redraw a linkage onto the rails of some subannulus. Next we need to show that this can be applied in a broader setting as follows: Suppose some rooted graph (H, J ) is a rooted minor of our rooted graph (G, R) and part of G is embedded in a disc ∆ containing, in particular, a large flat subwall of G. Inside of this subwall we may find a big railed annulus A separating an interior area of ∆ from everything of G not drawn into ∆.From here, the plan is to proceed in two steps. First, redraw the minor model of (H, R) such that it intersects the domain of A only in few rails and nowhere else. Second, use A to crop the interior part of the minor model and then redraw it within A itself, thereby avoiding the centre of the wall. 

10.3 Combing a topological minor 

Our first target is a generalisation of Theorem 10.1 to work also with fixed topological minors and not just with vertex-disjoint paths. As we are going to work with topological minors throughout most of this section, we now spend some time defining the necessary notions. 

Topological minors. We say that an annotated graph (J, T ) is a tm-pair if all vertices in 

V (J) \ T have degree precisely 2 in J and no component of J − T is a path. Given a graph G

and a vertex v ∈ V (G) of degree 2, the operation of dissolving v is defined as deleting v and introducing an edge between the two neighbours of v if they were not already adjacent. We denote by Diss (J, T ) the graph obtained from the tm-pair (J, T ) by dissolving all vertices of 

V (J \ T . A tm-pair of a graph G is a tm-pair (J, T ) such that J ⊆ G. We now say that a graph 

H is a topological minor of a graph G if there exists a tm-pair (J, T ) in G such that Diss (J, T )

is isomorphic to H. In this case we say that (J, T ) models H and J is a subdivision of H. The vertices of T are called the branch vertices and the paths in J with both endpoints in T but internally vertex-disjoint from T are called the subdivision paths of (J, T ), finally, the vertices in 

V (J) \ T are called the subdivision vertices .For our railed annuli, we already had a glimpse of the idea what a “partial” embedding might look like: In the case of annuli, we only really needed a sphere-rendition of our graph G with two vortices such that the railed annulus is grounded and the trace of each of its circles separates the two vortices in the sphere. Now, we go one step further and focus only on graphs that are “partially” embedded in a disc. 

Partially disc-embedded graphs. Let ∆ be a closed disc. We say that a graph G is ∆-embedded 

(via Γ) if Γ is an embedding of G in ∆ such that the intersection of bd (∆) and Γ is a subset of 

V (G). A partial ∆-embedding of a graph G is a triple (G1, G 2, Γ) where G = G1 ∪ G2, G 1 and G2

intersect in vertices only, and G1 is ∆-embedded via Γ. We say that G1 is the ∆-embedded part 

of G and we usually identify G1 and Γ. When we say that a graph G is partially ∆-embedded , we implicitly assume that G comes with a partial ∆-embedding. A graph G is said to be partially disc embedded if there exists a disc ∆ such that G is partially ∆-embedded .120 In a more general setting, we say that a sphere-rendition ρ of a graph G is a imperfect rendition in a disc if ρ has a unique vortex c0. In such a situation, we refer to the closure of the set obtained from the sphere by deleting c0 as the disc of ρ. We sometimes say that ρ is an imperfect rendition in a disc ∆ to say that ρ is an imperfect rendition in a disc with disc ∆. Notice that every partial ∆-embedding of some graph G gives rise to an imperfect rendition of G in a disc. 

A railed annulus in a disc. Let G be a graph with an imperfect rendition in a disc, ∆ be the disc of ρ, and A ⊆ G be a separating railed annulus in G with domain ⊚ ⊆ ∆. In such a situation we assume that the circles of A are numbered as C1, . . . , C s such that for all i < j ∈ [r],

the Ci-disc ∆i is contained in the Cj -disc ∆j .Now let b ≥ 0 be an integer and let (G, R ) be an annotated graph and ρ be an imperfect rendition in a disc ∆ for G, a set X ⊆ V (G) with R ⊆ X is split in ∆ by a railed annulus A with domain 

⊚, if i) A is blank, ii) there exists a set D ⊆ R such that, if G′ is the crop of G by ∆, then D = G′ ∩ R, and iii) if ∆′ ⊆ ∆ is the unique component of ∆ \ ⊚ which is homeomorphic to a disc, then D is a subset of the vertices of the crop of G by ∆′.We say that the set D is the set of trapped terminals .

Controlling topological minors. Let (J, T ) be a tm-pair in an annotated graph (G, R ). The 

detail of (J, T ), denoted by detail R(J, T ), is the integer d := |T \ R|. Let ρ be an imperfect rendition in a disc ∆ for G, then (J, T ) is carefully split in ∆ by a railed annulus A if (G, R ∪ T )

is split in ∆ where the set of trapped terminals is a subset of T \ R.Let now (J, T ) be a tm-pair in (G, R ), ρ be an imperfect rendition in a disc ∆ for G, and A be a railed annulus in G. We say that A confines (J, T ) if i) (J, T ) is carefully split by A, and ii) if ⊚ is the domain of A in ∆ and G′ is the crop of G by ⊚, then J ∩ G′ is a collection of subpaths of the rails of A.See Figure 25 for an illustration of these definitions. What follows is an analogue of the so-called “Model Combing Lemma” by Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [GST23b]. This may be seen as a strengthening of Theorem 10.1 where we replace the linkage with a topological minor model. 

Lemma 10.3. There exist functions f10 .3, g 10 .3, h 10 .3N3 → N such that for all integers k, b, d ∈ N,

graphs H, and annotated graphs (G, R ), if

carefully splits (J, T ).Let A′ be the h10 .3(k, b, d )-trimming of A. Then there exists a tm-pair (J′, T ′) modelling H in 

G such that, 121 Figure 25: A sketch of a tm-pair (J, T ) with terminals in red – those are the ones belonging to 

R – and magenta with a partial rendition in a disc ∆ depicted in gray. The magenta curves illustrate the paths of J between the vertices in T . ii) A′ confines (J′, T ′), and iii) if ⊚ is the domain of A and G′ is the crop of G by ⊚, then J − G′ ⊆ J′ − G′.Moreover, f10 .3(k, b, d ), g 10 .3(k, b, d ), h 10 .3(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k).Before we dive into the proof, we need a small auxiliary lemma on the bidimensionality of vertices in partially disc-embedded graphs. Let G be a graph v ∈ V (G) be a vertex and F ⊆ E(G) be a set of edges incident with v. The 

F -splitting of v in G is the graph Gv obtained from G by first subdividing each edge e ∈ F with a new vertex xe and then deleting v. Similarly, if S ⊆ V (G) is a set of vertices and F ⊆ E(G) is a set of edges such that each edge in F is incident with some vertex of S, the F -splitting of S

in G is the graph obtained from G by iteratively splitting the vertices in S at their respective edges in F . In case some edge e in F is incident with two vertices of S, this edge will receive two subdivision vertices. That is, after splitting the first endpoint u of e = uv in the process above and introducing the subdivision vertex xe, the edge xev must be added to F, replacing e.We call the set X of all newly introduced vertices xe for the F -splitting of S in G the residue of 

S and we call the elements of X the residual vertices . See Figure 26 for an illustration. 

Lemma 10.4. Let ∆ be a closed disc, (G, R ) be an annotated graph and (G1, G 2, Γ) be a partial ∆-embedding of G. Let S ⊆ V (G1) \ R be a set of vertices not drawn on bd (∆) and let F ⊆ E(G)1 be a set of edges such that each edge in F is incident with some vertex of 

S. Finally, let G′ be the F -splitting of S in G and let X be the residue of S in G′. Then, 

bidim (G′, R ∪ X) ≤ bidim (G, R ∪ X) + 2 ≤ bidim (G, R ) + |X| + 2 .122 (i) (ii) 

Figure 26: (i) A graph G with an edge set F – depicted in blue – and vertex set S – depicted in magenta. (ii) The F -splitting of G at S where the residue of S is depicted in yellow. 

Proof. Let s ∈ S be any vertex and let x1, . . . , x p be the residual vertices introduced when we split at s in the creating of G′. Notice that we inherit a natural partial ∆-embedding (G′

> 1

, G ′

> 2

, Γ′)

from (G1, G 2, Γ) where G2 = G′ 

> 2

and G′ 

> 1

is the F -splitting of S in G1. Then all of x1, . . . , x p sit on a common face in ∆. It follows that, if (H, Y ) is a red-minor of (G′, R ∪X) where H is planar and 

φ is a red minor-model of (H, Y ) in G′, if P = {v ∈ V (H) : there exists i ∈ [p] s.t. xi ∈ φ(v)},

and ΓH is the drawing of H obtained from G′, then there exists a face of ΓH which contains all of P . Hence, in any red grid-minor J of (G′, R ∪ X) there must exist a facial cycle of J

containing all involved vertices among the xi. Thus, the number of 4-cycles in any red grid-minor of (G′, R ∪ X) may exceed the maximum number of 4-cycles in a red grid-minor of (G, R ) by at most |S|. Notice that any grid has at most one facial cycle of length more than 4 – its “outer cycle” – and deleting this outer cycle reduces the order of the grid by 2. So after removing the outer cycle of any red grid-minor in (G′, R ∪ X), the total number of 4-cycles remaining cannot exceed bidim (G, R )2 by more than S. Thus, bidim (G′, R ∪ X) ≤ bidim (G, R ) + |S| + 2 as desired. 

Proof of Lemma 10.3. We first fix our functions as follows: 

f10 .3(k, b, d ) := f10 .1(k + ( k + d)2, b + d + 2) ,g10 .3(k, b, d ) := g10 .1(k + ( k + d)2, b + d + 2) , and 

h10 .3(k, b, d ) := h10 .1(k + ( k + d)2, b + d + 2) .

With Lemma 10.4 we are now able to simply reduce the claim to an application of Theorem 10.1. For this recall that (J, T ) is a tm-pair in G modelling a graph H, we have that detail (J, T ) ≤ d, 

(G1, G 2, Γ) is a partial ∆-embedding of G, and A is a railed annulus in G with s ≥ f10 .3(k, b, d )

circles and r ≥ g10 .3(k, b, d ) rails. Let now D be the set of trapped terminals for (G, R ∪ T ), A, and (G1, G 2, Γ) . Then D ⊆ T \ R

by definition. Moreover, since the vertices in D are trapped (by A) it follows that no vertex of 123 D is drawn on bd (∆) . Let now F ⊆ E(G) ∩ J be the set of all edges of J that are incident with some vertex in D. It follows that |F | ≤ (k + d)2.Now consider the F -splitting G′ of D in G and let X be the set of residual vertices in G′. Notice that (J, T ) naturally defines a pattern of order |E(H)| in G by assigning to each subdivision path in J its two endpoints as terminals. Similarly, in G′, we may define a pattern of order 

|E(H)| on the set X ∪ (T \ D) which is realised by the linkage L consisting of the paths in 

G′ obtained from subdivision paths of (J, T ) by replacing, in each subdivision path P, the endpoints of P that belong to D with the respective residual vertices. Finally, we may observe that bidim (G′, R ∪ (T \ D) ∪ X) ≤ bidim (G, R ∪ (T \ D)) + |D| + 2 by Lemma 10.4. Moreover, by Observation 2.1 we have that 

bidim (G, R ∪ (T \ D)) + |D| + 2 ≤ bidim (G, R ) + |T \ D| + |D| + 2 ≤ b + d + 2 .

Now, we apply Theorem 10.1 to (G′, R ∪ (T \ D) ∪ X) and L and A. This yields a linkage L′ in 

G′ with τ (L) = τ (L′) such that L′ is combed in the h(k, b, d )-trimming A′ of A. Moreover, if ⊚

is the domain of A, and G′′ is the crop of G′ by ⊚, then ⋃ L′ − G′′ ⊆ ⋃ L − G′′ . Notice that, if we let L′′ be the collection of paths obtained from the paths in L′ by replacing each vertex of X

with the corresponding vertex of D, then L′′ is a collection of internally vertex-disjoint paths in G with all endpoints in T . Indeed, it follows from the construction of G′, that (⋃ L′′ , T ) is indeed a tm-pair in G modelling H and our proof is complete. Notice that, if one was not interested in the bounds on the functions involved or in the explicit dependency on the bidimensionality, Lemma 10.3 is precisely the same as the Model Combing Lemma of Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos (Theorem 6 in [GST23b]). Indeed, when considering the bounds abstractly, the main difference is that our proof depends only linearly on the Linkage Function, while the original proof had a quadratic dependency. This difference is only visible in the proof though as this subtle difference in the degrees is hidden in the poly -notation. Our main application of Lemma 10.3 is to argue that another redrawing-type theorem from the literature – this time due to Baste, Sau, and Thilikos [BST23] – is applicable in our setting while maintaining the close-to-optimal bounds to the Linkage Function guaranteed by Theorem 9.1. Before we continue, we need to introduce a hand full of additional definitions in order to state the result of Baste et al. 

Contracting respectfully. Let G and H be graphs and Q ⊆ V (H) be a set of vertices. We say that a map ϕ : V (H) → 2V (G) is a Q-respecting contraction-mapping of H to G if    

> •⋃
> x∈V(H)

ϕ(x) = V (G), 

> •

for all x̸ = y ∈ V (H) it holds that ϕ(x) ∩ ϕ(y) = ∅, 

> •

for each x ∈ V (H), ϕ (x) is connected,  

> •

for each xy ∈ E(H), ϕ (x) ∪ ϕ(y) is connected, and  

> •

for each x ∈ Q it holds that |ϕ(x)| = 1 .One may interpret the idea behind Q-respectful contraction-mappings as an interpolation between topological minors and minors. For some special vertices – namely those in Q – we do not allow any form of further contraction, but for those outside Q normal minor rules apply. 

Dispersing vertices in a wall. Let G be a planar graph with an embedding Γ in a disc ∆.The outer face of Γ is the unique face that contains bd (∆) . Given two vertices x and y of G, 

124 we define their face-distance in Γ to be the smallest integer d such that there exists a curve γ

between x and y in ∆ such that γ is internally disjoint from the outer face of Γ, no internal point of γ is a vertex of Γ, and γ intersects at most d faces of Γ.We say that a graph G is plane if G comes identified with an embedding Γ of G in a disc ∆.In case G is a plane graph, the face distance between two of its vertices is the face distance between those vertices in Γ. Given two vertex sets X, Y ⊆ V (G), the face distance between X

and Y is the minimum integer d such that there are x ∈ X and y ∈ Y with face distance at most d. Let H1, H 2 be two subgraphs of G. The face distance of H1 and H2 is the face distance of V (H1) and V (H2).Given an integer d ∈ N and a vertex x ∈ V (G), we denote by FdG(x) the set of all vertices of G

that are within face distance at most d of x.Let G be a plane graph, s ∈ N, and a tm-pair (J, T ) of G, we say that (J, T ) is safely s-dispersed 

in G if i) any two distinct vertices t, t ∈ T are within face distance at least 2s + 1 in G, and ii) for every t ∈ T with deg J (t) = d, the graph J[FsG(t) ∩ V (J)] consists of d paths with t as a unique common endpoint. 

Layers and central subwalls. Let r ≥ 3 be an integer and W be an r-wall. Notice that W may be understood as an (r × r)-mesh with a perimeter. We define the layers of W recursively as follows: The first layer of W is its perimeter. For i ∈ [2 , ⌊r/2⌋], the ith layer of W is the (i − 1) th layer of the (r − 1) -wall obtained from W by removing its perimeter and iteratively removing vertices of degree 1.Given an integer q ∈ [3 , r ] such that r − q is even, the q-central subwall of W is the q-wall obtained from W by removing the first r−q/2 layers of W and then iteratively deleting vertices of degree at most 1.Next, we need a lemma that allows us to redraw planar parts of a tm-pair onto an annulus while also guaranteeing big distances between certain branch vertices and maintaining a fixed interface. 

Proposition 10.5 (Baste, Sau, and Thilikos [BST23]) . There exists a function f10 .5 : N3 → N

such that the following holds: Let c, r, r ′, ℓ ∈ N, r ′ ≤ r, let H be a ∆-embedded graph with ℓ + r′ vertices and let Z =
