# 11 An irrelevant vertex for folio 

In this section we finally prove the core statement of the Graph Minor Algorithm, that is Theorem 2.5. Towards this goal we need a number of additional definition and intermediate results. In broad strokes, what is left to do at this point is to first, explaining homogeneous flat walls in the abstract and then describing the set of colours which defines the homogeneity. Second, we explain how Theorem 10.6 may be used to show that any minor model invading deep into a flat homogeneous wall may be redrawn in a way that fully avoids its centre. Third, we require a way to efficiently find a flat wall whose compass has bounded treewidth and which allows us to efficiently compute the colours required in the first and second step. Combining the outcomes of all three steps with the tools from Section 8 for handling clique-minors then finally results in a proof of Theorem 2.5. 

11.1 Homogeneous walls 

In order to describe how minor models may invade the compass of a flat wall, we need to take into account the way such minor models may interact with the graphs contained in the cells of the rendition witnessing the flatness of our wall. Moreover, since most flat walls come under the condition that a small apex set has already been removed, we also need to take into account how the graphs inside the cells can be enhanced through the additional boundary represented by those apex vertices. In particular, to really say that the centre of some flat wall is irrelevant, we must ensure that these interactions with minor models are essentially interchangeable everywhere in the wall. Please note that below we talk about meshes, but since every wall is a mesh and every 2r-mesh contains an r-wall, the two definitions are interchangeable up to a factor of 2.Let H be a subgraph of a graph G. An H-bridge in G is a connected subgraph J of G such that E(J) ∩ E(H) = ∅ and either E(J) consists of a unique edge with both ends in H, or J is constructed from a component C of G − V (H) and the non-empty set of edges F ⊆ E(G) with one end in V (C) and the other in V (H), by taking the union of C, the endpoints of the edges in 

F, and F itself. Notice that the H-bridges induce a partition of E(G) \ E(H). The vertices in 

V (J) ∩ V (H) are called the attachments of B and the set V (J) \ V (H) is called the interior of 

J.

Homogeneous meshes. Let r ≥ 3 be an integer and M be an r-mesh in G and let CP be the perimeter of M . Let C ⊆ M be any cycle of M . The compass of C is the union of C

130 together with all C-bridges in G that are entirely contained in the compass of M . We denote the compass of C with respect to ρ by compass M (C) and we drop the ρ in the subscript if it is understood from the context. The interior of a cycle C ⊆ M, denoted by int M (C) is the graph compass ρ(C) − C. We also drop the M in the subscript if the wall is understood from the context. Recall the definition of q-colorful graphs. We allow for q to be 0. In this case, for every 0-colorful graph (G, χ ) it holds that χ(v) = ∅ for all v ∈ V (G). Moreover, in such a case we do not distinguish between the graph G and the 0-colorful graph (G, χ ).Given an arbitrary q-colorful graph (G, χ ), a set X ⊆ V (G) and a subgraph H ⊆ G, we define 

χ(X) := ⋃

> v∈X

χ(v),

as well as χ(H) := χ(V (H)) . Note that this should not be confused with the chromatic number of H, which is of no concern to us. Finally, we write (H, χ ) for the q-colorful subgraph of G, 

where we implicitly restrict χ to the vertex set of H.Let q be a non-negative integer and (G, χ ) be a q-colorful graph. Let r ≥ 3 be an integer and M

be an r-mesh in G such that M is flat in G witnessed by a sphere-rendition ρ. We say that M

is homogeneous in (G, χ ) if for every cycle C ⊆ M it holds that 

χ(int (C)) = χ(compass (M )) .

We stress that the original notions of homogeneous flat walls were introduced by Robertson and Seymour [RS95, RS12] however, as pointed out by Sau, Stamoulis, and Thilikos, the price of homogeneity [SST20, SST22, SST23] that the exponential price for obtaining a homogeneous wall is a major obstacle along the path towards an efficient graph minor theory. It was shown by Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht [GSW26] that in q-colorful graphs it is possible to find a large homogeneous flat wall within another huge flat wall by paying a price polynomial in q and the order of the target wall. 

Proposition 11.1 (Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht [GSW26]) . There exists a function 

f11 .1 : N2 → N with f (q, k ) ∈ O (q4 · k6) such that for all non-negative integers q and k and every 

q-colorful graph G with a flat f (q, k )-wall W0 there exists a flat k-wall W1 ⊆ W0 such that the tangle of W1 is a truncation of the tangle of W0 and W1 is homogeneous. Moreover, there exists an algorithm that takes as input a colorful graph (G, χ ) and a flat wall 

W0 and computes the flat wall W1 as above in time poly (q + k)|| G|| .The main problem now is to define the colours in a way that allows us to model our desired properties. 

Augmenting cells. Let a, r ∈ N with r ≥ 3. Let G be a graph, A ⊆ V (G) be a set of at most a

vertices, and let W be an r-wall in G that is flat in G − A witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ.We denote by C(W ) the collection of all cells contained in the disc defined by the trace of the perimeter of W which contains all non-perimeter vertices of W which are of degree 3 in W .For each cell c ∈ C(W ) we consider a labelling λc : N (c) → [3] such that the set of labels assigned by λc to N (c) is one of [1] , [2] , or [3] . In addition, we consider a bijection αA : A → [a].131 The labellings λc and αA will be used to fix labels for the boundaries of the annotated graphs 

(G[V (σ(c)) ∪ A], N (c) ∪ A) to translate them into rooted graphs. For this to fully serve out purposes, we require some additional definitions. Let c ∈ C(W ), we define the ordering Ωc := ⟨x1, . . . , x ℓ⟩ with ℓ ≤ 3 of the vertices of N (c) such that the following holds: i) ⟨x1, . . . , x q⟩ is a counter clockwise cyclic ordering of the vertices of N (c) as they appear on the boundary of c in ρ and ii) for each i ∈ [q] we have that λc(xi) = i.Notice that the cyclic ordering above is significant only in the case where |N (c)| = 3 in the sense that ⟨x1, x 2, x 3⟩ remains invariant under shifting, i.e., ⟨x1, x 2, x ⟩) is the same as ⟨x2, x 3, x 1⟩,

but not under inversion, i.e., ⟨x1, x 2, x 3⟩ is not the same as ⟨x3, x 2, x 1⟩. See Figure 28 for an illustration. The second condition, on the other hand, is necessary for us to align the topological information of ρ with the abstract information of the labelling λc which will give rise to our rooted graphs. For each cell c ∈ C(W ) we now fix πc : A ∪ N (c) → [a + |N (c)|] such that πc(x) = αA(x) for all 

x ∈ A and πc(x) − a = λc(x) for all x ∈ N (c). Finally, this allows us to define the augmented cell c as the rooted graph 

Rc := ( G[V (σ(c) ∪ A)] , r 1, . . . , r a+|N (c)|)

Palettes of augmented cells. With each c ∈ C(W ) we now associate a set of graphs. Let d ≥ 0 be an integer. We denote by d-palette (c) the set d-folio (Rc) to which we refer as the 

d-palette of c.Notice that |d-palette (c)| ∈ 2O(( a+d)2). In the following, we will treat the set of all rooted graphs of detail at most d with at most a + 3 roots, i.e., the largest possible choice for a set d-palette (c),

denoted by Folio (d, a + 3) , as the set of colours for our colorful graph. Hence, in the following we will have that q ∈ 2(a+d)2

for our colorful graph. 

A d-folio homogeneous wall. Let a, r ∈ N with r ≥ 3. Let G be a graph A ⊆ V (G) a set of at most a vertices and let W be an r-wall in G that is flat in G − A witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ. We define the (d, A, W, ρ )-folio augmentation of G, denoted by (G(d,A,W,ρ ), ϕ ), as 132 the colorful graph obtained from G by introducing, for each c ∈ C(W ), a vertex xc adjacent to exactly the vertices of N (c), setting ϕ(v) := ∅ for all v ∈ V (G) and ϕ(c) := d-palette (c) for every c ∈ C(W ). Notice that ρ may be extended to a sphere rendition ρ′ of G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A such that ρ is the restriction of ρ′ to G − A ⊆ G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A simply by setting σρ′ := σ(c) + xc for all c ∈ C(W ) and σρ′ (c) := σ(c) for all c ∈ C(ρ) \ C(W ). We call ρ′ the ρ-augmentation for 

G(d,A,W,ρ ).Notice that W is still flat in G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A as witnessed by ρ′.We say that W is (d, A )-folio homogeneous in G if W is homogeneous in (G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A, ϕ ).

A flat wall of small treewidth. Let G be a graph and W ⊆ G be a flat wall in G witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ. The treewidth of W in G is the smallest integer w such that the treewidth of the compass of W in ρ is at most w.

Suspended walls. Let r ≥ 4. Given a flat wall W in a graph G witnessed by a sphere rendition 

ρ, we say that a cell c ∈ C(W ) is an inner cell of W if the second and third layer of W separate 

N (c) from the perimeter of W in G.Let r ≥ 4 be an integer. Let G be a graph and W ⊆ G be an r-wall in G. We say that W is a suspended r-wall – or simply a suspended wall – in G if there exists a sphere-rendition ρ of 

G − A such that W is flat in ρ and for every cell c ∈ C(W ) the following tow properties hold: i) for every non-empty set S ⊆ N (c) there exists a connected subgraph of σ(c) containing all vertices from S and avoiding all vertices from N (c) \ S and ii) there exist |N (c)| vertex-disjoint paths from N (c) to the perimeter of W .We say that ρ witnesses that W is suspended in G.We are now ready to state the main result of this subsection. 

Theorem 11.2. There exists a function f11 .2 : N3 → N such that for all integers r ≥ 3 and 

d ≥ 0 the following holds. Let G be a graph, A ⊆ V (G) with a := |A|, and W0 be a flat 

f11 .2(a, d, r )-wall of in G − A with a sphere rendition ρ witnessing the flatness of W in G − A.Then there exists an r-wall W1 ⊆ W0 such that i) W1 is flat in G − A witnessed by ρ, 

ii) the tangle of W1 in G − A is a truncation of the tangle of W0 in G − A, and iii) W1 is (d, A )-folio homogeneous in G.In particular, if W0 is suspended in G−A witnessed by ρ, then so is W1. Moreover, f11 .2(a, d, r ) ∈

2poly (a+d) · poly (r) and there exists an algorithm that takes as input G, A, W 0, and ρ as above and computes W1 in time 2poly (w+a+d) · poly (r) · (|G| + || G|| ) where w denotes the treewidth of 

W0 in G.

Proof. Let us set q = q(d, a ) := |Folio (d, a + 3) |. We then set f11 .2(a, d, r ) := f11 .1(q, r ) ∈

2poly (d+a) · poly (r).Notice that |C(W0)| ∈ O(|G|). Moreover, ∑ 

> c∈C(W0)

|σ(c)| ∈ O(|G|). It follows, that we may use the algorithm from Proposition 2.4 to compute the (d, A, W, ρ )-folio augmentation (G(d,A,W,ρ ), ϕ )

of G in time 2poly (w+a+d) · | G|. We then apply the algorithm from Proposition 11.1 to the wall W0 in (G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A, ϕ ) to obtain the desired r-wall W1 in (G(d,A,W,ρ ) − A, ϕ ). It is now straightforward to verify that W1 is indeed the desired (d, A )-folio homogeneous wall in G.133 11.2 Avoiding the centre of a homogeneous wall 

We have now almost reached the core proof if this section. Our goal is to show that given a small apex set A in a graph G together with a large enough suspended wall W in G − A that is (d, A )-folio homogeneous in G, any tm-pair with small intrusion to the interior of W can be replaced with an equivalent tm-pair avoiding the compass of the centre of W .Before we can dive into this part of the proof, we need a way to bridge the setting of Theorem 10.6 and the setting of flat walls. This is because a flat wall comes together with a rendition, while Theorem 10.6 requires a partially disc-embedded graph. To deal with this we adopt the solution of Baste, Sau, and Thilikos [BST23] – see Figure 27 for an illustration how tm-pairs may be redrawn to avoid the central part of a homogeneous wall. 

Levellings. Let r ≥ 4 be an integer, G be a graph, and W ⊆ G be a flat r-wall in G as witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ. Let further ∆ denote the disc bounded by the perimeter of W and 

W ′ be the (r − 1) -central subwall of W such that W ′ is grounded in the restriction ρ′ of ρ to ∆.Let further G′ ⊆ G be the crop of G by ∆.We define the (W, ρ )-levelling of G, denoted by Level (G, W, ρ ) as the bipartite graph 

(N (G′) ∪ { vc : c ∈ C(ρ′)}, {xv c : c ∈ C(ρ′) and x ∈ N (c)}).

See Figure 29 for an illustration. That is, the vertex set of Level (G, W, ρ ) consists of the nodes contained in ∆, referred to as the node-vertices , on one side, one fresh vertex vc, called a 

cell-vertex , for each cell contained in ∆ on the other side, and edges xv c between nodes and cell-vertices whenever x is a node on the boundary of c. Notice also that Level (G, W, ρ ) is the incidence graph of the hypergraph with vertex set N (ρ′) and with hyperedges {N (c) : c ∈ C(ρ′)}.Moreover, it is easy to see that G′ has an embedding Γ′ in ∆ such that ∆ intersects Γ′ only in the nodes of W that belong to the perimeter of W .The partial (W, ρ )-levelling of G, denoted by PLevel (G, W, ρ ), is the partially disc-embedded graph with vertex set (V (G) \ V (G′)) ∪ V (Level (G, W, ρ )) obtained as the union of G′ and the graph 

G′′ which is obtained from G by deleting all edges of G′, all vertices of G′ \ perimeter (W ), where 

perimeter (W ) denotes the perimeter of W . It follows that (G′, G ′′ , Γ′) is a partial ∆-embedding of PLevel (G, W, ρ ), we refer to (G′, G ′′ , Γ′) as the natural ∆-embedding of PLevel (G, W, ρ ). The 

node-vertices of PLevel (G, W, ρ ) are the node-vertices of G′, the cell-vertices of PLevel (G, W, ρ )

are the cell-vertices of G′, and the outer-vertices of PLevel (G, W, ρ ) are the vertices of G′′ − G′.A key observation is that the cell-vertices of PLevel (G, W, ρ ) have maximum degree 3.

Observation 11.3. Let r ≥ 4 be an integer, G be a graph, and W ⊆ G be a flat r-wall in G as witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ. Let further ∆ be the disc bounded by the perimeter of 

W that contains all nodes of W and G⋆ := PLevel (G, W, ρ ) be the partial (W, ρ )-levelling of G.Then, for every cell-vertex vc of G⋆ it holds that deg G⋆ (v) ≤ 3.

First we need to ensure that any three paths entering a large enough wall and passing through it can always be connected into specific branch vertices of the wall while maintaining their endpoints in the perimeter and avoiding the central part of the wall. 

Lemma 11.5. Let s ≥ 14 and t ≥ 13 be integers, ∆ be a closed disc, and W be a ∆-embedded 

s-wall such that the perimeter of W separates bd (∆) from all non-perimeter vertices of W . Let now q ∈ [3] be an integer and v ∈ V (W ) be a vertex contained in the (t − 4) -central subwall W ′

of W . If there exist internally vertex disjoint paths P1, . . . , P q in W such that  

> •

v is an endpoint of Pi for all i ∈ [q], 

> •

Pi has its other endpoint xi on the perimeter of W for all i ∈ [q], and  

> •

the Pi are indexed such that their edges incident with v appear in ∆ in counter-clockwise order. Then there exist pairwise vertex disjoint paths Q1, . . . , Q q in W such that i) there are vertices yi, i ∈ [q], on the perimeter of W ′ appearing in counter-clockwise order, each with a neighbour in the interior of the disc ∆′ bounded by the perimeter of W ′,

ii) Qi has endpoints xi and yi for each i ∈ [q], and iii) Qi is internally disjoint from ∆′ for each i ∈ [q],

Proof. This is a simple application of Proposition 3.1. Let C1, . . . , C 4 be the first four layers of W ′, let Y denote the set of all vertices of C4 which have degree 3 in W and a neighbour in the interior of the disc bounded by C4. Finally, let F be the collection of all minimal 

V (C1)-V (C4)-paths in W . Notice that |F| ≥ 4 since t − 8 ≥ 5.136 Now let G′ be the graph consisting of the union of the Pi, i ∈ [q], together with the union of the 

Ci, i ∈ [4] , and the union of all paths in F. Let Q be an X := {xi : i ∈ [q]}-Y -linkage in G′

of maximum order. We claim that |Q| = q which would imply the assertion. So suppose that 

|Q| < q . Then Proposition 3.1 gives a separator S with |S| < q ≤ 3 between X and Y in G′.However, by our choices we now know that S must miss one of the Pi, one of the cycles Cj and at least 3 of the paths from F, say F1, . . . , F 3. Since |S| ≤ 2 we know that on C4 there must be some vertex y of Y and h ∈ [3] such that there exists a subpath of C4 between the endpoint of 

Fh on C4 and y in G − S. As this is enough to certify that G′ − S still contains an X-Y -path we have reached a contradiction. Next, we wish to show that any cell deep in a suspended wall can be linked to a set of prescribed branch vertices on the perimeter of the same wall. Together with Lemma 11.5, this acts as the foundation allowing us to replace cell-vertices with actual cells when translating between tm-pairs in the levelling of some suspended wall and tm-pairs in the actual graph. Let r and s be integers with 2r ≥ s + 3 and let W be an r-wall. We say a brick B of W is 

s-central if is a brick of the s-central wall of W .

Lemma 11.6. Let r and s be integers with 2r ≥ s + 25 . Let G be a graph with an r-wall W

suspended in G as witnessed by the sphere rendition ρ.If c ∈ C(ρ) is a cell contained in the compass of an s-central brick of W together with the labelling λc of N (c) and x1, . . . , x |N (c)| are distinct vertices of degree 3 on the perimeter of W

numbered in counter-clockwise order according to their appearance on the perimeter of W, then there exist |N (c)| pairwise vertex-disjoint paths P1, . . . , P |N (c)| such that for each i ∈ [|N (c)|],Pi has endpoints xi and λc(i).

Proof. Let A ⊆ W be the annulus defined on the first 8 layers of W together with all minimal subpaths of W with one end on the perimeter of W and the other on the 8th layer of W . Let further W ′ denote the 7-central subwall of W .Then A and W ′ coincide precisely in the perimeter of W ′.

Claim 11.6.1 . There exists a linkage L1 of order |N (c)| between N (c) and the set Y of vertices on the perimeter of W ′ that have neighbours in W which do not belong to W ′. Moreover, L1 is entirely contained in the compass of the 7th layer of W .

Proof of claim. We have assumed that W is suspended in G as witnessed by ρ. Hence, there exist |N (c)| pairwise vertex disjoint paths between N (c) and the perimeter of W . It follows that there also exists a linkage P of order |N (c)| between N (c) and the perimeter of W ′ which is entirely contained in the compass of the 7th layer of W .Let C now denote the 4th layer of W ′. Then there exists a linkage Q of order 5 between Y and 

C in W ′ which is internally disjoint from the compass of C. Now a similar argument as the one used in the proof of Lemma 11.5 together with Proposition 3.1 yields the existence of the desired linkage L1. ⊣

Now consider the layers 2 to 7 of W together with all minimal subpaths of W between these two layers. This results in an annulus A′ with 6 circles and at least 6 rails. Recall that the vertices 

x1, . . . , x |N (c)| are numbered in counter-clockwise order along the perimeter of W and observe 137 that the endpoints of the paths in L1 on the perimeter of W ′ are forced to follow the labelling 

λc which is also based on the counter-clockwise orientation of ∆. Let now ⊚ be the domain of A

and ⊚′ ⊆ ⊚ be the domain of A′. We may now find a drawing of the graph G′′ that is the union of A′ ∪ ⋃ L1 together with three pairwise vertex-disjoint subpaths of W between the vertices xi

and the 2nd layer of W in ⊚ such that A′ is drawn in ⊚′, the xi lie on one boundary component of ⊚ while each path in L1 has one endpoint on the other boundary component of ⊚. It follows from our discussion above and this construction that the topological linkage with pattern

is topologically feasible in ⊚. Hence, an application of Lemma 4.13 yields that this linkage is also realisable in G′′ . Let us denote this linkage by L2. We now obtain the desired paths Pi by combining the linkages L1 and L2.We immediately obtain the following corollary.

Reconstructing (H, RH ). Our goal is to now use the homogeneity of W and the tm-pair

Once this is done, we have shown that for every member of (k, d )-folio (G, R ) there exists a rooted minor model avoiding the interior of ∆⋆g10 .6(4 d,b,k )+ h10 .6(67 ,4d,k,b ) which implies in particular that the entire compass of the r-central subwall of W is irrelevant. 11.3 Quickly finding a good flat wall 

An important assumption on our flat wall W in Theorem 11.4 is that W must be suspended in 

G − A. The other core assumption is that W is (k, d )-folio homogeneous. In order to guarantee the second condition we may call upon Theorem 11.2. Indeed, if the input wall for Theorem 11.2 141 was already suspended, then so is the (k, d )-folio homogeneous wall that comes out on the other side. However, in order to apply Theorem 11.2 we first need a suspended flat wall whose compass has bounded treewidth. Finally, there is a third assumption on W in Theorem 11.4, that is, its compass should be disjoint from R. Among all three properties, this is the easiest to guarantee as here, we may use a simple pigeonhole argument based on our bidimensionality bound. Indeed, here we are able to mimic a result from the work of Gorsky, Protopapas, and Wiederrecht [GPW26]. We slightly change their proof to obtain more clear and better dependencies. 

Lemma 11.8. For all integers r ≥ 2 and b ≥ 1 and every annotated graph (G, R ), if W is a flat 

w-wall with w ≥ (3 b + 1) r + 1 wall in G as witnessed by the sphere-rendition ρ, then one of the following holds: i) bidim (G, R ) ≥ b, or ii) there exists an r-subwall W ′ of W such that the compass of W ′ does not contain a vertex from R.Moreover, if ρ witnesses that W is suspended in G, then ρ also witnesses that W ′ is suspended in G. There also exists an algorithm that, given G, ρ, and W as input finds either a red 

(( b + 1) × (b + 1)) -red-minor in G or the wall W ′ as above in time poly (b + r)|| G|| .

Proof. We treat W as a mesh and number its vertical paths as P1, . . . , P w and its horizontal paths as Q1, . . . , Q w as in the definition of meshes. Let ˜W be the wall obtained by taking the horizontal and vertical paths Pi·(r+1)+1 and Qi·(r+1)+1 

for all i ∈ [0 , 3b + 1] . Then ˜W is a (3 b + 2) -subwall of W . Moreover, the compass of every brick 

B of ˜W contains an r-subwall WB of W which is disjoint from B itself. Without loss of generality we may assume that every vertex in a cell c in the compass of W is connected to one of the nodes of c.Now there are two cases. 

Case 1: The compass of every brick of ˜W contains a vertex of R in its interior. Then, for each of the (3 b + 1) 2 bricks B of ˜W there exists a vertex of R which can be connected to B by a path that is otherwise disjoint from ˜W . In this case it follows immediately that (G, R )

contains a red (b × b)-grid as a red-minor and thus bidim (G, R ) ≥ b.

Case 2: There exists a brick B of ˜W such that the interior of its compass does not contain a vertex from R.In this case it follows that the compass of WB does not contain a vertex from R and our proof is complete. From now on we only need to be concerned with finding a large suspended wall whose compass has small treewidth. This is because in annotated graphs of bounded bidimensionality, Lemma 11.8 can then easily guarantee the third requirement of Theorem 11.4. Before we can go there, we need to sit down and have a discussion about the history of the flat wall theorem and its application in algorithm design. In many papers that utilise the Irrelevant Vertex Technique and the Flat Wall Theorem, the flatness of a wall W under an apex set A is encoded as a so-called “flatness pair”. Below we provide a high-level discussion of flatness pairs 142 and some of their properties. Note that we do not state the definitions involved as we are not working with these definitions and only require very specific implications thereof. A flatness pair is essentially just a pair (W, R) where the object R holds the information about the graph G, 

the apex set A and the rendition ρ that witnesses the flatness of W in G − A. Sau, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [SST24], the authors investigate a set of fundamental properties of the cells inside the compass of a flat wall and on its boundary. They classify and further refine those cells and show that they are able to always guarantee that their flatness pairs have a property called “regularity”. The only core property of regular flatness pairs we are interested in is encapsulated in the following definition: 

Regular flat walls. Let r ≥ 4 be an integer. Let G be a graph and W ⊆ G be an r-wall in G.We say that W is a regular flat r-wall – or simply a regular flat wall – in G if there exists a sphere-rendition ρ of G − A such that W is flat in ρ and for every cell c ∈ C(W ) and for every non-empty set S ⊆ N (c) there exists a connected subgraph of σ(c) containing all vertices from 

S and avoiding all vertices from N (c) \ S. We say that ρ witnesses that W is a regular and flat wall of G − A.Next, we need the following corollary of Lemma 5.8 of the work of Baste, Sau, and Thilikos on the minor-deletion problem [BST23]. It follows directly from regularity and the fact that for any rendition ρ of some graph G and any non-vortex cell c ∈ C(ρ), if G contains two vertex-disjoint paths P1 and P2 such that none of the endpoints of P1 and P2 belongs to σ(c) − N (c), then at most one of the two paths can contain edges or non-boundary vertices of σ(c).

Proposition 11.9 (Baste, Sau, Thilikos [BST23]) . Let r ≥ 4 be an integer. Moreover, let G be a graph and W ⊆ G be a regular flat (r + 2) -wall in G as witnessed by the sphere-rendition ρ.Then the central r-subwall W ′ of G is suspended in G and the suspension of W in G is witnessed by ρ.In light of Proposition 11.9, in order to find a large suspended wall whose compass has bounded treewidth, all that is required is to find a slightly larger regular flat wall whose compass has bounded treewidth. This brings is to the core proposition we import from the work of Sau, Stamoulis, and Thilikos [SST24]. 

Proposition 11.10 (Sau, Stamoulis, Thilikos [SST24]) . There exists a functions 

f11 .10 , g 11 .10 : N → N such that for every integer t ≥ 1, all odd integers r ≥ 5, and every graph G one of the following holds: i) G contains a Kt minor, ii) G has a tree-decomposition of width at most at most f11 .10 (t) · r, or iii) there is a set A ⊆ V (G), where |A| ≤ g11 .10( t) together with an r-wall W that is regular flat in G − A as witnessed by the sphere-rendition ρ and the treewidth of W in G is at most f11 .10 (t) · r.Moreover, f11 .10 (t) ∈ 2O(t2 log t), g 11 .10 (t) ∈ O(t24 ), and there exists an algorithm that finds one of the three outcomes above in time 22O(t2 log t)·r log r+O(r2) · | G| + 2 2O(t2 log t)·r3 log r.Notice that the proof of Proposition 11.10 uses two theorems from the literature. The first is the flat wall theorem of Kawarabayashi, Thomas and Wollan [KTW18] which is the source of the dependency g11 .10 (t) ∈ O(t24 ). It is possible to replace the use of this theorem with the Flat 143 Wall Theorem as proven by Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht [GSW25] to obtain the bound 

g11 .10 (t) ∈ O(t3). Similarly, the proof uses a theorem by Kawarabayashi and Kobayashi [KK20] stating that every Kt-minor-free graph with treewidth in 2Ω( t2 log t) · r contains an r-wall. In light of Proposition 7.8, also this dependency can be tamed to show that f11 .10 (t) ∈ poly (t).However, these improvements do not impact the final running times and dependencies of our results which is the reason why we do not spend further efforts on implementing these stronger bounds. 

11.4 Proof of Theorem 2.5 

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem 2.5 which is the core engine of our algorithm for the 

Folio problem. This is mostly done by combining our previous findings in this section. Let us start by preparing the function f2.5.For this, we first need to fix the constant for dealing with clique-minors. 

t(b, d ) :=  

> ⌊

52

> ⌋

b2 + 3 d2 + 1 

Now we may proceed with the main dependency of Theorem 2.5. 

f2.5(k, b, d ) := f11 .10 (t(b, d )) · ((3 b + 4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) + 4 )

The claimed bounds now follow directly from the bounds in Theorem 2.5, Proposition 11.10, Theorem 11.2, and Theorem 11.4. Therefore, from here, all we have to do is explain the algorithm and prove its correctness. 

Input: An annotated graph (G, R ) with bidim (G, R ) ≤ b.

Step 1: As long as |E(G)| ∈ 2Ω( t(b,d )2 log t(b,d )) |G| run the algorithm from Proposition 8.4 to find a Kt(b,d )-minor in G. Then forward the corresponding minor model to Step 7 .

Step 2: Run the algorithm from Proposition 11.10 looking for a regular flat ((3 b +4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) + 4 )-wall W0. This can have three outcomes. 

Case 2.1: The algorithm returns a model of a Kt(b,d )-minor . Then forward the clique-minor model to Step 7 .

Case 2.2: The algorithm returns a tree-decomposition (T, β ) of width at most f2.5(k, b, d ).

Then return (T, β ) and terminate .

Case 2.3: The algorithm returns an apex set A ⊆ V (G) with |A| ≤ g11 .10 (t(b, d )) together with a regular flat ((3 b + 4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) + 4 )-wall 

W0 in G − A and a sphere-rendition ρ of G − A witnessing the regular flatness of 

W0. Moreover, the algorithm guarantees that the treewidth of W0 in G is at most 

f2.5(k, b, d ). Then forward A, W 0, and ρ to Step 3 .

Step 3: Input: A set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , together with a ((3 b +4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) + 4 )-wall W0 in G − A and a sphere-rendition 

ρ of G − A witnessing the regular flatness of W0 such that W0 has treewidth at most 

f2.5(k, b, d ) in G.144 Then let W1 be the (3 b + 4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) -central subwall of 

W0 which is now suspended in G − A as witnessed by ρ due to Proposition 11.9. Forward 

A, W 1, and ρ to Step 4 .

Step 4: Input: A set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , together with a (3 b +4)( f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) + 1) -wall W1 in G − A, and a sphere-rendition ρ

of G − A witnessing that W1 is suspended in G − A such that W1 has treewidth at most 

f2.5(k, b, d ) in G.

Then run the algorithm from Lemma 11.8. Since bidim (G, R ) ≤ b it follows that the algorithm returns a suspended f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3) -subwall W2 of W1 such that R is disjoint from the vertices in the compass of W2. Moreover, the treewidth of W2

is still at most f2.5(k, b, d ) in G and ρ witnesses that W2 is suspended in G − A. Forward 

A, ρ, and W2 to Step 5 .

Step 5: Input: A set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , together with a

f11 .2(g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , 4d, f 11 .4(b, d, k, 3)) -wall W2 in G − A, and a sphere-rendition ρ of 

G − A witnessing that W2 is suspended in G − A such that W2 has treewidth at most 

f2.5(k, b, d ) in G and its compass is disjoint from R.

Then run the algorithm from Theorem 11.2 in W2 and A to find a f11 .4(b, d, k, 3) -subwall 

W3 of W2 that is suspended in G − A, has treewidth at most f2.5(k, b, d ) in G, whose compass is disjoint from R, and that is (4 d, A )-folio homogeneous. Forward A, ρ, and W3

to Step 6 .

Step 6: Input: A set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most g11 .10 (t(b, d )) , together with an f11 .4(b, d, k, 3) -wall W3 in G − A, and a sphere-rendition ρ of G − A witnessing that W3 is suspended in 

G − A such that W3 has treewidth at most f2.5(k, b, d ) in G, its compass is disjoint from 

R, and W3 is (4 d, A )-folio homogeneous. 

Then let W4 be the 3-central subwall of W3 and let v ∈ V (W4) be any vertex of degree at least 3 in W4 not on the perimeter of W3. Return v and terminate .

Step 7: Input: A minor model of Kt(b,d ) in G.

Then run the algorithm from Lemma 8.3 to find an irrelevant vertex v ∈ V (G), return 

the irrelevant vertex v, and terminate .

Correctness of the algorithm. We begin by discussing the correctness of the algorithm above. With Step 1 we only call upon Proposition 8.4 and this correctly determines either that the number of edges in G is below the threshold or finds a Kt(b,d )-minor model in G. If the minor model is found, Lemma 8.3 correctly finds an irrelevant vertex v. Indeed, this vertex v must now be strongly irrelevant due to the way it arises from the clique-minor and thus, Step 1 is correct. Similarly, in Step 2 , Case 2.1 also refers to Step 7 and is therefore correct. Case 2.2 is the only case where the algorithm might determine that the treewidth of G is small which is one of the two valid outcomes of Theorem 11.4 and therefore safe. Finally, Case 2.3 finds the initial large wall W0 which is flat in G − A for the additional set A ⊆ V (G) of bounded size as guaranteed by Proposition 11.10. From here, the wall, its rendition, and the apex set are passed through Proposition 11.9, Lemma 11.8, and Theorem 11.2 in order. The result of the application Theorem 11.2 in Step 5 is the guarantee that all vertices in the compass of the 

3-central subwall of W3 are irrelevant. That is, if x ∈ V (G) is a vertex from the compass of W3,

then (k, d )-folio (G − x, R ) = ( k, d )-folio (G, R ). Continuing to Step 6 the algorithm now chooses 145 a vertex from W4 that has degree 3 in W4 but does not belong to the perimeter of W4. Since 

W4 is an r-wall with r ≥ 3 such a vertex always exists and by the arguments above, v is indeed irrelevant for the (k, d )-folio of (G, R ). This concludes the discussion on the correctness of the algorithm. 

Running time analysis. What is left is to discuss the running time. There are essentially only two crucial steps requiring discussion. The first is an application of Proposition 8.4 running in time 2O(t(b,d )2 log t(b,d )) |V (G)|. If this step is successful it immediately jumps to Step 7 , returning an irrelevant vertex in linear time. Otherwise, the algorithm proceeds to Step 2 . Since we did not immediately jump from Step 1 to Step 7 , from here we may assume that |E(G)| ∈ 2O(t(b,d )2 log t(b,d )) |V (G)| which allows us to replace all dependencies on the number of edges in all of the following algorithms with the number of vertices of G. This guarantees that the entire algorithm runs in time linear in |G|.Since f2.5(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k), the only other step where the running time cannot be deduced immediately from the running times of the subroutines is the combination of Step 2 

and Step 5 . The algorithm from Proposition 11.10 runs in time 

22O(t(b,d )2 log t(b,d )) ·f2.5(k,b,d ) log f2.5(k,b,d )+ O(f2.5(k,b,d )2) · | G| + 2 2O(t(b,d )2 log t(b,d )) ·f2.5(k,b,d )3 log f2.5(k,b,d ).

With the bound on f2.5(k, b, d ) above this means that this step of the algorithm runs in time 

22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|

which still fits into our predicted bounds. Notice that the treewidth of Wi in G is at bounded by f2.5(k, b, d ) for all i ∈ [4] . The running time of the algorithm from Theorem 11.2 is 

2poly (w+a+d) · poly (r) · (|G| + || G|| )

which, due to our analysis of the consequence of Step 1 , simplifies to 

2poly (w+a+d) · poly (r) · 2poly (b+d) · | G|

in our case. The value a in the expression above is the size of A which is in O(t(b, d )24 ) by Proposition 11.10. The value w on the other hand is the treewidth of W3 which satisfies 

f2.5(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k). It follows that Step 5 runs in time 

22poly (b+d)·poly (k)|G|

as required. This completes the running time analysis of our algorithm and the proof of Theorem 2.5. 

# 12 Two lower bounds for the irrelevant vertex technique 

In this section we show that the irrelevant vertex technique cannot be expected to yield substantially better bounds than those in our main results. The two lower bounds rely on the same general principle: we construct graphs of very large treewidth in which every vertex is 146 Figure 32: The graph ˆΓ5 with a vital linkage L of order 5 in it. essential for preserving a prescribed minor-folio behaviour. The first construction exploits the graph ˆΓk together with its vital linkage structure and yields an exponential lower bound in terms of bidimensionality. The second construction enriches ˆΓk with many carefully chosen non-isomorphic attachments, forcing any potential irrelevant vertex to preserve a large amount of combinatorial information, which in turn yields a lower bound in terms of the parameter d.Our starting point is the family of graphs introduced in [AKK +11, AK19], which provides canonical obstructions for irrelevant vertex arguments based on linkage uniqueness. Intuitively, the graph ˆΓk contains a highly rigid system of k terminal pairs: there is a linkage connecting these pairs, and this linkage is so constrained that deleting any vertex may destroy the possibility of realizing the same pattern. In [AKK +11, AK19] a parametric graph ˆΓk, k ∈ N≥2 was defined as follows: Let mk = 2 k − 1.We start with a (mk × mk)-grid whose “leftmost” (resp. “rightmost”) vertices are v1, . . . , v mk

(resp. u1, . . . , u mk ), ordered as they appear from above to below. We first add all edges in the set {uiumk −i+1 | i ∈ [mk−1]} and for i ∈ [2 , k ] we add the edges in 

Ei := {v2i+j v2i+1 −j | j ∈ [2 i − 1] }.

We define τk := {(si, t i) | i ∈ [k]} as the set of pairs of vertices (terminals) in ˆΓk, where 

si = v2i−1 , i ∈ [k], t i = v3·2i−1 , i ∈ [k − 1] , and tk = u2i−1 . We also denote by Tk the vertices in the pairs of τk. For a drawing of ˆΓ5 and vital linkage in it with 5 pairs of terminals, see Figure 32. 

Proposition 12.1. For every k ∈ N≥2 the graph ˆΓk contains a vital linkage L of order k whose pattern in τk.The relevance of Proposition 12.1 is that it combines two features that are central for lower bounds: on the one hand, the graph ˆΓk has exponentially large treewidth in k, and on the other 147 hand, the linkage pattern prescribed by τk is rigid. This tension is exactly what makes ˆΓk anatural witness against overly optimistic irrelevant vertex bounds. As observed in [AKK +11, AK19], ˆΓk contains as a subgraph a (m × m)-grid, therefore, tw ( ˆΓk) ≥

2k − 1. Also, it is known that every (m × m)-grid has a path decomposition {X1, . . . , X m(m−1) },

where {v1, . . . , v m} ⊆ X1 and {u1, . . . , u m} ⊆ Xm(m−1) . This readily implies that tw ( ˆΓk) = 2 k −1

(actually the same we can say for the pathwidth of ˆΓk). 

12.1 Optimality of Theorem 1.2 

We first revisit the construction ˆΓk from the perspective of the parameter bidim ( ˆΓk, T k). The point is that the lower bound from [AKK +11, AK19] is not merely an artifact of the number of terminals: it remains significant even when expressed through bidimensionality. Thus, to understand the limitations of Theorem 1.2, it is enough to estimate how the bidimensionality of 

(ˆΓk, T k) grows with k.For every k ∈ N≥2 we set bk = bidim (ˆΓk, T k).

Lemma 12.2. There is a constant c such that, for every k ∈ N≥2, 1/2

√bk ≤ k ≤ c · b2

> k

.

Proof. Notice that for every annotated graph (G, S ), |S| ≥ (bidim (G, S )) 2. This fact applied for 

(ˆΓk, T k) and given that |Tk| = 2 k, implies 1/2

√bk ≤ k.We define the graph Zs by considering the (s × (s(2 s + 1)) -grid, where we denote the top vertices ordered from the left to the right by x1, . . . , x 2s(s+1) , and where, for every i ∈ [s], we add all edges in {x(2 s+1)( i−1)+ j x(2 s+1) i−j+1 | j ∈ [s]}. We also set As = {x(2 s+1)( i−1)+ s+1 | i ∈ [s]} (see Figure 33 for a drawing of (Z5, A 5) where the added edges are depicted in red). We claim that there is a constant c′ ∈ (0 , 1) such that bidim (Zt2 , A t2 ) ≥ c′t. To see this, observe first that (Z2s, A 2s), for odd s, contains the (s × 2s(s + 1)) -grid as a minor where the s vertices in Bs := {((2 s + 1)( i − 1) + s + 1 , (s + 1) /2) | i ∈ [s]} correspond to s connected sets of (Z2s, A 2s),

each containing at least one vertex from A2s. To visualise Bs consider the (s × 2s(s + 1)) -grid drawn in Figure 33 for s = 7 by omitting the red edges; the set Bs consists of the blue vertices. Also to visualise the minor relations, each shaded square of Z2s is contracted to a single “vertical” path containing one of the vertices in Bs in its centre. Next observe that the (s × 2s(s + 1)) -grid, when s = 2 ℓ2 contains the (ℓ × ℓ)-grid as a topological minor where the branch vertices are chosen to be ℓ2 vertices from Bs. Combining these two transformations together, we have that there is some constant c′, such that bidim (Zt2 , A t2 ) ≥ c′t.By applying the claim above for ⌊√t⌋ instead of t, we have that bidim (Zt, A t) ≥ c′ · ⌊ √t⌋.Next observe that ˆΓk contains a subdivision of Z⌊k/ 2⌋, where the vertices of A⌊k/ 2⌋ correspond to the terminals t⌊k/ 2⌋, . . . , t k. bk = bidim (ˆΓk, T k) ≥ bidim (ˆΓk, {t⌊k/ 2⌋, . . . , t k}) ≥ bidim (Z⌊k/ 2⌋, A ⌊k/ 2⌋) ≥ c′ · ⌊ √k/ 2⌋.

Therefore there is a positive integer c such that k ≤ c · b2

> k

.The estimate in Lemma 12.2 shows that the bidimensionality of ( ˆΓk, T k) is polynomially related to the order of the vital linkage. In particular, replacing the number of terminals by bidimensionality 148 Figure 33: The annotated graph (Z7, A 7).does not fundamentally weaken the obstruction: the exponential behaviour in k still translates into an exponential lower bound in a polynomial function of bk.We now observe that the exponential lower bound from [AKK +11, AK19] applies not only in terms of the number of terminals, but also in terms of bidimensionality. 

Corollary 12.3. If Theorem 1.2 holds for some function β : N2 → N, then β(0 , b ) = 2 Ω( √b).

Proof. Assume that Theorem 1.3 holds for some function β : N2 → N. Then, for every 

k ≥ 2, we may apply it to the annotated graph ( ˆΓk, T k) and deduce by Proposition 12.1 that 

tw (ˆΓk) < β (k, b ). Therefore, as tw (ˆΓk) = 2 k − 1, by Lemma 12.2, β(k, b ) = 2 Ω( √b).Notice that in Corollary 12.3 we only used the “easy” upper bound to bk in Lemma 12.2. We chose to also argue on how to lower bound bk in terms of k, in order to illustrate that the gap between the size of the terminal set Tk and its bidimensionality in ˆΓk is polynomial. 

12.2 Optimality of Theorem 1.3 

We now turn to lower bounds for the folio version of the irrelevant vertex technique. In contrast with the previous subsection, where the obstruction came from the rigidity of a single linkage instance, here we build a family of graphs that encodes many pairwise non-isomorphic gadgets of small size. The role of these gadgets is to make every vertex indispensable: deleting a vertex necessarily destroys the ability to realise a certain prescribed minor. 

Asymptotic enumeration of 5-regular graphs. The first ingredient is a supply of many small pairwise non-isomorphic connected graphs of bounded degree. We obtain these from asymptotic enumeration of regular graphs as follows. 

Lemma 12.4. For all sufficiently large even integers n, there exist at least nn pairwise non-isomorphic connected 5-regular graphs on n vertices.

# 13 Conclusion 

This paper provides strong evidence that the algorithmic side of the Graph Minors Series is much closer to the “solar system” of mainstream parameterized algorithms than its longstanding “galactic” reputation may suggest. 

13.1 Résumé 

Our main contribution is a quantitatively controlled version of the irrelevant-vertex machinery. More precisely, we prove that the vital-linkage threshold and, more generally, the irrelevant-vertex threshold for folios admit bounds of the form β(k, b, d ) ∈ 2poly (b+d) · poly (k), where the exponential dependence is governed by the bidimensionality parameter and the detail of the folio, rather than the number of terminals. Our lower bounds further show that the combinatorial source of this exponential behaviour can be identified precisely, isolated, and bounded in an essentially optimal way. 152 Beyond the technical core of the proof, we see our results as part of a broader program whose goal is to make the structural and algorithmic content of the graph minors framework quantitatively explicit. Our arguments build on a sequence of recent works [Chu15a, GSW25, GSTW25, GSW26, GPW26] that replaced enormous or implicit dependencies in several key ingredients of the series by polynomial ones (see also [KTW21, SST21, TW23, PPTW25, PTW25, TW26] for precursors). In this sense, the present paper may be viewed as the culmination of this line of research: once these ingredients are brought under polynomial control, the algorithmic heart of the graph minors framework – namely, the irrelevant-vertex technique for routing and rooted pattern detection – can finally be restated with bounds that are explicit, structurally meaningful, and essentially optimal. 

13.2 Bidimensionality, annotation irrelevancy, and more 

In the introduction we mentioned, as a direct consequence of Theorem 1.4, the following. 

Lemma 13.1. There exists an algorithm that, given an annotated graph (G, R ) with 

bidim (G, R ) ≤ b and |R| ≤ k, computes (d, k )-folio (G, R ) in 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G|2 time. Since bidim (G, R ) ≤ √|R|, this yields the following corollary. 

Corollary 13.2. There exists an algorithm that, given an annotated graph (G, R ) with |R| ≤ k, 

computes (d, k )-folio (G, R ) in 22poly (k+d)

· | G|2 time. The algorithm in Lemma 13.1 is based on removing a set of vertices that produces an equivalent instance (G′, R ), where G′ is a subgraph of G, t := tw (G′) ∈ 2poly (b+d)poly (k), and 

(k, d )-folio (G′, R ) = (k, d )-folio (G, R ). (2) Since each removed vertex is strongly irrelevant , as certified by Theorem 1.3, the equality in (2) does not require any bound on the size of R.By standard dynamic programming, computing (k, d )-folio (G′, R ) can be done in 2poly (t,k,d ) · | G′|,

as the tables of the dynamic program encode (t + k + d)-rooted graphs of detail d, of which there are 2poly (t,k,d ). This implies the following. 

Theorem 13.3. There exists an algorithm that, given an annotated graph (G, R ) with 

bidim (G, R ) ≤ b, computes (d, k )-folio (G, R ) in 2poly (k)·2poly (b+d)

· | G|2 time. A natural challenge beyond Theorem 13.3 would be to eliminate the dependence on b from the complexity of the algorithm, again without assuming any bound on R. We believe that there exists an algorithm computing the (k, d )-folio of an annotated graph (G, R ) in 

222poly (k+d)

· | G|2 (3) time. In other words, we conjecture that Corollary 13.2 holds without any restriction on R. An 

f (k, d ) · | G|2-time algorithm – albeit with a huge and unspecified f – already follows from the meta-algorithmic results of [GST23c, SST25]. We believe that such an algorithm can be achieved by employing the “annotation irrelevant” argument that is implicit in [SST25] (see also [GST23c, GST23b]), where bidimensionality 153 plays a pivotal role. (For the combinatorial and algorithmic properties of bidimensionality, see [PPTW24a, PPTW25, PTW25].) Roughly speaking, the approach is as follows: if R has sufficiently large bidimensionality, then it is possible to find a vertex in R that is annotation irrelevant ; removing it from R – while keeping it in the graph – yields an equivalent instance. By iteratively removing such vertices from R, we can eventually bound the bidimensionality of the annotated set R, at which point Theorem 13.3 becomes applicable. Working out the details of this procedure is technically involved, as one must carefully control dependencies to remain within polynomial bounds. Addressing these issues is beyond the scope of this paper. Nevertheless, we emphasise that the idea of removing “annotation irrelevant” vertices from the annotation set is not specific to folio computation, but rather indicative of a broader principle. It underlies the meta-algorithmic framework of [SST25], which is driven by low bidimensionality and whose parameter dependencies are strongly influenced by the linkage function, which we now optimise. We expect that many of the algorithmic consequences of [SST25] can now be translated into more streamlined algorithms using our optimal bounds on the linkage function. More generally, for annotated graphs (or even colourful graphs [PTW25]), bidimensionality plays a role analogous to that of treewidth in many graph problems: it provides a threshold parameter above which annotation-irrelevant vertices can be found and below which irrelevant vertices can be identified, until eventually a bounded-treewidth equivalent instance is obtained. Based on this general algorithmic scheme, we expect that our bounds and algorithms will have sufficient gravitational pull to liberate more algorithms from their “galactic” reputation. This, however, should be the subject of a broader future project. 

13.3 Other research directions 

Reducing degrees of polynomials. A natural question is how far the quantitative bounds obtained here can be sharpened further. The near-optimality of our results leaves limited room for improving the general asymptotic form of the Linkage Function from Theorem 1.2. Still, even within a bound of the form β(k, b ) ∈ 2poly (b) · poly (k), it would be very interesting to reduce the degrees of the polynomials appearing both in the exponent and in the multiplicative factor. Our current proof still produces rather large constants in these degrees, and reducing them would require a careful quantitative refinement of several parts of the argument and the results which feed into our proof – particularly Proposition 3.8. Such improvements would be valuable not only from a technical point of view, but also for applications in more specialised settings. In particular, when one studies the Linkage Function under the exclusion of specific parametric graph classes, additional structure may become available and could lead to substantially better estimates. 

Other linkage-like constellations. Moving beyond graph minors, it would be very interesting to establish analogous linkage theorems for other rooted containment relations, such as labelled minors, immersions, or containment relations in directed graphs. Each of these settings appears to require genuinely new ideas, but the present work suggests that one should again search for the structural parameter that governs the exponential part of the bound. It is also natural to ask whether our proof techniques can be used to improve the parameter dependencies in several recent results in structural algorithmic graph theory, such as the edge-disjoint linkage theorem 154 for the strong immersion relation in Eulerian digraphs from [CKK25], and the group-labelled linkages studied in [LY25]. 

Meta-folios. We define the (k, d )-meta-folio of an annotated graph (G, R ) as follows 

(k, d )-meta-folio (G, R ) := {(k, d )-folio (G, R ′) | R′ ⊆ R}.

Notice that the meta-folio of an annotated graph can be seen as a “2nd order object”, as it contains a collection of collections of k-rooted graphs. By the strong irrelevance property in Theorem 1.3, the repeated application of the irrelevant vertex argument produces, in 

22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|2

time, an annotated graph (G′, R ) such that G′ is an induced subgraph of G, tw (G′) ∈

2poly (b+d)poly (k), and, moreover, the (k, d )-meta-folio of (G, R ) coincides with that of (G′, R ).Since standard dynamic programming can compute the (k, d )-meta-folio of (G′, R ) in 

22poly (tw (G′)) 

· | G′|

time, this yields an algorithm for computing the (k, d )-meta-folio of (G, R ) in 

22poly (b+d)·poly (k) · | G|2 + 2 22poly (b+d)·poly (k)

· | G| (4) time. This establishes an interesting trade-off between the parametric dependence and the polynomial dependence for the computation of the meta-folio, seen as a 2nd order object. Can the dependencies in (4) be improved? More broadly, do such “higher-order” extensions of folios inherently involve higher towers of exponentiations and under what trade-offs? 

Reducing levels of exponentiality. On the algorithmic side, the most immediate challenge is to improve the running time of the folio-computation algorithm in Theorem 1.4 to 

2poly (d,k ) · | G|O(1) ,

or to obtain algorithms for k-Disjoint Paths with running time 

2poly (k) · | G|O(1) .

For k-Disjoint Paths , some progress has already been made for planar graphs in [LMP +20] and [COO23], where algorithms with running times 2O(k2)|G|6 and 2O(k2)|G|, respectively, were obtained. We believe that ideas from the proof of this paper, combined with those of [COO23], may help extend these results to more general minor-closed graph classes or even in general and for folios as well. In fact we are curious whether the (heavy) dynamic programming part in (4) or the running time in (3) for computing (k, d )-folio (G, R ) can be reduced by one level of exponentiality. 155 Reducing polynomial dependence. As a final challenge, we would like to highlight the recent result of Korhonen, Mi. Pilipczuk, and Stamoulis [KPS24b]. They gave an algorithm that, given an annotated graph (G, R ) with |R| ≤ k, computes (k, d )-folio (G, R ) in time 

f (k, d ) · m1+ o(1) ,

where m is the total number of vertices and edges of G. Moreover, it follows that this running time can be improved to O(f (k, d )·|| G||· log |G|), for some (galactic) function f : N2 → N [KPS26]. For surface-embeddable graphs, the dependence on the input size can even be made linear, as proved by Golovach, Kolliopoulos, Stamoulis, and Thilikos in [GKST25]. The parameter dependence hidden in the function f, however, remains galactic. This naturally raises the following question: is it possible to obtain a true “best of both worlds” theorem, combining the near-linear dependence on the input size achieved in [KPS24b] (or [GKST25]) with the controlled parameter dependence of Theorem 1.4? 156 References
